Science Fiction The Court of Borrowed Voices PDF

The Court of Borrowed Voices

A witness can spend his last life proving he was killed.

T. K. Arven

Prologue — The Last Route Out

Iven kept one hand on choir bell C-9 while the floor shed heat beneath his boots.

The bell occupied half the route room, its smoked auric glass webbed with pale stress lines. Each time the thermal lattice failed over another district, those lines brightened and the recording field lost a fraction of depth. Iven watched the phase margin contract on the service strip. Thirty-one usable layers had become six. The room air had cooled enough to make his breath show.

“North loop is gone,” Lio said through his collar speaker.

Her voice carried pump noise and the clipped delay of an overloaded civic channel. She had stayed at the grid exchange four levels below the inhabited caverns. Iven pictured the route to her without effort: two freight shafts, a pressure stair, sixty meters along the red coolant trunk. He could not picture the expression she wore.

“How long?” he asked.

“For the exchange, perhaps forty minutes. Dayward Three may hold longer.”

“That was not my question.”

A relay snapped somewhere behind the wall. C-9 drew reserve power, and a warning square changed from amber to red.

Lio let two breaths pass. “I am not climbing four levels while twelve thousand people still need the valves sequenced.”

“You cannot sequence them after the trunk freezes.”

“Then I have until the trunk freezes.”

Iven pressed his thumb to the deposition plate. The bell took his pulse pattern, mapped his breath, and opened another clean layer. Route marks appeared under the glass like hairline scratches catching a lamp. He had already recorded the collector failures, convoy bearings, population shelters, and every Crown instruction that reached Eiren Reach after the shear began. A deposition needed sequence more than eloquence. Sequence could survive damaged language.

On the wall display, three rescue bells held their triangular approach. Their combined mass could detune toward Eiren and carry emergency heat exchangers through the disturbed corridor. Crown control had transmitted the requirement without dispute: three bells, nineteen hours outside the Mouth schedule. The route existed. The convoy had fuel. Eiren still had receiving locks.

“Crown has not acknowledged the final vector,” Iven said.

“They acknowledged it six minutes ago.”

“Not the vector I sent.”

Lio understood before he explained. “Show me.”

He pushed the route table into their private channel. The convoy line approached the shear boundary, waited at the decision mark, then bent away from Eiren. Its three bells moved toward the Meridian Mouth’s failing alignment instead. A priority code rode beneath the turn, followed by an instruction to preserve corridor stability.

For several seconds, pump noise filled the connection.

“They chose the Mouth,” Lio said.

“They chose its schedule.”

“No. Give them the whole action.”

Aster Crown’s rescue lights remained visible through the route-room slit as three hard points. Iven had watched them brighten for four hours. Now they crossed the reference marks one by one, turning away toward infrastructure that served worlds beyond this dying moon.

He added the commanded vector to C-9. The bell accepted it with a pressure pulse against his palm.

Another message arrived from convoy control. It did not address the colony. It ordered all emergency telemetry compressed into standard loss fields and warned that uncoordinated detuning might worsen the shear. A second line advised Eiren route staff to preserve navigational data for later inquiry.

“Later,” Iven said.

“What?”

“They want a record for later.”

Lio made a sound that might have been a laugh if there had been air to spare. “Then make it expensive to misunderstand.”

The civic board lost two shelter indicators. Iven opened their status packets before the board could discard them. Four hundred people in West Reservoir had moved into the machine galleries for residual heat. The old transit hostel held three hundred twelve temporary workers absent from the resident count. Their supervisor had filed the correction nine times. The registry still displayed 18,204.

“I have the transients,” he told Lio.

“Keep them with the routes.”

“Your exchange log too.”

“Take the non-conscious record. Do not spend a deposition layer carrying my voice.”

Her instruction hurt because it made technical sense. Neural telemetry consumed depth. Instrument logs compressed cleanly. Iven transferred the grid sequence without her speech and placed her duty code beside the last manual valve changes.

C-9’s margin fell to four layers.

A knock sounded at the sealed route-room door. Through its small glass panel, civic runner Tal Vey held up a slate showing evacuation assignments. Iven released the lock. Cold air entered with him.

“Lower caverns need the final route,” Tal said. “They still think the convoy can turn back.”

Iven looked toward the wall display. The three lights had crossed the recoverable bearing. Nineteen hours would have opened a path toward Eiren. Crown had spent that interval before it began.

“Give them subsurface transfers,” Lio said over the collar channel. “Dayward Three may accept another two thousand.”

Tal heard her. “The connector is losing pressure.”

“Then send small groups. Shut each section behind them.”

Iven built the route while Tal waited. He marked doors that needed manual closure and removed a lift already disabled by ice. No path reached safety. Some paths reached another hour of heat. Route work did not permit him to confuse those outcomes.

Tal took the slate. “Are you coming?”

“When the bell seals.”

“Your room has no independent loop.”

“I know where my room goes.”

The runner left at a measured pace until the door shut, then sprinted down the corridor.

A third shelter indicator vanished. C-9 showed three usable layers.

Iven could spend one on an appeal. The bell still held a narrow emergency carrier aimed at Crown control. He could give population counts, projected deaths, or Lio’s estimate of time. The facts had already reached the convoy. Repeating them would consume the cleanest remaining state while pretending ignorance had caused the turn.

He armed the deposition field instead.

“Iven,” Lio said.

“I am here.”

“Route the living first.”

“I sent Tal with Dayward Three.”

“That is not what I meant.”

He found her duty code on the grid display. Its location marker remained fixed at the exchange. “Your route closes in eleven minutes.”

“Yes.”

“I can reach the pressure stair in seven.”

“You can reach a locked exchange in seven. I sealed the lower level.”

His free hand closed around the edge of the plate. “Open it.”

“No.” Her next words arrived under a burst of static. “Name the choice accurately. Then leave if a route remains.”

C-9 registered the rise in his pulse and asked whether to include autonomic data. Iven denied it. Grief could authenticate distress, but Crown could also use it to classify the record as panic.

He spoke the convoy identifiers, the command time, and the turn geometry. He stated that the rescue remained technically viable when Crown diverted the three bells. He stated the projected risk to the Meridian Mouth: closure uncertain, recovery measured in years. He did not conceal that cost. A choice became visible only when the record preserved both losses available at the moment of decision.

The bell’s second layer filled.

Grid noise swallowed half of Lio’s next sentence. He caught only his name and the word enough.

“Say again.”

No reply came. Her duty marker persisted for four seconds, then changed to an instrument-only trace. Pressure at the exchange fell below survivable range.

Iven placed his forehead against the cold housing. The glass did not answer. Bells recorded energy and route state; they offered no comfort and made no promises about who would later listen.

One clean layer remained.

He opened the emergency carrier. Crown control appeared as an address without a person attached. Iven could still plead. He could ask the convoy to reverse an impossible turn or ask strangers to remember him kindly. Neither request would add evidence.

Instead, he named Lio Senn at the thermal grid. He named the temporary workers omitted from the registry and appended their supervisor’s correction. He named the inhabited caverns losing heat in sequence. Last, he described the rescue lights crossing away from Eiren while every required bell remained under command.

The carrier dropped before confirmation. C-9 held the deposition locally.

A sealing prompt appeared beneath his hand. Once accepted, the bell would fuse its outer phase skin around the record. Opening it would require a destructive read.

Iven checked the route board one final time. Dayward Three still carried heat. Tal’s small groups moved through the connector as intermittent pressure signatures. No path led from the route room to them before the next doors closed.

He pressed seal.

Auric glass tightened around the deposition in a slow amber band. The service strip went dark from right to left. Beyond the slit, Crown’s three rescue lights reached the Mouth alignment and steadied there.

The last heat left the floor. Iven kept his palm against choir bell C-9 until its sealing pulse ended.

Chapter 1 — The Signal Under the Signal

Nia had both hands inside the choir bell when its damaged layer answered her.

The response reached her first as pressure in the probe handle, a precise double tap against the pads of her fingers. She stopped turning the ceramic collar. Around her, Callis rolled through the dark on its three slow rings, and the archive gantry translated that rotation into a faint pull toward the bell’s black housing. Osei Marr floated at her left shoulder with the repair cloth looped through his belt. He had been reading fastener temperatures from a slate. Now he watched her hands.

“Did you send a confirmation?” she asked.

“Nothing since the seam test.”

Nia kept the probe still. Choir bell C-9 filled the cradle in front of them, three meters of tuned mass and smoked shielding whose usefulness depended on tolerances smaller than a blood cell. A stripe of corrosion ran beneath its service lip. Callis had acquired the bell seven years ago with a lot of decommissioned Crown equipment, and the archive had used it only as ballast. Even quiet mass needed inspection. Neglect could turn a stable schedule into a drifting one.

The handle tapped again.

This time Osei felt it through the shared brace. His boots flexed under their toe loops.

“That came from inside,” he said.

“Or from the ring.”

“The ring doesn’t know our test interval.”

She withdrew the probe by four centimeters, slow enough to keep the brittle service collar from scraping. The exposed seam showed the repair problem they had expected: a line of pale oxidation along the bell’s outer auric glass, where a bad seal had admitted traces of coolant vapor. The response had come from deeper than the corrosion. It had crossed the service layer and traveled up a tool designed not to conduct phase motion at all.

Osei lifted his slate. “I can isolate the cradle.”

“Not yet.”

“If that is active telemetry, we should get it out of the ring circuit.”

“If you isolate before we establish cause, the isolation becomes an intervention.” Nia locked the probe against the collar and clipped its lead to a passive meter. “Read me the last five pulses.”

He did. Seam test at fourteen millivolts. Reflection check at six. Thermal baseline. Two probe-identification ticks. No confirmation request and no diagnostic call that could have produced the taps. His voice grew flatter as the list shortened. Osei had worked under Nia for eighteen months and had learned that surprise did not excuse imprecision.

A fan changed pitch behind the cradle. The vibration crossed the gantry in a low, continuous thread. Nothing inside C-9 moved.

Nia closed her eyes for one breath and reconstructed her hands’ sequence. She had seated the probe, tested the service seam, rotated the collar clockwise, then paused when resistance climbed above limit. The first answer had come three seconds later. A passive phase layer could return energy, but not after a delay shaped around a technician’s movement. Some maintenance systems answered pressure. C-9 had no live maintenance system.

“Bring the custody lamp,” she said.

Osei unclipped a narrow amber tube from the emergency board. When he pressed his thumb to it, the lamp wrote time, place, and both their identities into an optical thread that could not be erased without snapping. Nia waited until its green point steadied.

“Callis Archive, mass ring two, cradle seventeen,” she said. “Unscheduled response during exterior corrosion repair. Bell remains connected. No active query sent. Witnesses Nia Daro and Osei Marr.”

“Recorded,” Osei said.

Only then did she release the probe and seal it in a clear sleeve. Osei’s fingers moved quickly, but he did not rush. They capped the exposed seam with a field collar and fastened three passive pickups around it. Each pickup measured one thing: heat, mechanical vibration, or phase displacement. A machine that measured more than one thing at once made a poor witness when those measurements disagreed.

Before activating the pickups, Nia made Osei photograph every tool shadow across the housing. The archive had learned that discipline after a disputed freight recorder arrived with a technician’s wrench mark mistaken for impact damage. He rotated the custody lamp through four angles while she held a scale strip beside the corroded seam. Tiny flakes had collected in the collar, gray at their edges and amber where they had pulled glass with them. Nia folded each flake into a numbered well. If the buried response later proved important, someone would ask whether opening the service lip had created it. She intended to have an answer that did not depend on anyone trusting her memory.

Osei passed her the seventh sample card. “You think this reaches a court?”

“I think every record deserves work that survives an unfriendly question.”

“That is a court answer.”

“It is an archive answer. Courts borrowed it.”

He sealed the sample well and pressed its corner against the custody lamp. The optical thread flashed once. Nia entered the bell’s local gravity, gantry temperature, ring-load schedule, and the exact torque she had applied before the first tap. None of those details felt connected to a delayed response. That made them easier to omit and therefore necessary to keep. A useful chain of custody preserved what the present could not yet recognize as relevant.

Only after the sample cards sat in a tethered row did Nia authorize the pickups. The extra minutes gave C-9 every chance to return to ordinary silence. It did not.

The heat line remained level. Mechanical vibration matched the gantry fan. Phase displacement rose by a fraction when Nia’s gloved thumb crossed the old Crown serial plate.

Osei leaned close enough that his breath fogged the inner edge of his face shield. “Again.”

She moved her thumb back.

The phase line fell. She crossed the plate once more, careful not to press.

A narrow peak appeared.

“Capacitance,” Osei said, but his tone rejected his own answer.

“Through an insulated glove?”

“Static from the housing.”

“Measure it.”

He fixed a grounding whisker to the plate. The next pass produced the same peak. Nia changed hands, then asked Osei to make the motion. His crossing left the line flat.

Neither of them spoke for several seconds.

The old serial plate carried a Crown inventory string beneath years of scuffing: C9-ER-204 / TRANSIT LOSS / INERT. ER could have meant a depot, a manufacturer, or a route class. The word loss belonged to an accounting system rather than a maintenance history. Someone had declared the bell inert. Someone else had sent it to Callis with a corrosion-prone seal and no interior map.

Nia pulled up the acquisition record on Osei’s slate. The archive summary offered eleven lines. Bell received in mixed mass transfer. No recoverable emergency telemetry. Outer phase glass cleared under salvage protocol. Interior access prohibited because destructive examination could alter tuned density. Origin field blank.

“Show the signed record,” she said.

“That is the signed record.”

“Show the source attachment.”

Osei opened the attachment index. Three entries appeared, all checksum stubs without files. One identified a Crown disposal office that no longer existed. Another named a Callis intake clerk who had died four years ago. The third field contained only a retention code: ER-47.

A cold feeling moved through Nia’s forearms, not fear yet, but the body’s recognition of a pattern before the mind admitted one. She knew that retention code. Everyone in resonance work did.

“Eiren Reach,” Osei said softly.

“Do not resolve it aloud until we verify the registry.”

“You just did.”

“I recognized a code. That is not a provenance finding.”

His mouth tightened. He turned to the archive terminal fixed below the cradle and entered the public disaster index. The screen returned thirty-seven references: thermal lattice collapse, convoy navigation loss, population unrecovered, choir assets presumed destroyed. Forty-seven years had sanded the event into neutral categories. Children learned the name from transit-safety lessons. Engineers cited Eiren when arguing for redundant heat loops. No intact bell had appeared in the public inventory.

Osei selected asset cross-reference. C-9 did not exist.

The field collar around the service seam gave a faint click.

Both of them looked at it. Phase displacement climbed, held for two seconds, and dropped.

“That followed the search,” Osei said.

“It followed a terminal load.”

“Which is connected to a different bus.”

“All buses touch the ring ground somewhere.”

He looked at her instead of arguing. Nia could hear how much effort she was spending on ordinary explanations. Ordinary explanations deserved effort; most anomalies died under it. The dangerous technician wanted wonder. The useful one wanted the mistake.

She searched for the mistake.

They powered down the terminal. The next registry query ran from Osei’s isolated slate. C-9 answered with a displacement peak before the result appeared. They moved the slate three meters away, shielded it, and repeated the query with the same timing. Nia asked for an unrelated asset. Nothing happened. She asked for Eiren’s civic register. The buried line climbed.

Osei’s voice dropped. “It knows the query.”

“No. It responds to some condition correlated with the query.”

“From behind two passive layers.”

“That remains to be established.”

“What would establish it for you?”

Nia looked at the bell’s serial plate. The question carried no challenge. Osei needed to know the threshold before their fear began moving it.

“A response containing information the query did not supply,” she said. “Until then, we have an unexplained coupling.”

The archive’s emergency rules offered three paths. A dangerous active system could be isolated immediately. A suspected human record required a custodian before examination. Unknown salvage belonged under administrative seal until ownership could be found. C-9 might qualify for all three, and each path forbade a step another path required.

Nia called the duty custodian.

Cal Orsen’s office answered with a junior clerk who listened without interruption, then asked whether the bell contained a conscious process. Nia said she had no evidence of consciousness. The clerk asked whether it contained human neural telemetry. Nia said the source remained unknown. The clerk asked whether Crown held current title. Nia read the acquisition line showing title transferred to Callis.

“Then maintain present conditions,” the clerk said. “Send a passive record. Do not query.”

“Present conditions include a suspected active response on a bell connected to the mass ring.”

“Can you disconnect it without changing the record?”

“No.”

“Then do not disconnect it.”

The clerk’s caution made legal sense and technical nonsense. Nia requested Cal directly. She was told he was in a ration appeal and could not leave for thirty minutes.

Osei had opened a tools cabinet. “The bell may not give us thirty.”

“You are assuming intention again.”

“I am assuming change.” He pointed to the passive phase line.

The baseline had begun to pulse. Not the double tap from before. Six low rises, evenly spaced, followed by a pause. Then five. Then six again.

Nia watched three cycles. The sequence did not match any bell diagnostic she knew. It could be damage cascading through lamination boundaries. It could be a recording responding blindly to vibration. It could be an old emergency code. Every possibility carried a different obligation, and delay already counted as a choice.

She opened the bell’s maintenance ontology, a list of the tiny standardized pulses a technician could send without triggering a destructive read. A calibration hello used two rises, one long and one short. It asked no question beyond can this layer reflect? An inert layer returned an immediate mirror. A damaged layer returned noise. An active controller replied with its manufacturer class.

Osei read the entry over her shoulder. “The custodian said no query.”

“A calibration hello is maintenance, not semantic access.”

“You told him any intervention could alter the record.”

“I told his clerk.”

“That distinction will comfort the review board.”

Despite herself, she almost smiled. “You may step off the gantry and record an objection.”

“I object from here.” He activated the custody lamp again. “Osei Marr objects to an active pulse before judicial authorization. Osei Marr also believes delay presents an unmeasured risk to a possibly active record.”

“That is not an objection.”

“It contains one.”

Nia stated her decision for the lamp. A single low-energy calibration pulse, below the outer layer’s reflection threshold, chosen because ongoing phase activity created an immediate preservation risk. No broad query. No destructive read. The bell would remain connected. If the response exceeded a passive mirror, they would stop.

She built the pulse on an isolated emitter rather than the probe that had received the taps. Two rises appeared on the preview, one long and one short. Osei checked amplitude and timing. Nia checked his check. They fastened the emitter to the outer service plate without opening the seam.

“Custody lamp continuous,” Osei said.

“Mechanical pickup?”

“Level.”

“Heat?”

“Level.”

“Phase baseline?”

“Pulsing six-five-six.”

Nia rested one finger above the send key. Her glove did not touch it.

The old disaster index remained open on the slate, Eiren reduced to casualty fields and engineering reforms. She thought of the unknown intake clerk accepting a bell the public record declared destroyed. Perhaps the clerk had known what arrived. Perhaps the blank origin field reflected haste, corruption, or mercy. Provenance work punished stories assembled before evidence, especially compassionate stories. Sympathy could contaminate a chain as thoroughly as bribery.

She pressed send.

The emitter delivered its long rise and short rise. C-9’s outer layer returned the expected mirror so quickly it seemed simultaneous.

A second response followed from deeper in the bell.

The passive meter overflowed its small display and threw the data onto Osei’s slate. Lines spread across the screen, not voltage curves but positional relations: mass bearings, transit gradients, relative windows. Nia knew enough route work to see structure without reading destination. Three bells sat in a triangular schedule. A convoy line approached from spinward. One vector bent away from a fourth marker that carried no label.

The map redrew itself.

This time the convoy line continued toward the unlabeled marker. The three bells dimmed. A pressure fault opened across the Meridian Mouth. Numbers populated the lower margin in an old Crown notation: nineteen hours of detuning, projected closure uncertain, rescue window viable.

Osei made a sound through his teeth. “That did not come from our pulse.”

“No.”

“You set the threshold.”

“I remember.”

The display changed before she could reach for capture controls. The viable rescue line vanished. In its place, the convoy bent toward the three-bell formation. The Mouth stabilized. The fourth marker lost heat in successive bands.

Nia understood the marker then. Not because a label appeared, but because its thermal geometry matched the public diagrams every resonance apprentice studied.

Eiren Reach.

A final line wrote itself beneath the map in compressed route notation. Osei expanded it through the public key. The result contained no words, only a checksum and an instruction to seek a deeper layer.

“Stop the emitter,” Nia said.

“It already stopped.”

“Break its power anyway.”

He pulled the physical link. The route map remained on the slate. Within C-9, the buried phase signal settled from six-five-six into the same double tap Nia had felt through the probe.

Callis did not manufacture weather, yet a tremor like distant thunder passed through the mass ring as the station trimmed its balance. Nia secured the slate to the gantry. Her hands had begun to shake. She curled them around the rail until the movement stopped.

Osei stared at the map. “That showed two outcomes.”

“It showed two modeled routes.”

“One rescues Eiren. One holds the Mouth.”

“We do not know whether either route occurred.”

“The public record says the convoy was lost.”

“The public record may be incomplete without being false.”

He turned toward her. “How incomplete?”

She had no answer that belonged in a custody record.

The duty line chimed before she could call it. Not Cal’s office. The connection identified Aster Crown Transit, legal operations, priority infrastructure channel. Crown had detected the pulse or the phase output through a system supposedly transferred away from them seven years earlier.

Nia accepted audio only.

A woman’s voice gave her name as Ada Neme, operations director and counsel. She did not ask what Nia had found.

“Bell C-9 has produced an unauthorized phase excursion,” Ada said. “Do not send another pulse. Do not disconnect the cradle. Crown quarantine personnel are en route under corridor-safety authority.”

“Callis owns the bell.”

“Crown retains emergency authority over any mass capable of destabilizing the Meridian Mouth.”

“Your public transfer record identifies C-9 as inert.”

A pause followed, brief enough to preserve the appearance of certainty.

“That classification is now disputed,” Ada said.

“On what evidence?”

“The excursion you just created.”

Nia looked at Osei’s custody lamp, its green point holding every second in sequence. “We recorded an internal response before the calibration pulse.”

Another pause. Longer.

“Preserve that record,” Ada said. “You will provide it to the quarantine lead.”

“The record belongs to the Callis Court until custody is decided.”

“Nia Daro, if C-9 contains what Crown has reason to believe it contains, ordinary archive custody cannot protect your station.”

The phrasing mattered more than the threat. Not if it contains a dangerous fault. Not if it contains old telemetry. Crown had reason to believe the hidden layer held something specific, and Crown knew enough to fear its exposure.

Ada ended the channel.

Across the ring, pressure doors began closing in sequence. White Crown authority lights appeared on the service corridor beyond the gantry, reflected in the bell’s smoked housing as three approaching arcs.

Osei dimmed the slate, but the route lines remained visible beneath his palm. “Do we surrender the capture?”

Nia unclipped the custody lamp from the brace. Its optical thread ran through the tool like a filament of trapped amber.

“We preserve it,” she said.

“That was not my question.”

“No.”

She sealed the lamp against her chest, keyed the gantry into judicial hold, and watched the first quarantine officer reach the closed pressure door. Deep inside C-9, something struck the passive meter twice, waited, and struck twice again.

Chapter 2 — A Body Made for Testimony

Nia held the respirator seal while the vocal body took its first breath.

Warm saline ran from the body’s mouth into a catch tube below the cradle. Its ribs lifted once, stopped, then lifted again under the ventilator’s measured pressure. A clear membrane covered both eyes. The tissue had finished growing twenty-three minutes earlier, but growth did not mean readiness. Nerves needed a coherent pattern. Breath needed a reason to continue after the machine released it.

“Phase transfer at twelve percent,” Osei said from behind the isolation glass.

Nia watched the monitor rather than him. Twelve percent marked the authorized limit for this first contact, enough to establish orientation and immediate preference without opening broad recall. Cal Orsen’s preservation order had not yet been argued. Crown had agreed to nothing. The Callis duty court had authorized one minimal respeaking because a possible person faced active decay inside C-9.

A Crown quarantine officer stood outside the room with one hand resting near the physical cutoff. Nia had refused to let Crown touch it. The cutoff belonged to the body team and to Cal’s clerk, who recorded from an axial chamber two decks away.

The phase line climbed through the body’s neck lattice. Fine contractions moved under pale skin where the temporary nerves learned their routes. Neutral vocal bodies carried no face from the source pattern. This one had a broad unfinished brow, close-cropped growth hair, and features selected to avoid resemblance to any stored civic identity. The resemblance that mattered would come through movement.

“Transfer holding,” Osei said. “Respiratory center receiving.”

Nia loosened the respirator seal by one degree.

The next breath arrived without mechanical pressure. It scraped through a throat grown for speech and little else. The body’s fingers closed around the linen safety strap, then released it. Nia noted the grip sequence. Thumb last. Not seizure activity.

She removed the membrane from the left eye.

The lid opened beneath her hand.

Its pupil contracted against the room lamp. The eye moved past Nia, found the ceiling’s acoustic baffles, then tracked the saline line to its hanging bag. No panic yet. No recognition either.

“Can you hear me?” Nia asked.

The mouth shaped air without sound.

“Do not force speech. Blink once if you can hear.”

One blink.

“Blink twice if breathing hurts.”

The eye closed twice, with a longer pause between the motions than she had requested.

“Pain at four,” Nia told the record. “Likely throat and chest. Reduce support pressure by two points, increase local warmth.”

A heating mesh brightened under the cradle sheet. Osei adjusted the glucose line from his station. The room carried the medicinal smell of fresh tissue and the dry mineral scent of auric-glass couplings. Nia had worked in vocal-body rooms before, always with remnants whose legal status had already been settled. None had awakened under armed quarantine.

She peeled the second eye membrane free.

Both eyes turned toward her face. Their focus moved in short corrections, measuring distance. The body swallowed against the tube, winced, and found a sound.

“Where?”

“Callis Archive,” Nia said. “You occupy a temporary vocal body. Your source remains in a choir bell under preservation.”

The eyes closed. His breath count rose from nine to fourteen.

“Do you understand those words?”

“Callis.” His voice carried no regional inflection yet. Tissue tone came first; learned cadence required more of the pattern. “Court station.”

“Yes.”

“Route.”

Nia looked through the glass. Osei had frozen over his slate.

“What route?” she asked.

His right hand pulled against the linen restraint. “Three masses. Spinward approach. Shear boundary.”

“Do you see a route now?”

“No.”

“Are you remembering one?”

A long breath passed through him. “I know where it bends.”

Nia marked the answer as spatial recall without prompting. The route geometry from C-9 had not been projected into the room. He could have recovered it from the signal layer during transfer, but the question had supplied none of its particulars.

Cal’s clerk spoke through the ceiling channel. “Custodian asks whether identity can be tested within the authorized depth.”

“Not yet,” Nia said.

The Crown officer shifted outside the door. “Callis received authority for orientation and preservation assessment. Not testimony.”

Nia did not turn toward him. “Then stop asking for identity.”

“I asked nothing.”

“You came here knowing the source might answer.”

The body’s gaze moved toward the officer’s voice. His fingers closed again, this time around the edge of the sheet.

“Crown,” he said.

No one in the room had named Aster Crown.

Nia placed two fingers against the pulse point at his wrist. His heart rate had climbed to one hundred twenty. “Reduce auditory input from the corridor.”

Osei lowered the room’s outer channel. The officer’s mouth continued moving behind the glass, but his voice vanished.

“Stay with me,” Nia said. “You are on Callis. We will not ask for broad memory. I need to know whether this body can support you without causing avoidable harm.”

“Support.” The word came out dry. “For how long?”

“We measure coherence, not time. Today’s authorization remains narrow.”

“Whose authorization?”

“The Court of Continuance.”

His attention sharpened. Nia felt the change under her fingers before the monitors showed it. Pulse steadied. Breathing shortened by choice rather than weakness.

“Am I evidence?” he asked.

“We have not decided what legal category applies.”

“That means yes.”

“It means the court has not decided.”

His mouth drew sideways. The expression made the neutral features briefly specific. “Courts do enjoy changing a noun after the verb.”

Osei lowered his head, hiding a reaction. Iven, if this person carried Iven’s pattern, retained humor. Nia could not record that as identity. She could record syntax not prompted by the source question.

“Can you tell me a name?” she asked.

He looked down at his chest. The sheet hid the body except for its arms and shoulders. “This does not help.”

“No.”

“Do you know it?”

“Not with enough confidence to give it to you.”

“That is careful.”

“It has to be.”

“Careful people built this?” He lifted one hand until the safety strap stopped it. The movement cost him two breaths. “A speaking body without a face.”

“A face could contaminate recognition. The body has speech, basic sight, proprioception, and limited endurance.”

“Hunger.”

“Yes.”

“Cold feet.”

“The cradle heat has not reached them.”

“Then your basic design has ambitions.”

Nia adjusted the lower mesh. The body flinched at the first warmth, then let his legs settle. She had been trained to treat such complaints as calibration data. The training now felt insufficient. Pain did not become less personal because it helped tune a machine.

A route prompt appeared on her slate, sent by Cal’s clerk. It contained three unlabeled points and a fourth marker outside their triangle. The test could establish whether he reconstructed geometry beyond the exposed record. It could also draw him deeper into a pattern that each question spent.

Nia declined the prompt.

“Reason?” the clerk asked.

“Orientation remains unstable. We can test without recall demand.”

She reached for three sealed instrument trays and placed them on the table where he could see. Their corners formed an uneven triangle. A saline clamp lay near the cradle rail.

“I am going to move the clamp,” she said. “Tell me where it would sit if each tray exerted equal route influence.”

His eyes crossed the arrangement once. “Not on the table.”

“Explain.”

“The far tray carries less mass.”

“All three trays weigh the same.”

“No. That one is tethered to the wall.”

Nia followed his gaze. A transparent custody thread linked the third tray to a seal point. She had not noticed its slight tension.

“Assume the thread carries no load.”

“It carries one. Your handrail rotates under us. The wall pulls spinward against the table.”

He described a point three centimeters above the surface and left of Nia’s first estimate. Osei entered the room’s current ring load into the geometry slate. The calculated point appeared within four millimeters of the location he had named.

The body had no vestibular history, yet he had inferred Callis rotation from pressure, tether angle, and visible drift. The skill could have survived in a route cartographer’s pattern. It could not identify the man.

Nia set the clamp down and checked the tremor in his forearm. It had acquired a regular interval, too slow for neural noise. When she touched the wrist, his hand rotated away from her with more control than the first grasp had shown.

“I need to test whether sensation agrees with movement,” she said. “No memory questions.”

“Your categories keep improving.”

“Lift your left index finger.”

He did. The effort pulled a line through the tendons of his forearm.

“Now close your eyes and tell me which finger I touch.”

“Will it hurt?”

“No.”

“You should have started there.”

His eyes closed. Nia touched the smallest finger, then the thumb, using the blunt end of a sterile marker. He answered both correctly. On the third contact, she touched nothing and waited.

“You stopped,” he said.

“How do you know?”

“The sheet moved when your sleeve crossed it before. Now your breathing changed, but the linen did not.”

Nia recorded the distinction. He had not merely received sensation. He had formed an expectation from two prior trials and rejected a false one. Cal might consider that relevant to agency. Crown would call it adaptive processing available to any sufficiently complex archive. Both arguments could spend him long before either side admitted what the spending meant.

She offered him a glucose swab. “The body needs energy. This will taste sweet and slightly metallic.”

“Does it have to?”

“You may refuse. Hunger will shorten the safe interval.”

He considered the swab as though it occupied a disputed route. “Would refusal tell your court something?”

“Everything you do may enter the record. That does not make every act a legal test.”

“That sounds like a test designed by someone who fears answers.”

“It describes a room where every instrument records.”

After another breath, he opened his mouth. Nia touched the swab to his tongue and withdrew it before the jaw could tire. His face tightened at the taste.

“You understated the metal,” he said.

“I will correct the warning.”

“For the next body?”

“For your next choice, if there is one.”

The words brought his gaze back to her. Nia wished she could retrieve them. A next choice implied another activation, and she had no evidence the source could afford one. Yet pretending this interval stood alone would hide the possibility he had already raised.

At the room’s outer wall, the quarantine officer pressed his seal to the observation pane. Aster Crown’s three nested route arcs glowed white around his thumb. He was marking a procedural objection without opening audio. The timing mattered: not during orientation, not during the route inference, but after Iven asked whether another body might follow.

Nia asked Osei to capture the seal time. Osei did so without looking away from the phase graph.

“Does he object to me eating?” the man asked.

“He objects to the scope of contact.”

“Does scope include whether I happen more than once?”

“We will ask him after you rest.”

“That protects him from answering me.”

“It protects you from his answer becoming another demand on recall.”

His tongue moved against the glucose taste. “You may believe that.”

“I do.”

“Did I pass?” he asked.

“There is no pass yet.”

“Another careful noun.”

Nia removed the trays. A tremor had begun in his left forearm. She checked the transfer graph. Coherence demand remained within the narrow allocation, but the body’s glucose uptake had doubled.

“Rest your eyes,” she said.

“If I do, will I wake?”

“Not without authorization.”

“That did not answer me.”

“No,” Nia said. “I cannot promise you another waking.”

He kept his eyes open.

The room clock advanced through seventy seconds of breathing. Crown’s officer stood with his shoulder turned to the glass, speaking into a private channel. Cal’s clerk sent no further prompts. Osei monitored the phase draw from C-9 and tapped two fingers against his own wrist whenever the curve changed. They had agreed on that silent signal because open discussion of coherence might alarm the person in the body. Nia now recognized the concealment as a decision made for their comfort.

“You are consuming a finite state,” she said. “The process changes the source. I do not yet know by how much.”

His eyes shifted to her. “You told me.”

“I should have said it before the tests.”

“Would I have refused?”

“I do not know.”

“Neither do I.”

The answer left no dramatic trace on the monitors. It changed the room anyway. Nia had expected confusion, gratitude, or accusation. Instead he located the missing choice precisely and left it between them.

Osei raised two fingers, then one. Phase demand had begun rising.

“We need to reduce depth,” Nia said. “I want one orientation question before we bring you down. You may decline.”

“Now I may.”

“Yes.”

“Ask.”

“What place do you associate with the route you described?”

His eyelids tightened. “Dark side. Collector light on the rim. Caverns under black carbon.”

“Do you know its name?”

A shallow line formed between his brows. “Eiren.”

The Crown officer stopped speaking behind the glass.

“Eiren Reach?” Nia asked.

The body’s heart rate surged. His right hand pulled hard enough to crease the strap.

“Do not supply it,” he said.

“I am sorry.”

“You put the answer in my mouth and call the mouth neutral.”

Nia withdrew one step. The accusation landed at the exact point where procedure had failed. She had used the public registry name to complete uncertain recall. A hostile reviewer could now challenge every later recognition of Eiren.

“Record examiner contamination,” she said. “Place name supplied by Nia Daro before confirmation.”

Cal’s clerk acknowledged it.

The man watched her speak for the record. His expression changed from anger to concentration. He focused on her lips, then on the hand she held near the cradle control.

“Do that again,” he said.

“Do what?”

“Count before you touch it.”

“I did not count.”

“You breathed four in, held two, then moved on the next out-breath.”

Nia’s hand remained above the control. She used that cadence during delicate phase work: four to settle, two to test for tremor, action on release. Many resonantists used breath discipline, but not that exact sequence. Her father had taught it to her with cargo seals when she was twelve. She had adapted it years later for auric glass.

“Where did you learn that cadence?” she asked.

His gaze lost focus. The phase graph rose another point.

“Glass room,” he said. “Not this one. Bright restraints. Your voice behind me.”

Nia felt pressure across her fingertips as if she still held the probe inside C-9.

“How do you know it was my voice?”

“I don’t.” He swallowed. “The count belongs to you.”

“You have heard me today.”

“Today you said the numbers inside your breath. Before, you tried not to.”

Osei stared through the isolation glass. Nia could see the objection forming in him: memory contamination, pattern completion, stress. Each offered a possible explanation. None explained why the man associated her private cadence with another room.

“Do you remember what I said?” she asked.

The phase alarm gave one low tone.

Osei entered the room without waiting for permission. “Depth is climbing. We stop.”

Nia checked the graph. The authorized layer had begun drawing from a neighboring band. Broad recall could tear sequence from the remaining pattern, and they lacked any estimate of what C-9 could survive.

“Reduce transfer to orientation minimum,” she said.

The body turned his head toward Osei. “He trusts you less than he did.”

“That is not relevant.”

“It may be the only new fact I have.”

Osei reached the secondary control. “You can blame me when breathing does not cost you memory.”

The man examined him with a route worker’s economy, locating hands, controls, and exits. “Good. Keep that sentence.”

Nia lowered the first coupling. Warmth drained from the neck lattice. His eyes widened.

“Wait.”

“We have reached the safe limit.”

“I need my name.”

“You do not have to produce it now.”

“I had it.” His breath hitched. “Route office. Duty code IK-seven. Registry hand signs the second mark low because the first I tears the paper.”

Nia did not help him.

His mouth opened once without sound. Then the pattern aligned behind his face.

“Iven Ko,” he said.

The room record placed the name after his route inference, his description of Eiren, and his recognition of Nia’s cadence. It arrived without anyone offering it. That sequence mattered. Nia felt no relief.

“Iven Ko,” she repeated. “Do you consent to us preserving today’s record for the Court?”

His gaze fixed on her hand above the coupling. “Preserving which part?”

“All of it, including my contamination and your request to stop.”

“I did not request to stop.”

“Osei did on safety grounds.”

“Then preserve that too.”

“I need your answer.”

“Yes. Keep it.”

She lowered the second coupling.

His pupils expanded as phase support narrowed. The vocal body continued breathing, but the person’s control over it began to loosen. His fingers moved against the sheet in small route angles. Nia recognized the same triangular relation C-9 had drawn on Osei’s slate.

“Crown already tried you,” she said, careful to make it a question through tone rather than grammar.

The fingers stopped.

His eyes found the quarantine officer beyond the glass, then came back to Nia.

“No,” Iven said. “You already tried me.”

The words stripped distance from the old glass room. Not Crown alone. A voice using Nia’s cadence. Her breath guiding a control she could not remember touching.

“Where?” she asked.

Osei caught her wrist before she could raise transfer again. “Nia.”

Iven’s breathing failed to match the body. The ventilator resumed support with a soft valve click.

“Do not spend more,” Osei said.

Nia released the control. She hated him for one second because his hand prevented an answer. Then the hatred found its proper object.

She told the record, “No further recall. Bring him to rest.”

The coupling dimmed in stages. Iven looked toward the ceiling as the room receded from him. His voice came through one final breath, too quiet for the corridor glass but clear under Nia’s hand at his throat.

“How many times have I been called?”

No one answered before his eyes closed.

Chapter 3 — The Novel Preference

Cal broke the emergency seal while Crown’s objection still occupied the recorder.

The seal came apart in two paper-thin disks. One remained on the preservation order before him. The other adhered to his thumb, warm from the timing lamp. Callis kept physical seals for orders affecting bodies because a clerk could not revise torn alloy after the fact. Cal placed his half beneath the lamp and watched the minute enter its surface.

Across the intake chamber, a Crown duty counsel spoke from the wall screen. “Aster Crown Transit objects to any order that presumes personhood. Bell C-9 entered Callis inventory as salvage mass. The phase layer remains part of that property.”

“Your objection reached the record before my order,” Cal said. “No one will mistake the sequence.”

“That does not answer title.”

“It preserves the subject long enough to answer title.”

Crown’s counsel looked past the camera, likely toward someone supplying language. Quarantine had closed the archive ring to ordinary traffic and stalled three mass inspections. Every additional minute gave Crown another operational claim. Delay usually protected Cal from irreversible mistakes. Here, delay consumed the object under dispute.

Nia Daro sat at the side table with her hands flat beside the activation record. Osei Marr occupied the chair behind her. Neither wore archive work gloves. Cal had required bare hands so the chamber record would show they carried no phase tools. The precaution bordered on theater, but theater could keep a frightened institution from inventing a weapon.

“State the operative line,” Nia said.

Cal read from the order. “Until formal intake determines classification, no party may move, query, destructively examine, transfer, erase, or deepen access to the phase layer held in choir bell C-9.”

“Does that include Callis?”

“It includes everyone.”

“Does it include medical support for the vocal body?”

“Support may continue at orientation depth. No recall prompt without separate leave.”

Nia’s fingers moved closer together. “Orientation depth still spends coherence.”

“Can the body be maintained without phase support?”

“The tissue can. Iven cannot.”

Crown’s counsel leaned toward the screen. “The name remains unverified.”

Cal turned one page in the intake packet. “A source recovered the name without prompting after demonstrating occupation-specific route calculation. Verification remains incomplete. The record may identify him as Iven Ko with that limitation attached.”

“Your language moves the dispute.”

“All language moves a dispute. Accurate limits tell us how far.”

He had used the same answer during ration appeals when storage authorities called hungry households demand units. A neutral noun often concealed the person required to carry its consequences. Yet courts also harmed people by naming them too early, then using sympathy to excuse weak evidence. Cal intended to make Crown attack the facts rather than his concern.

A low chime came from Nia’s slate. She read it and stood.

“The body has begun fighting ventilation,” she said. “If he reaches full motor control without orientation support, he may injure his throat.”

Cal checked the emergency authorization. It allowed contact necessary to prevent immediate harm. “Open audio only. Ask whether he wants support raised enough to speak.”

Crown’s counsel objected.

“Recorded,” Cal said.

Nia linked the chamber to the vocal-body room. The wall displayed no image at first, only a breathing trace and tissue temperatures. Cal heard the ventilator click. A second rhythm moved beneath it, irregular and deliberate.

“Iven,” Nia said, “this is Nia Daro. You are still on Callis. Your body is breathing under support.”

The irregular rhythm stopped.

“We can raise phase support enough for speech,” she continued. “That will consume some of the state in C-9. You may also remain at rest while we maintain the tissue.”

Three breaths passed.

One finger signal appeared on the monitor, then a second. Nia interpreted it. “He wants speech.”

“Did you establish that signal before?” Cal asked.

“No. One finger could mean yes by ordinary convention. Two could mean repeat, pain, or error.”

Cal bent toward the channel. “I am Cal Orsen, intake custodian for the Court of Continuance. If you understand that raising support may cost you future coherence and you still want enough support to speak, close your left hand once. If you do not want it, close your right.”

The left grip sensor rose.

“Raise to speech threshold,” Cal said.

Crown’s screen filled with a written objection rather than a voice. Cal let it remain visible. Nia adjusted the remote coupling. The breathing trace steadied, and a raw sound entered the chamber.

“Too many hands,” Iven said.

“On what?” Cal asked.

“My breathing.”

“That concern belongs in this intake.”

“Does it?”

“Yes. We need to decide whether the law treats you as property, a protected record, or a person with standing in continuance.”

The phrase drew silence from the vocal-body room.

Cal continued, “Under the Continuance Articles, a transmitted remnant begins as property. That rule permits preservation and ordinary analysis. It also allows an owner to control access unless a court finds another protected status.”

“Who owns me?”

“Crown claims the phase layer. Callis holds C-9 under a transfer record. Custody and title remain disputed.”

Iven took two assisted breaths. “You answered who owns the glass.”

“Yes.”

“That was not the question.”

“No,” Cal said. “It was not.”

Nia looked at him. Her expression held no gratitude. She wanted less accuracy from him and more protection, though she would have rejected either if it lacked a record.

Crown’s counsel restored audio. “Custodian, the Articles do not allow a remnant’s rhetorical distinction to establish legal personality.”

“I agree.”

The answer checked both the counsel and Nia. Cal turned the Novel Preference Test page toward the timing lamp, though Iven could not see it.

“The test asks whether you can form a preference not recoverable from the intent encoded in your source signal,” Cal said. “A stored request repeated accurately does not suffice. Nor does skill, self-description, fear, or pain by itself. The preference must arise under present conditions and differ in substance from what the record already contains.”

“Why?” Iven asked.

“Because records can model answers. Personhood requires evidence of someone encountering a condition the source did not already resolve.”

“Your court trusts novelty more than continuity.”

“It distrusts ownership more when novelty appears.”

Iven’s next breath carried the edge of a laugh. “That sounds rehearsed.”

“It has survived hostile questions.”

“Not the same measure.”

Cal marked the exchange. Dry humor had persuasive force but no legal weight. He could not let liking the man alter the burden.

Nia drew the activation record closer. “Give him an example that does not disclose another person’s case.”

Crown’s counsel said, “The Articles do not require instructional assistance.”

“They require comprehension,” Cal replied. “A test hidden from its subject measures obedience to the examiner.”

He chose the ordinary training example used for custodians. “Suppose a navigator transmits a record whose only request directs rescuers to recover a vessel. Later, during valid contact, the navigator learns the vessel carried a dangerous contamination unknown at transmission. A present choice to destroy that vessel could not come from the original rescue instruction. That choice might satisfy the test if the person understands the new information and the consequences.”

“What if the navigator still chooses rescue?” Iven asked.

“Then we examine why. The same action can carry a new preference if present reasons differ from the encoded ones, but proving that requires care.”

“So you inspect reasons until one pleases your law.”

“We separate a current decision from a stored instruction.”

“With questions that consume the answerer.”

“Yes.” Cal did not soften it. “That defect has greater force in your case than in most.”

Nia tapped the phase-use line with one fingernail. “The training example assumes repeated interviews cause no damage. Here, each attempt to separate motive from memory spends the state you claim to assess.”

“Agreed.”

“Then order a presumption in favor of personhood.”

Crown’s counsel leaned forward. “On the basis of a technician’s objection to the statutory design?”

“On the basis of uncertainty created by a destructive test,” Nia said. “Crown wrote a salvage classification that kept the layer outside every medical review. It cannot use the resulting absence of proof as proof of absence.”

The counsel’s reply came without delay. “Callis accepted the transfer and maintained the same classification for seven years.”

Osei moved in his chair. “We received a bell with blank provenance and missing attachments.”

“You are not a party to this intake.”

“He witnessed the discovery,” Cal said. “He may correct a factual claim.”

“Then require him to state whether Callis ever challenged the classification before yesterday.”

Osei’s jaw tightened. “Not in the record I reviewed.”

Crown’s counsel let the admission remain.

Cal looked at the sealed order. Presuming personhood would protect Iven now but expose the order to quick reversal. Refusing any protection would let property law perform the damage before a court could hear him. The Articles offered a narrow bridge: preserve first, test under bounded conditions, and accept that the bridge itself consumed what crossed it.

“I will not presume a final status,” Cal said. “I will assign the highest temporary protection needed to prevent the jurisdictional question from being destroyed. That protection binds Callis as tightly as Crown.”

“Does it bind me?” Iven asked.

“In what sense?”

“If I ask you not to wake me again before your test, do you preserve that choice or call it invalid because I have not passed?”

The chamber recorder showed every channel active. Cal could have answered narrowly: the present authorization concerned medical support, not future refusal. Narrow answers had helped him keep bad law from spreading during ration appeals. They had also left people carrying consequences the order did not name.

“You may refuse medical contact during this interval,” he said. “A broader refusal of future embodiment raises the same status question we have not resolved. I would preserve the request and prevent access long enough for argument. I cannot promise the present law would honor it permanently.”

Iven breathed through two assisted cycles. “So property can ask not to be used.”

“Anyone capable of asking creates a fact the court must address.”

“That still avoids the noun.”

“It does.”

From behind Nia, Osei said, “He noticed your avoidance. Does that count as novelty?”

“No,” Cal said. “The ability to infer does not establish a preference.”

“Neither did route skill, pain, a name, or choosing speech.”

“Each matters to whether we have a competent subject. None alone answers the statutory question.”

Osei looked toward the blank wall rather than Crown’s counsel. “How much of a person do you need before you permit him to want something new?”

Cal had no answer that improved when spoken quickly. “Enough to keep an owner from manufacturing the novelty through prompts. Enough to keep a damaged recording from inheriting obligations it never chose. The burden protects as well as excludes.”

“Who does it protect here?” Nia asked.

“At present, I do not know.”

A soft valve noise entered from the vocal-body room. Iven’s breath caught, then resumed. Cal watched the support trace recover. Every legal distinction in the chamber depended on a body enduring their time.

On Crown’s screen, counsel sent a summary of C-9’s registered contents. Distress routing, convoy geometry, personnel identifiers, and a request for inquiry appeared in a narrow column. The summary omitted the hidden layer’s responsive behavior. It also treated every intention in the deposition as recoverable from the record, which made any related choice presumptively old.

“Can you administer the Novel Preference Test now?” Nia asked.

“Not through invention,” Cal said. “A valid test needs a condition absent from the source, a choice with meaningful alternatives, and enough orientation for the subject to understand the consequence.”

“The body cannot safely remain at speech depth while counsel designs a condition.”

“Then we do not design one today.”

Crown’s counsel nodded once. Agreement from that screen warned Cal more than opposition.

“Without a passed test,” counsel said, “the source remains analyzable property. Crown requests a title-preserving transfer into our quarantine vault.”

“No.”

“On what authority?”

“My preservation jurisdiction.”

“You cannot issue permanent protection to property held under contested title.”

“I am not issuing permanence.” Cal placed the second seal half on the order. “I am preventing an irreversible act while the court can still determine which law applies.”

“Callis cannot prevent natural phase decay.”

“Natural decay differs from removal, analysis, or erasure. My order reaches acts.”

Counsel’s face sharpened. “A failure to move C-9 may also constitute an act if the bell threatens a mass schedule.”

“Provide the engineering basis under seal. General corridor authority will not defeat a specific bodily risk before classification.”

“You just called it bodily.”

“I called the risk bodily. We have a vocal body under active support. Classification remains open.”

Cal pressed the seal halves together. Their inner surfaces fused, binding the text. The preservation order now held until the intake could be replaced by a standing determination. Crown could appeal it, but not edit its terms.

In the linked room, the ventilator increased pressure by a fraction. Iven coughed. Nia reached toward her slate, then stopped short of touching the control without asking.

“Iven,” she said, “your throat is drying. We can administer a comfort block. It will reduce pain and damp higher recall for several minutes.”

“Will I still speak?”

“Yes, but abstract recall may become difficult.”

“Names?”

“Possibly.”

Cal heard linen move near the body microphone. The breathing trace spiked and settled.

“Do you consent?” Nia asked.

“No.”

Crown’s counsel began, “A refusal of medication cannot satisfy the test when bodily discomfort itself supplies the preference.”

“I have made no finding,” Cal said.

Nia kept her focus on the remote room. “Pain is increasing. You can change your answer at any point.”

“I know.”

“Do you want the support interval ended?”

“Not yet.”

“What do you need?”

A delay followed. Cal watched the trace for signs of instability. Iven had declined immediate comfort after learning it might impair names. That choice belonged to the present body, but the source deposition had centered on identifying the dead and preserving their duties. Refusing comfort to continue the same task might reveal agency. It might also repeat the strongest encoded purpose in C-9.

“How many reached Dayward Three?” Iven asked.

Nia looked at Cal before answering. “We have not reconstructed Eiren’s final civic record.”

“Did anyone?”

“The public registry lists no recovered embodied survivors.”

“That does not answer the shelter.”

“No. We do not have that answer yet.”

Iven’s grip sensor tightened against the sheet. “Names?”

“We have a partial registry,” Nia said. “It has not been compared with your deposition.”

“Then compare it before you ask me who I am again.”

The room absorbed his sentence through its acoustic baffles. Cal marked the timing: comfort offered, consequence explained, comfort refused, inquiry directed toward the dead. No prompt had suggested Eiren, Dayward Three, or the registry during the interval. Iven had made a choice and spent something to sustain it.

Crown’s counsel spoke carefully. “The distress record contains registry concerns. This preference remains recoverable from source intent.”

“Yes,” Cal said.

Nia turned on him. “He chose pain.”

“He chose delay of comfort to pursue an encoded objective. That matters, but it does not yet pass the test.”

“What would?”

“A choice the deposition could not have prepared.”

Iven’s voice reached them before counsel could reply. “You want me to betray my purpose so you can recognize me.”

Cal felt the old ration chamber around him for one unwelcome second: families required to prove unusual need by displaying damage different from everyone else’s. A test could protect against fraud and still punish continuity.

“I want the law to lose its claim on your body,” Cal said. “The Articles give me one route to do that. I will not pretend you have completed it when Crown can overturn the finding by reading your own deposition.”

“Reversible orders,” Iven said.

Cal’s hand stopped above his notes. “What about them?”

“You speak as if each door stays open.”

No one had told Iven Cal’s judicial habit. The remark might come from ordinary inference. Cal had described temporary orders, deferred tests, and preservation. A skilled listener could hear the pattern.

“This door does not,” Cal said. “Your coherence declines even if the court proceeds carefully.”

“At last, a noun with a cost.”

The phase-support alarm sounded through the remote feed. Nia read its curve.

“We reduce him now,” she said. “No extension.”

Cal addressed Iven before she touched the control. “I am issuing the preservation order. It bars destructive access and transfer pending a standing hearing. You have not passed the Novel Preference Test today. The hearing will consider a properly bounded condition and your ability to choose under it.”

“When?”

Cal looked to Nia.

“Three days for a stable body field,” she said. “Longer would be safer.”

“How long can the source wait?”

“I do not know. C-9’s preservation margin changed after today’s contact.”

Delay no longer offered moral shelter. Cal opened the Court calendar. The earliest full chamber sat five days away. He overrode it, displaced two commercial matters, and created an emergency session in seventy-two hours.

“Day five of this action,” he said. “We will begin at first civic light.”

Crown’s counsel objected to notice, venue, testing scope, Callis custody, and the displaced schedule. Each objection entered as a separate line. Cal accepted them without argument.

Nia lowered phase support. Iven’s speech thinned.

“Compare the names,” he said.

“We will,” she answered.

“Before you call.”

“Yes.”

His breathing continued under the ventilator after his control receded. Cal watched the left-hand grip sensor flatten. A living tissue body remained in the room, warm and maintained, while the person whose law he had just postponed became inaccessible by order.

Cal sealed the hearing notice with a fresh alloy disk. He set the timing lamp for seventy-two hours and sent copies to Crown, Callis Archive, and the medical custodian. The lamp began its count beside the fused preservation order.

Before the vocal body could safely remain awake, the Court would have to decide whether its next awakening belonged to an owner or to Iven Ko.

Chapter 4 — Crown Quarantine

Ada pressed the injunction key into Callis’s pressure door and felt the lock reject it.

A red line traveled around the key’s rim, paused at the Crown authorization mark, then reversed. On the other side of the glass, Nia Daro stood with her hands clasped behind her back. The archive resonantist had removed every tool from her belt except a timing lamp. She looked less disarmed than prepared to prove who entered carrying force.

“Your corridor authority reached the door,” Nia said through the intercom. “Cal’s preservation order reached it first.”

Ada examined the lock log. The Court’s seal occupied the highest local layer. Her injunction controlled movement of hazardous mass, not access to a body under judicial protection. Callis had configured the door to require both keys while leaving each side able to accuse the other of obstruction.

“Open under joint custody,” Ada said.

“State purpose.”

“Inspection of the alleged vocal embodiment, review of mass-schedule risk, and service of Crown’s title claim.”

“Will you touch C-9?”

“Not without a separate record.”

Nia brought her timing lamp to the glass. Ada placed the injunction key beside it. Their seals aligned across the pressure pane, and the door opened by six centimeters before checking both air systems. Archive air carried oil cloth and a trace of coolant. Ada stepped through when the strip turned green.

Two Crown officers waited behind her. She ordered them to remain in the corridor.

“If this is hazardous mass,” one began, “protocol requires escort.”

“If it becomes hazardous while I am inside, your presence will not improve its tuning.”

The officer looked at the three route arcs on her collar and stayed outside.

Nia led Ada toward the vocal-body room without offering a handrail route. Callis’s archive ring pulled sideways under rotation. A visitor who failed to read the floor’s narrow wear stripes could drift into the wall at each connector. Ada had crossed worse industrial rings before Nia finished resonance school. She matched the spin and kept pace.

“Your duty counsel described the person as a source throughout intake,” Nia said.

“Counsel used the classification currently in law.”

“He asked for transfer after hearing Iven speak.”

“Hearing speech does not settle whether a record generates it.”

“You heard him on the activation record.”

“Yes.”

Nia glanced at her. “And?”

Ada did not answer while they passed an open maintenance recess. Every wall surface could carry a local pickup under quarantine. She had reviewed the record twice during transit from Crown’s operations spine. A damaged man had inferred ring load, named himself, remembered Nia’s cadence, and asked how many times he had been called. Ada had also reviewed adaptive route archives that produced convincing first-person error reports without consciousness. Certainty in either direction offered comfort she could not afford.

The vocal-body room stood behind a clear isolation wall. Its lights had been lowered to protect the resting tissue. A man lay beneath a gray sheet with warm saline circulating through a throat line. Neutral features revealed no age beyond tissue maturity. The left hand rested open beside the hip. Monitors showed respiration, glucose use, peripheral temperature, and a flat line for phase support.

Ada stopped farther from the glass than Nia expected.

She knew the room.

Not its current equipment or Callis markings. She knew the arrangement preserved in an old Crown risk file: body cradle against a phase wall, operator behind the right shoulder, emergency cutoff beyond the subject’s sight. Twelve years earlier, while reviewing inherited corridor liabilities, Ada had opened a restricted file labeled ER-47 / ROUTE REMNANT / RESPONSE CONTROL. Its diagrams showed a bright restraint room. No body images survived. The notes warned that repeated contact produced unstable sequence and uncontrolled accusations.

The file had not named Iven Ko in its searchable fields.

“You recognize something,” Nia said.

Ada shifted her gaze to the neck lattice. “Crown used a similar room architecture.”

“When?”

“In historical resonance work.”

“On C-9?”

“I cannot establish that from architecture.”

“Can you establish it from his memory of bright restraints?”

“No.”

Nia held the timing lamp between them. “Say what you can establish.”

Ada could state that a restricted risk file connected an Eiren route remnant to response-control work. Doing so would concede prior access before Crown reviewed privilege, custody, and operational consequences. She could also deny recognition and let the activation record become the first lie spoken under Callis seal.

“I have seen a Crown file concerning an Eiren-associated route remnant,” she said. “It described attempts to obtain a controlled account. I have not verified whether that remnant occupied C-9.”

The lamp marked her words.

Nia’s hands separated. “How many attempts?”

“The extract I saw did not provide a reliable count.”

“What did they cost?”

“It did not quantify coherence.”

“Who authorized them?”

“Executive fields were masked in the review copy.”

Nia looked through the glass at the resting body. “Convenient file.”

“No. Dangerous file. Convenience would have made it easier to use.”

Ada’s wrist channel pulsed. Jalen Orr requested private priority contact. She declined once and received an override warning. Crown chairs rarely used that flag because the use itself entered corporate retention.

“I need a sealed room,” Ada said.

“You have one.” Nia indicated the vocal-body observation bay. “Nothing spoken there reaches the body channel while phase support remains off.”

“Does Callis record it?”

“Everything inside quarantine has a custody record.”

“Then I need a room Crown can seal.”

Nia considered, then opened a service booth across the corridor. “Five minutes. The booth records access and duration, not content, unless your channel declares an emergency.”

Ada entered and pressed her palm to the privacy field. Jalen appeared above the narrow console, scaled smaller than life. His silver hair and dark coat looked untouched by his previous twenty hours awake.

“You saw it,” he said.

“I saw a maintained body with no phase support.”

“You reviewed the intake.”

“Yes.”

“Then you know the risk category.”

“I recognize a likely match.”

Jalen’s image looked toward something beyond his camera. “The Callis order cannot survive a corridor-safety appeal. Serve the injunction, isolate the bell, and collapse the responsive layer before the standing hearing.”

Ada kept both hands in view of the console. “Collapse how?”

“Routine inerting. Outer-field saturation followed by mass normalization.”

“That would erase the layer.”

“It would remove an unauthorized instability from a scheduled bell.”

“C-9 has no active schedule.”

“All tuned mass participates in Mouth stability whether or not it opens a window.”

The claim carried enough truth to mislead a general court. A bell in Callis’s ring contributed passive load. Its hidden phase structure could alter density under stress. Yet an inerting pulse strong enough to collapse the responsive layer would produce a signature across every balance monitor on the station.

“Cal’s order expressly bars erasure,” Ada said.

“Your injunction predates his competence over Crown mass.”

“The transfer record gives Callis title on its face. We can dispute that record. We cannot treat it as nonexistent while relying on its mass declaration.”

Jalen lowered his voice. “Ada, the Meridian Mouth holds medicine routes, heat-sink changes, food catalysts, and every political promise Crown has made around travel time. If Callis broadcasts a conscious accusation tied to an old emergency choice, governors will demand bell suspensions before anyone understands what suspension costs.”

“I understand the risk.”

“Then remove the trigger.”

Ada brought the live Mouth schedule onto the booth console. The current window map showed freight already committed across three rotations. A catalyst train waited at the far alignment; two medical carriers occupied the next return corridor. Their labels did not establish imminent catastrophe, but they showed why a rumor of bell sabotage could spread faster than a court order.

“If governors suspend Crown bells after disclosure,” Jalen said, “the corridor loses recoverable alignment.”

“That assumes every governor responds at once.”

“It assumes they respond to their voters.”

“And your proposed erasure assumes no one responds to a visible phase collapse inside a court quarantine.”

Jalen expanded the schedule. Morcant’s refinery code appeared beside an outbound mass request. Ada had been born under that habitat’s low copper ceilings. Her mother still described travel windows by the medicine they brought. None of those people had chosen what Crown did at Eiren. Their dependence gave Ada a reason to control disclosure, not permission to eliminate the witness.

“You know what interrupted windows mean at Morcant,” Jalen said.

“Do not use my birthplace as your authorization.”

“I am using a supply ledger.”

“Then show the ledger to the Court and accept questions about contingency stock.”

“Public questioning creates the interruption.”

Ada closed the schedule overlay. “That circular claim can justify secrecy forever. It cannot justify an act that announces itself through the mass ring.”

“The response can be damped.”

“Not below Callis’s local instruments. C-9 sits inside their archive ring. A saturation pulse changes its phase density before any Crown field can counterbalance it. Their gantry probes will record the onset. Their ring trims will record the correction. Your own operations spine will issue an automatic anomaly packet.”

“Packets can be held.”

“The physical timing lamps cannot.”

For the first time, Jalen looked uncertain rather than displeased. Ada knew he had received the inerting proposal from counsel or an executive technician who viewed Callis through remote schematics. Neither had stood beside the bell’s smoked housing or seen the custody lamp Nia wore like a second pulse.

“Inerting would register as a destructive phase event under active court seal. Callis would detect it at the bell. The Mouth control spine would detect the mass correction. Every insurer with a balance feed would see the timestamp. We would convert an allegation of old concealment into proof of present destruction.”

“Only if you describe the signature that way.”

“Physics will describe it before counsel does.”

Jalen’s expression tightened by a degree. “You have defended the Eiren seal for twelve years.”

“I defended continuity while no lawful inquiry could preserve both evidence and infrastructure. I did not agree to destroy evidence after a court took custody.”

“You defended Crown.”

“I defended a system people need. If Crown forces me to choose between the system and an act that exposes it to seizure, I will refuse the act.”

Silence held the private channel. Ada heard the service booth fan increase as her body heat accumulated.

Jalen said, “Then give the court a lawful reason to examine the layer before it confers personhood.”

The alternative opened immediately. It carried its own damage.

“Compulsory analysis,” Ada said.

“The Articles already provide it.”

“Only for non-person property.”

“Which Cal has declined to classify otherwise.”

“A destructive read could spend the subject before the test.”

Jalen’s eyes did not move. “Then design a narrow one.”

Ada ended the channel before he could frame the instruction as agreement. The booth displayed a retention notice for the override and the call’s duration. She sealed both into her injunction packet.

When she returned to the observation bay, Nia had not changed position.

“He asked you to erase C-9,” Nia said.

Ada looked at the timing lamp. Its green point remained active.

“Yes.”

The direct answer struck harder than any denial could have. Nia’s face lost color, but her voice stayed level. “What did you say?”

“No.”

“Because Iven may be a person?”

“Because erasure violates the order and would leave an operational signature visible across Callis and Crown control. Also because destroying disputed evidence during a hearing creates a crime no title argument can cure.”

“Personhood came fourth.”

“I did not list it.”

“You did not.”

Ada accepted the judgment. “I cannot certify consciousness from one activation. I can refuse illegal destruction without that finding.”

Nia turned toward the body. “He knew my cadence from a room I cannot remember.”

“I heard the record.”

“You knew such rooms existed.”

“I knew one file described them.”

“Did you preserve it?”

“The review system prevents local copies.”

“That was not my question.”

Ada remembered closing the risk file twelve years earlier and accepting its retention flag without demanding the masked annex. At the time, a corridor shutdown model had projected medicine failures before any substitute schedule could begin. She had treated the old remnant as one more inherited hazard attached to a living system.

“I left the file under Crown restriction,” she said.

“So no.”

“So not in a form you can reach.”

Nia lifted the timing lamp close to Ada’s collar. “Will you identify its registry key to Cal?”

“I will disclose that the file exists and seek protected review. If I release the key directly, Crown will claim privilege breach and remove me before I can authenticate it.”

“You want time.”

“I want evidence that survives the way we obtain it.”

Nia’s mouth tightened. “That answer sounds familiar.”
