The resemblance to Nia’s own archive discipline did not make Ada’s position kinder. It made refusal harder to dismiss.

A monitor beside the resting body changed color. Tissue temperature had fallen by two tenths. Nia adjusted the warming mesh from the exterior panel, then placed her hand against the glass above Iven’s open fingers. She did not wake him to ask permission for ordinary care. The distinction between maintaining tissue and summoning the person had become visible in the motion.

Ada opened her injunction packet on the observation console. “Crown will challenge Cal’s order under the Compulsory Evidence Clause.”

Nia turned. “You just refused destruction.”

“The clause does not require maximum access. It allows a court to order analysis of property when evidence cannot be obtained otherwise.”

“He is not property.”

“Cal has not found that yet.”

“You intend to exploit the interval his caution created.”

“I intend to force the classification question into the standing hearing instead of leaving it inside a corridor injunction. If Crown relies only on title, it can keep appealing custody. Under the clause, Crown must specify the evidence sought, the method, and the cost.”

“The cost falls on Iven.”

“Yes.”

Ada let the word remain without qualification. A narrow compulsory read might consume less than another broad activation. It might also give Crown a lawful path into the phase layer before Iven gained rights. Her duty to prevent catastrophic interruption did not convert either possibility into virtue.

Nia read the draft over Ada’s shoulder as it formed. Crown sought analysis of route integrity, source responsiveness, and the relation between C-9’s phase structure and Meridian Mouth stability. Ada removed a sentence requesting unrestricted semantic extraction. She limited physical access to existing Callis equipment and required continuous Court recording.

“You are writing my tools into the application,” Nia said.

“Would you prefer Crown equipment?”

“I would prefer no destructive query.”

“That preference will enter your response.”

“Do not reduce my objection to a filing slot.”

“Then give Cal the engineering reason it cannot be done safely.”

“We do not know the safe depth because prior access records remain hidden in your file.”

Ada stopped typing. That fact connected Crown’s concealment directly to present uncertainty. It gave Nia a strong argument against any analysis. It also gave Ada grounds to demand the old file before Jalen could direct another counsel to proceed without her limits.

She added a condition: no access until Crown produced all known prior-intervention metadata for in camera review.

Ada opened a method annex and forced each requested measurement into a separate line. Outer-layer temperature required no pulse. Static phase topology required a passive pickup. Response capacity required an active query and therefore remained disputed. Semantic extraction required more depth than Crown had justified. She struck the last request entirely.

Nia leaned closer despite herself. “Your static topology line hides a calibration touch.”

“Show me where.”

“A passive pickup has to be seated. Seating pressure can couple through damaged glass.”

“Then specify a no-contact field reader.”

“Callis has one in the resonance vault. Its resolution may not distinguish old stress from recent reads.”

“May it detect whether intervention occurred at all?”

“If the thermal residue survived.”

Ada inserted the no-contact reader and marked its limitations. “No one touches C-9 before this measurement.”

“Crown will argue your reader lacks certification for title evidence.”

“This application concerns preservation risk. Certification follows if anyone later offers the map on merits.”

Nia traced the method sequence without touching the console. The order of operations mattered: non-contact thermal imaging first, passive topology second, any active access only after judicial review. Crown had sought a route into the layer. Ada’s annex gave Callis a route around it, at least for the first inspection.

“Osei can run the thermal instrument,” Nia said.

“You supervise.”

“My cadence may already exist in the source as contamination.”

“That argues against your operating an active query. It does not prevent you from witnessing a passive map.”

Nia’s eyes moved toward the corridor leading back to C-9. “If we find evidence of earlier reads, the method stops.”

“The application will say prior destructive access triggers immediate Court review.”

“Not Crown review.”

“Court review. Crown receives notice and no instrument control.”

Ada added the sentence. The clause now constrained the party invoking it more tightly than Jalen intended. A court might still authorize deeper analysis, but the first lawful act would examine what Crown had already done rather than what Iven could be forced to provide.

Nia read it twice. “He will remove that.”

“He can try. My seal will show the change.”

“You still invoke the clause.”

“Yes.”

“Why help him?”

Ada looked through the glass at the body built to speak for a disputed layer. “Because if I do nothing, Crown will send someone who calls erasure maintenance. If I file this, the court sees the demand before any tool reaches C-9.”

“That protects procedure.”

“It protects a window in which you can prove the cost.”

Nia lowered the timing lamp. She did not agree. Agreement would have simplified Ada’s record and betrayed the body on the other side of the glass.

Ada pressed her seal into the application. The three nested arcs of Aster Crown appeared beneath the clause heading. She served one copy on Nia, one on Cal, and one on Crown control before Jalen could replace her draft.

The pressure door unlocked for her departure. Behind it, Crown’s officers straightened. Inside the observation room, Nia remained beside Iven’s resting hand with the application glowing on the glass.

Crown no longer asked to erase the answering layer. It asked the Court to call that layer analyzable property and compel it to yield evidence.

Chapter 5 — Heat in the Glass

Nia held the no-contact reader above C-9 while Osei lowered the gantry lights.

Darkness removed the bell’s smoked reflection from its own surface. Under the reader’s narrow field, auric glass emerged as layered amber lines, each one following a phase boundary laid down forty-seven years ago. The instrument sent no pulse. It measured residual thermal disorder by comparing how quickly each microscopic region released the room’s ordinary heat.

“Field stable,” Osei said.

“Custody channels?”

“Court lamp, local archive, Crown notice feed.”

“Crown instrument control?”

“None.”

Nia checked his slate. Ada’s method annex occupied the upper corner under three nested route arcs. The annex allowed one passive thermal map before any further argument under the Compulsory Evidence Clause. Crown would receive the processed map after Cal reviewed it. Raw sensor output would remain under joint seal.

That sequence no longer felt adequate.

She adjusted the reader’s height by half a millimeter. A shadow moved inside the seventh visible glass layer, too regular for corrosion and too small for the naked eye. It appeared as a crescent of retained heat around a cooler center. Osei increased contrast without changing field strength.

The crescent separated into three overlapping marks.

“Stop translation,” Nia said.

The reader froze above the service plate. Nothing touched C-9. The ring’s fan vibration traveled through Nia’s boots and up the gantry brace. She waited for two cycles, checking whether mechanical motion shifted the mark. It remained fixed within the glass.

Osei leaned toward his slate. “That should not be warm.”

“It is not warm in the ordinary sense.”

“Residual disorder?”

“Possibly.”

“From yesterday?”

Nia measured its depth. The crescent sat beneath the layer their calibration pulse had reached. Yesterday’s response had traveled through it but could not have created a deeper boundary without leaving a continuous path.

“No,” she said. “Older.”

She recorded the reader position, ring load, room temperature, and the exact contrast transform Osei had applied. Then she ordered a second pass from the opposite side of the bell. A false mark from the instrument would rotate with the field. A mark in the glass would hold its relation to C-9.

They shifted the reader along its air rail without crossing the service seam. Osei routed the power lead through a separate brace so its waste heat could not enter the image. The new angle took four minutes to settle.

The crescent appeared again.

Its cooler center widened under the oblique view. Nia saw a set of fine radial lines extending outward, like cracks without broken glass. She knew that geometry from certification work.

“Destructive read boundary,” she said.

Osei stopped breathing for a moment. “Certain?”

“No. I want the material atlas.”

He opened Callis’s reference library and selected the auric-glass failure set. They compared the mark against pressure shocks, field saturation, manufacturing voids, and slow thermal cycling. Each produced a different edge. Pressure damage spread along stress lines. Saturation left a smooth shell. Voids carried no radial release pattern. Only destructive phase extraction produced a cool center surrounded by a crescent where displaced energy had recombined.

The reference image matched.

Nia enlarged the center until the slate showed sensor pixels rather than a pleasing curve. Interpretation became dangerous when the instrument cleaned an image for the eye. At raw scale, the mark lost symmetry. One side carried a narrow tail that followed the phase grain.

Osei pointed without touching the display. “Read direction.”

“Yes.”

“Someone opened it before us.”

“At least once.”

The words changed custody. Until then, prior contact existed as Iven’s damaged recollection and Ada’s restricted-file recognition. The glass now carried physical evidence. Crown’s application required immediate Court review before any active access. Nia should have stopped the inspection, sealed the one finding, and called Cal.

Osei looked along the dark length of the bell. “If there is one, there may be others outside this slice.”

“The annex authorizes a complete passive thermal map.”

“It also says prior access stops the method.”

“It says prior access triggers review before active access.” Nia opened the text. Ada had chosen the words precisely. “The passive map may continue.”

“You think she expected this.”

“I think she wrote a route that lets us find the cost before Crown argues over whether we may look.”

“Trusting her now?”

“No.”

Nia moved the reader to the bell’s forward lamination. Trust did not determine whether a sound method deserved completion. A hostile source could still supply useful procedure if every step remained visible.

They built the map in narrow bands. Each pass took ninety seconds. C-9’s mass made the gantry trim after every rail movement, and Osei waited for the trim to settle before capturing data. Nia refused automated stitching. The software would smooth boundaries between passes and might merge distinct marks. Osei laid each strip beside the next with raw overlap preserved.

The first mark appeared in band four.

A second emerged eleven centimeters deeper, smaller and sharper. Its center had released less heat, which suggested a narrower read or a later intervention after some phase depth had already been spent. Nia labeled it B rather than assigning chronology. Position alone did not establish time inside folded glass.

Band six revealed nothing. Band seven showed two crescents nearly touching.

Osei zoomed out. “Could be one event with a broken field.”

“Compare tails.”

The first tail ran spinward. The second angled toward the bell’s central axis. A single instrument could shift during extraction, but the cool centers also held different grain compression.

“Two,” he said.

“Provisionally.”

They repeated the band at lower contrast. Both centers persisted. Nia labeled C and D.

By the midpoint of the bell, the map contained six separate boundaries.

Osei had stopped making comments. He read positions aloud while Nia entered them into the custody record. The neutral cadence they used during corrosion surveys now sounded like an effort to keep anger from becoming measurement.

“Band eleven, center at point three-four axial,” he said. “Crescent width fourteen microns. Tail bearing local spinward plus six.”

“Mark F. Repeat from high angle.”

“Field rail has to cross the old serial plate.”

“No contact.”

“I know.”

His reply came too quickly. Nia waited until he looked at her.

“If you cannot keep the sequence ordinary, step off the gantry.”

“Osei Marr objects to being told ordinary work remains possible after six destructive reads.”

“Objection recorded. Can you move the rail without touching C-9?”

“Yes.”

“Proceed.”

He did. His hands shook once when he released the rail lock, then steadied against the air control. The repeat image confirmed F.

The seventh mark appeared beneath the old Crown serial plate.

Nia felt it before she understood why. Not through the glass or the instrument. Recognition tightened her breathing into a sequence her body had used for years: four in, hold for two, act on release.

Mark G carried a double-entry tail. The extraction field had begun, paused for less than a second, then resumed at lower amplitude. A tiny gap separated the two energy fronts. Most operators avoided such pauses because they left an uneven read. Nia used one whenever calibration resistance rose above limit. The gap gave her time to test whether the tool or material caused the resistance.

Osei looked from the mark to her face.

“No,” Nia said.

He had not spoken.

She pulled her hands away from the reader controls. “Seal this band.”

Osei activated the custody lamp. Amber light ran around the image border.

“Do not annotate operator identity,” she said.

“What identity?”

“Mine.”

The word entered the record before she could reduce it to a hypothesis.

Osei’s expression emptied. “You remember reading C-9?”

“I remember calibrating a Crown route model when this bell arrived at Callis.”

“Seven years ago.”

“Yes.”

“You told me the acquisition record had no interior map.”

“It did not. Crown brought a temporary field rig and described the work as transfer certification. I was assigned to verify that an inert navigation layer would not couple into the mass ring.”

“Did you see a vocal body?”

“No.”

“Hear one?”

“No.”

“Did the rig draw phase state?”

“I saw a load increase. Their engineer called it normalization.”

Osei turned back to mark G. “That gap could belong to another resonantist.”

“It could.”

“Do other people use your pause?”

“Not with that recovery slope.”

Nia enlarged the tail. After the pause, amplitude returned in three short steps rather than one continuous rise. She had written that ladder into Callis calibration practice because older auric glass could fracture under a full return. No Crown manual used it at the time of transfer. She had taught the sequence to two archive technicians since. Neither had worked on C-9.

Recognition did not satisfy her own evidence standard. Nia opened the calibration archive and requested every surviving field trace bearing her operator seal from the month C-9 entered Callis. Four records appeared. One concerned a freight recorder with new glass and offered no useful comparison. The others came from old tuned masses whose outer layers had required resistance pauses.

“Control images,” she said.

Osei understood. He aligned the three historical tails beside mark G without normalization. Their amplitudes differed, but each carried the same interruption: a four-count approach, a pause just below threshold, then three recovery steps spaced according to Nia’s breathing rather than the instrument clock.

“Could the rig have copied your calibration profile automatically?” he asked.

“Only after I supplied it.”

“Meaning you could have calibrated a safe reference and left before the read.”

“I stayed through completion.”

“How do you know?”

Nia enlarged the last recovery step. Its slope bent downward before reaching target. “I reduced pressure by hand because the rig overshot. An automatic profile would have completed the step at constant rise.”

Osei searched the archived controls for the same correction. The oldest trace showed it twice. Nia remembered that job: Callis had acquired a cracked bearing mass from a hospital spindle, and she had spent six hours keeping the outer layer from splitting. The correction belonged to her hand as distinctly as the cadence belonged to her breath.

They ran a blind comparison. Osei hid the source labels, rotated every trace into the same local grain bearing, and asked the archive matcher for operator similarity. The software assigned mark G to Nia’s controls with high confidence. It assigned the other heat blooms no known Callis operator.

“Put the confidence measure in a separate annex,” Nia said. “The raw geometry remains primary.”

“You still think the matcher might be wrong.”

“I think a probability should not replace the thing it measured.”

Osei restored the labels. Mark G sat between two ordinary calibration jobs completed within eleven days of the transfer. Nia’s operator seal connected all three. Seven years had passed, but the gap in her memory no longer concerned whether she touched the field. It concerned why she had never asked what answered from inside it.

The reader measured old heat, but her hands supplied memory around it. A temporary rig. Bright work lamps pointed away from the operator. Crown technicians controlling every semantic channel. Nia behind the bell rather than a body, hearing a voice only as filtered test tones through her headset. She had thought the tones came from an adaptive route model answering calibration.

Four in. Hold two. Resume in steps.

Iven had recognized the cadence while looking at her hand above the vocal-body control.

Osei said, “Did you authorize extraction?”

“I authorized a calibration pulse.”

“That did this.”

“Yes.”

“Then they used your authorization for a read.”

Nia looked at the cool center of mark G. “Or I ignored evidence that calibration had become one.”

“What evidence?”

“The load exceeded the transfer sheet by eleven percent. I stopped for the gap. Crown’s engineer produced an amended tolerance before I resumed.”

“Do you have the amendment?”

“It should be in the intake packet.”

“We saw three missing attachments.”

She remembered the attachment stubs from yesterday. One carried a disposal office. One named the dead Callis clerk. The third held only the Eiren retention code. No calibration amendment appeared in the public acquisition file.

Osei folded his arms. “They removed your reason for continuing.”

“That does not remove the fact that I continued.”

“You did not know a person occupied the layer.”

“I did not ask what the test tones represented.”

“Because they told you inert route model.”

“Procedure does not become care merely because someone completes its form.”

The sentence left her mouth with more force than she intended. Nia stopped. She had believed the opposite for most of her career. If the work stayed within signed limits, responsibility remained distributed among competent roles. Mark G condensed that belief into fourteen microns of damaged glass.

A chime came from the annex monitor. Crown notice requested interim images. The automated timer had reached its first disclosure point.

Osei moved to deny transmission.

“Do not,” Nia said.

“They will bury this.”

“If we block the required notice, they will challenge the whole map.”

“If we send raw data, they can prepare an explanation before Cal sees it.”

“The notice feed receives processed images only after Court review.”

“Unless their seal has a duplicate route.”

Nia opened the data path. Ada’s seal linked the reader to Cal’s intake vault, then branched toward Crown. The branch remained closed pending judicial acknowledgment. A second route sat beneath it, unlabeled and inactive. Callis’s system identified it as a vendor diagnostic inherited with the reader.

The no-contact instrument had come from Crown stock sixteen years earlier.

“Disconnecting the vendor route alters custody,” Osei said.

“Leaving it permits access outside the annex.”

“Can they activate it remotely?”

“I do not know.”

Nia photographed the route table with the physical timing lamp. Then she closed the diagnostic path using a reversible local shutter, not a deletion. The action entered both the archive log and Ada’s annex record.

“Crown will call that interference,” Osei said.

“They may.”

The disclosure chime repeated.

“We finish the passive map,” Nia said. “Then Cal receives a copy through a channel Crown does not administer.”

Osei looked at her. “Outside their seal.”

“Inside Court custody.”

“That is not what the annex says.”

“The annex says Crown receives notice. It does not give Crown exclusive custody of evidence that its own diagnostic route could reach.”

“You are reading around it.”

“Yes.”

Nia returned her hands to the reader. Mark G remained sealed. Whatever consequences followed, they needed the count before Crown could convert uncertainty into permission.

The eighth boundary appeared near the bell’s central mass channel. Its crescent had partly collapsed into an earlier stress line, but two view angles separated the extraction tail from manufacturing damage. Mark H.

They found the final center close to the inner preservation layer. It carried the narrowest tail and the greatest local disorder, as though the operator had pushed through a region already weakened by every previous read.

Osei entered the label and did not speak the total.

Nia counted the sealed regions from the outer lamination inward: A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, I. She checked each center against the alternate angle. None depended on image stitching. None overlapped yesterday’s calibration path.

“Nine,” she said.

Osei brought up the whole bell. The thermal map showed nine activations, each preserved as a separate heat bloom in C-9’s auric glass. Mark G carried Nia’s seven-year-old calibration ladder.

The number answered Iven’s last question from the body room.

Nia could not answer him now without calling him again.

They sealed the full map under the Court lamp. Osei generated a processed copy for the annex and left it waiting for Cal’s acknowledgment. Nia took the raw sensor sequence, its instrument settings, the vendor-route photograph, and the sealed image of mark G. She wrote them into the timing lamp’s optical thread.

“That lamp leaves the ring, Crown will challenge chain,” Osei said.

“It will not leave custody.”

“Where does it go?”

“Cal’s hand.”

“Crown officers control the quarantine door.”

“They control mass movement. Not a Court witness carrying a physical seal.”

Osei unhooked his own custody lamp. “Then take two.”

“One copy creates a point of failure.”

“Two creates corroboration.”

“Two creates another person they can discipline.”

“I am already on the record operating the reader.”

Nia met his gaze. Keeping him outside the choice would protect neither of them. It would only preserve her authority by pretending he remained a subordinate after discovering the same fact.

“Build your copy independently,” she said. “Use raw output, not mine. We compare checksums under Cal’s lamp.”

Osei began the transfer.

Before leaving, Nia reopened the acquisition record. Seven years earlier, her calibration sign-off appeared beneath Crown’s inert declaration. The signature field showed no warning, no semantic access, and no human-subject designation. Her name still authorized the completed transfer. She attached the record to mark G without adding an explanation.

At the pressure door, the Crown officer asked whether the inspection had found a stability hazard.

“Yes,” Nia said.

“Does C-9 threaten the Meridian schedule?”

“The threat concerns destructive access.”

“By whom?”

“Open the door.”

He checked her Court seal and saw Ada’s method annex listed beneath it. The joint lock released.

Cal met them in the axial connector rather than making them carry the evidence through public court corridors. He wore no robe, only a dark work coat with the preservation-order seal clipped at the throat. Nia placed her timing lamp in his palm. Osei placed his beside it.

“What changed?” Cal asked.

“We found prior destructive reads.”

“How many?”

“Nine.”

His fingers tightened around the lamps. “Can you establish source?”

“One heat bloom carries my calibration signature from the transfer seven years ago. I did not know the layer held a person. I authorized the pulse that opened it.”

Cal did not tell her the ignorance excused her. He aligned both optical threads with his seal and began the custody comparison.

Behind Nia, the quarantine door closed between the archive ring and Crown’s officers. Ahead, Cal’s timing lamp accepted both copies and wrote the same nine scars into the Court record.

Chapter 6 — Four Point Six Returns

Iven woke with a numbered strip pressed around his wrist and a route missing from the room.

The ceiling held six acoustic baffles in two offset rows. A saline feed entered his left forearm. Linen restraints crossed his thighs without tightening, their slack measured to let him turn but not rise. He could place every object by distance from his eyes, yet the door beyond his feet had no relation to any corridor he knew. The absence made his stomach contract before memory supplied the name Callis.

Nia Daro sat beside the monitoring rail with both hands visible. She had changed clothes since his last waking. Dark archive fabric covered her wrists, and a custody lamp glowed green against her collarbone. A broad window at her back showed smoked glass rather than a view.

“Do you know where you are?” she asked.

“Inside a room designed by someone who mistrusts corners.”

Her gaze went to the breath counter. “Do you know the station?”

“Callis.” His mouth tasted of glucose and warm metal. “You asked about place before identity last time.”

Nia did not confirm that there had been a last time. “Tell me your name.”

“Iven Ko.”

“What work did you perform?”

“Route cartography for Eiren Reach.”

The name opened a set of distances. He saw Eiren’s terminator from no human vantage, as a table of transfer loads between dayward mirrors and inhabited caverns. Lines joined the thermal grid to freight locks, shelter wells, water loops. Nothing in the geometry told him how those places had sounded. He knew where the south tram crossed the fourth heat trunk. He could not recall the color of the car.

Nia marked three fields on a slate. She did not praise correct answers. That restraint gave him more comfort than kindness would have.

“How long has it been since Eiren failed?” he asked.

“Forty-seven years.”

He took one breath, then another because the body required sequence even when memory did not. “And since you first called me?”

“We are still establishing that record.”

“That means more than once.”

Her thumb stopped above the slate. A man stood behind the room’s clear partition, older than Nia, with a court seal fixed to his gray jacket. Iven remembered him as Cal Orsen, though the memory offered no first meeting. Cal held a paper file instead of a screen. Another man, Osei Marr, watched the phase instruments beside the door.

Iven raised his wrist until the numbered strip came into view. “What does this measure?”

“Body chemistry,” Nia said.

“Not the number on it.”

The strip read R-4.6 / PRESERVATION LOAD. Ink had transferred faintly to his skin.

Cal touched the partition control. “It identifies the public reserve estimate attached to your archive.”

“Four point six hours?”

“Returns,” Nia said. “A return estimates enough coherence for one controlled activation under ordinary stress.”

The room offered too many ways to understand that sentence. Iven chose the narrowest. “How much of one am I using now?”

“We cannot measure consumption in real time with confidence.”

“That did not answer me.”

“No,” she said.

Cal entered through the pressure door. Its seals folded inward with almost no sound. He stopped outside the reach of Iven’s unrestrained arm and set the paper file on a wall shelf.

“The archive lists 4.6 returns before this activation,” Cal said. “The estimate may be wrong. We found evidence of prior access yesterday.”

“How many accesses?”

Nia looked at Cal. The exchange between them lasted less than a second, but Iven recognized a route choice in it. Each wanted the other to accept responsibility for the next sentence.

“Nine heat blooms,” she said. “They may correspond to nine destructive reads.”

His right hand closed around the sheet. The fabric bunched between his fingers. He waited for shock and found calculation instead.

“Nine plus the calls I remember?”

“We do not yet know which calls you remember,” Cal said.

“How many do you know about?”

“This public process includes your first controlled activation two days ago and the present memory check.”

Iven turned his head toward the smoked window. His neck tired halfway through the motion. “Then 4.6 returns cannot tell you how many times I can answer.”

“It cannot,” Nia said.

“Why put it on my body?”

“The clinical system copied the archive estimate onto the strip.”

“A machine performed the discourtesy.”

Osei looked down, concealing either pain or amusement. Nia did neither.

“I should have removed it before waking you,” she said.

“Would that have changed the estimate?”

“No.”

“Leave it, then.”

The breath counter climbed though he lay still. A soft tone sounded from the saline pump. Nia checked the line, reduced the glucose rate, and waited until the tone stopped. Her hands moved without wasted correction. He knew those hands. The certainty arrived without a scene attached to it.

“You have done this before,” he said.

“I have calibrated Crown phase equipment.”

“Not equipment. Me.”

“I found my calibration signature on one of the heat blooms.” Her voice stayed level. “Seven years ago, I believed the material contained an inert route model. I do not remember an embodied person.”

Iven searched for her face in his prior awakenings and found a cadence instead: two low pulses, one held pause, a question delivered by instrument. Geometry had preserved her approach while discarding the room around it.

“You spoke through a probe,” he said.

“I may have.”

“You waited too long after the second pulse.”

Nia’s breathing changed. Only one count, shallow enough that the monitor ignored it.

Cal opened the paper file. “We can stop.”

“Can you?” Iven asked.

“Legally and clinically.”

“That makes two meanings. I asked for one.”

Cal accepted the correction with a slight inclination of his head. “You may stop this check. The preservation order prevents broader access until a standing hearing.”

“May I choose to continue?”

“Within Nia’s safety limits.”

The answer supplied a boundary without pretending to supply freedom. Iven loosened his grip on the sheet.

“Continue,” he said. “But remove the strip.”

Nia cut the paper band with blunt archive shears. The severed number curled in her palm. She sealed it in a custody sleeve rather than throwing it away.

They began with route tables because those cost him less. Osei displayed a blank Eiren grid on the smoked window. Iven placed civic shelters, thermal trunks, choir-relay stations, and freight approaches by calling coordinates. Each answer drew a pale line. The map became familiar before it became inhabited.

At grid sector five he paused.

A maintenance branch should have crossed the route from dayward collector two to the central distribution well. He knew its pressure loss, capacity, and inspection interval. A person belonged there. His memory held a shoulder under gray work cloth, a hand with two fingertips scarred white, boots wedged against a pipe brace.

Before he supplied the name, Osei changed the map scale. The duty paths contracted into thin amber threads, and a cluster of old closure marks appeared around the east transfer loop. Iven read them faster than the key could render. Pressure door E-12 had failed its morning check. A coolant cart blocked the south gallery. Someone on grid watch had opened a service crawl rather than send workers through a hot junction.

“Do those marks come from the public packet?” Iven asked.

“Three do,” Osei said. “The crawl authorization does not.”

“Then I gave it to you before.”

“No one in this room entered it.”

Nia moved the custody lamp nearer the display. “Can you identify who authorized the crawl?”

The geometry tightened around a pair of routes. One ended at an inspection platform above the east trunk. Another crossed behind the thermal manifold and reached the same place by six more turns. He remembered irritation at those six turns. He remembered a laugh that came after his irritation, low and brief, followed by two scarred fingertips tracing the safer route across his wrist.

A face tried to gather around the gesture. Dark hair resolved first, then the line of a cheek. The eyes would not hold. When he pressed for them, the map blurred and the breath counter quickened.

“I know the route she chose,” he said. “I know why she refused mine. The junction had started shedding insulation into the air flow.”

“What did she say?” Cal asked.

Iven listened to an empty place shaped like speech. “That did not survive.”

Osei restored the wider scale without being told. The amber routes loosened their grip on the absent face, but the safer path remained clear from end to end. Memory had preserved the decision and spent the person who made it.

“Lio Senn,” he said.

Osei’s stylus stopped.

Nia lowered the next test card. “Who was she?”

“My wife.” The fact carried none of the uncertainty surrounding the room. “Thermal-grid engineer. Senior watch on the east transfer loop.”

“What do you remember about her?” Cal asked.

The question sounded ordinary. Its breadth made it dangerous.

Iven closed his eyes. He could route himself from their apartment to her duty station by three paths. Main stair to tram level, tram south, maintenance rise at trunk four. During pressure closures, west gallery to the service lift. If both failed, a handrail route crossed the collector maintenance tubes in low gravity. He could draw each turn.

He could not see the apartment door.

“She stayed at the grid,” he said.

“When?” Nia asked.

“During the failure.”

“How do you know?”

He tried to place the conversation. Lio stood beside a heat diagram. Then she stood in a corridor with a tool case against her knee. Then her voice came through a civic channel stripped of place. The scenes refused order. Any could have preceded the others.

“She told me to take the bell route.”

“Did she tell you before or after the thermal collapse began?”

His breath caught on the saline scent. The body interpreted an absent sequence as danger, though no muscle could repair it.

“I don’t know.”

Nia leaned toward the monitor. “We can move to recognition without chronology.”

“No. Ask again.”

“Stress is increasing consumption.”

“You said you cannot measure it in real time.”

“We can measure the conditions that accelerate loss.”

“Then tell me the conditions.”

Nia named them without softening: broad associative recall, fear, pain, conflicting sensory prompts, prolonged waking, and forced reconstruction of damaged sequence. She gave him numbers only where the instruments supported them. His pulse had risen by twenty-eight beats. Oxygen demand had doubled from rest. The phase coupling between C-9 and the vocal body showed widening noise around autobiographical prompts.

“Does the noise become damage?” he asked.

“It can.”

“Has it now?”

“I cannot tell until the return closes.”

There it was again, information arriving only after its subject could no longer use it.

Iven opened his eyes. “Ask about the convoy.”

Cal’s expression tightened. “That falls outside the approved memory sequence.”

“The approved sequence seems designed to discover whether I can choose. I am choosing a question.”

No one answered immediately. The room’s saline pump clicked once, moving a precise amount of warmth into his arm.

Cal went to the partition and spoke with someone outside by private channel. Iven watched his posture rather than trying to read his lips. Cal listened longer than he argued. When he came back, he did not reopen the file.

“One limited prompt,” he said. “No sensory simulation. You may refuse at any point.”

“Show me the route table from the final rescue window.”

Osei drew a chair to the smoked glass. “The public packet contains several tables. Which reference point?”

“Eiren civic north. Mass bearings relative to collector one. Remove outcomes.”

Osei built the display in layers. A terminator line crossed the moon’s curve. Eiren’s heat system appeared as a compact lattice below it. Farther out, three tuned masses marked a triangular schedule around the Meridian Mouth. A convoy approach entered from spinward. The reconstruction stopped before any line bent.

Iven’s hands began to shake.

He knew the triangle. Its long edge had carried Crown timing reserves. Shifting one point would alter the other two. Detuning all three would open a route toward Eiren, but the Mouth would lose its held shape. He saw the requirement as space, not arithmetic: three bells displaced for a span whose length touched the edge of memory.

A different image broke through. White authority lights crossing a cockpit. No, not a cockpit. A bell bay. The surface under his hands sloped toward a route board. Someone breathed close to his right shoulder.

“Lio?” he said.

Nia answered from the left. “You are on Callis.”

He lifted one finger toward the display. “The rescue line went there.”

Osei did not draw it. “Can you describe the line without adding it?”

“From the convoy approach through the near mass, then across the lower shear boundary. It needed all three.”

“What happened?” Cal asked.

Lights moved inside the recollection. Three nested arcs glowed white on a bulkhead, Crown’s mark enlarged by haze. Beyond them, rescue lamps pivoted across a route display. The turn began in clean increments. Not drift. Not impact. Commanded motion carried each vessel away from Eiren.

He heard Lio speaking, but the words came from two directions. In one sequence she told him to take the bell and preserve the route. In another she asked whether Crown had accepted the detuning. He could not decide which came first. Her last conversation had lost its hinges.

“The Crown lights were turning away,” he said. “I saw the convoy bearing change.”

Nia’s face held stillness now, but her fingers had closed around the sleeve containing the cut wrist strip.

“From Eiren?” Cal asked.

“Yes.”

“Toward what?”

“The Mouth.”

Osei said, “Did you observe an order?”

Iven reached for the answer and found three possible rooms occupying one place. A dispatch bay. The grid. Choir bell C-9 while its recording layer sealed. Each contained Lio’s voice at a different distance.

“I observed the turn.”

“Do you remember who ordered it?”

“No.”

“Any dispatcher identifier?”

A field of symbols surfaced, then folded before he could read it. Pain struck behind his left eye. His jaw clenched hard enough to make the body alarm sound.

Nia cleared the display at once. The Eiren grid vanished. She placed her palm against his forearm, avoiding the saline line.

“Stop,” she said.

“I can still speak.”

“That does not mean the process remains safe.”

“What did that question cost?”

She reached for the monitoring rail. “We need to close the return before we assess it.”

“What did it cost?”

“Iven.” Cal’s voice had the careful weight of an order he hoped not to issue. “Nia cannot provide a number she does not have.”

“She can tell me what you knew before asking.”

Nia’s hand stayed on his arm. Heat moved from her skin into the vocal body. He wondered whether that warmth belonged to her intent or merely to tissue doing what tissue did.

“We knew autobiographical sequence carried greater risk than route recall,” she said. “We knew distress memories could raise demand. We did not know this prompt would produce pain at that speed.”

“You expected some pain.”

“Yes.”

“Did you tell me?”

“No.”

The admission changed the room more completely than an apology could have. Cal closed the paper file. Osei dimmed every display except the clinical monitor.

Iven looked at the severed wrist strip. 4.6 returns had entered the room as reassurance, though no one had called it that. The number pretended to count future choices while hiding the reads that had already spent them. Even its uncertainty had been organized around the institution’s need to proceed.

“I asked to continue,” he said.

“You did,” Nia replied.

“I chose with the information you gave me.”

“Yes.”

“If you leave out the cost, you are choosing the missing part for me.”

The breath counter showed a slower rhythm now. His vision narrowed at the edges. Return closure approached as fatigue rather than sleep, a retreat of available distances. The door seemed farther away. Nia’s face remained exact because he had fixed on it, but Cal and Osei had begun to lose detail.

Cal moved closer. “What do you want recorded before deactivation?”

Iven considered the question. Eiren pulled at him through incomplete routes. Lio remained at the thermal grid without a reliable before or after. Crown lights continued turning away whenever he touched the final map. He still wanted the choice named. Wanting truth did not cancel his claim to know what truth would consume.

“I prefer to continue,” he said, “only if Nia tells me what each process can cost before I agree to it. Not afterward. Not inside a clinical note I cannot read while asleep.”

Cal repeated the preference for the custody lamp, preserving each condition. He asked Iven to confirm it. Iven did.

Nia unfastened the loose linen across his thighs. The restraint had never tightened, but its removal let his legs settle apart by a few centimeters. She checked the saline temperature, then lowered the room lights without turning the smoked glass into an image of Eiren.

“Can you make that promise?” he asked.

“I cannot always give you a number.”

“That is not what I asked.”

Her eyes met his before the body’s vision softened again.

“No activation without explanation,” Nia said. “If the cost cannot be measured, I will explain the uncertainty. If someone limits what I may tell you, I will tell you that too.”

Cal said nothing. His silence left the promise where she had put it, between custodian and person rather than inside a court’s permission.

Iven let his eyes close. The room receded by bearings: monitoring rail to his right, Nia’s hand at his forearm, pressure door beyond his feet, choir bell C-9 somewhere through layers of Callis. Lio’s route to the thermal grid remained open in him even though the order of their last words did not.

Before breath and phase uncoupled, Nia removed the strip marked 4.6 returns from its custody sleeve and placed it face down on the rail.

Interlude I — Return Ledger

SOURCE ASSET: C9-ER-204
SUBJECT PATTERN: KO, IVEN / ROUTE CARTOGRAPHER
ACCESS BASIS: Infrastructure-loss analysis
PUBLIC DISCLOSURE: None authorized
OPERATIVE OBJECTIVE: Extract a control statement separating convoy motion from deliberate route choice.
CAUTION: Each read alters the sole surviving phase state. Duplication unsupported.

RETURN 1

Offset: Eiren loss + 11 months
Facility: Crown recovery bay, Aster Three
Authorization: Executive emergency minute; annex withheld from medical file
Embodiment: Auditory cortex, speech tract, limited proprioception; 8 minutes 14 seconds

Subject identified Eiren civic north from an unmarked route plane. He supplied the three-mass rescue geometry without prompting and described convoy lamps changing bearing. Asked whether navigation loss could explain the turn, he answered: “A lost route does not keep formation.”

Autobiographical access remained broad. Subject named spouse Lio Senn, her thermal-grid duty, and their last shared meal. Sequence around the final dispatch could not be stabilized before cardiac load crossed limit.

Disposition: No releasable statement. Retain layer under corridor-risk privilege.

RETURN 2

Offset: Eiren loss + 3 years
Facility: Crown medical annex, Nacre Dock
Authorization: Operations counsel concurrence; no external custodian
Embodiment: Speech and vision; 11 minutes 02 seconds

Interviewers presented two route diagrams. Diagram A showed rescue detuning. Diagram B showed Mouth stabilization. Subject selected A as the route he had expected and B as the motion he observed. He rejected the suggested phrase “uncommanded correction.”

Recognition check produced accurate names for four dispatchers and eleven route stations. Subject misordered two calls with Lio but corrected himself when shown grid timestamps.

Extraction note: Counsel requested a shorter answer suitable for a public incident supplement. Subject refused the prepared wording.

RETURN 3

Offset: Eiren loss + 7 years
Facility: Meridian scheduling laboratory
Authorization: Technical-risk committee, closed circulation
Embodiment: Minimal vocal assembly; 6 minutes 47 seconds

No personal names were supplied in the opening prompts. Subject nevertheless asked whether Lio had cleared the east transfer loop. He located that loop on a blank grid and stated that thermal failure had begun before the convoy completed its turn.

When asked, “Did Crown intend Eiren to fail?” subject distinguished expected outcome from private motive. He would not infer an executive’s thoughts from route motion. He did state that the three bells remained synchronized after rescue detuning became possible.

Damage observation: Forty-three seconds of blank interval followed the spouse prompt. Phase noise persisted after shutdown.

RETURN 4

Offset: Eiren loss + 13 years
Facility: Aster Crown legal archive
Authorization: Chair’s sealed directive
Embodiment: Speech only; no visual feed; 9 minutes 31 seconds

The examiner withheld all dates. Subject placed the shear window within nineteen hours of the convoy bearing change by reconstructing mass recovery intervals. Asked to repeat “the convoy could not safely reach Eiren,” he answered: “You can make that sentence safe only by removing when.”

A proposed control statement attributed the loss to telemetry uncertainty. Subject requested the underlying dispatch clock. The request fell outside approved exposure and was denied.

Continuity change: Subject could no longer retrieve the name of Eiren’s chief dispatcher, although he retained the dispatcher’s station position and handoff route.

RETURN 5

Offset: Eiren loss + 21 years
Facility: Crown compliance chamber, Morcant
Authorization: Infrastructure-defense review
Embodiment: Speech, basic vision, right-hand motor map; 12 minutes 05 seconds

Reviewers showed six convoy images, five authentic and one composite. Subject identified the composite from an impossible bell shadow. He recognized Crown’s nested route arcs but did not recognize his own archived portrait.

A mirror was provided after consent language approved by Crown counsel. Subject asked whose face the body carried. Staff explained that the vocal body did not reproduce his original appearance. Subject then asked why an image labeled IVEN KO looked unfamiliar.

Medical stop: Elevated phase scatter after self-image prompt. Remaining questions deferred.

RETURN 6

Offset: Eiren loss + 29 years
Facility: Mobile bell tender Kestra
Authorization: Schedule-security waiver
Embodiment: Auditory channel and speech; 7 minutes 56 seconds

Route tones replaced visual diagrams. Subject reconstructed all three bell bearings and the Mouth-holding sequence. He placed Eiren’s rescue line correctly but joined two personal calls into one event.

The interviewer offered a neutral account: convoy crews responded to shear and lacked a viable rescue window. Subject asked for the bells’ detuning limits. Access to those values had been prohibited. After the refusal, he said: “Then you are testing my agreement, not my memory.”

Operational result: No usable concession. Future returns to exclude argumentative exchange.

RETURN 7

Offset: Eiren loss + 40 years
Facility: Crown phase laboratory, Callis service orbit
Authorization: Disposal calibration order coded as inert-route maintenance
Embodiment: Partial speech pathway; technician isolated from semantic output; 4 minutes 18 seconds

External calibration staff received no notice of a human phase pattern. Crown operators routed prompts beneath the service layer while the contracted resonantist supplied timing pulses. Subject responded to the resonantist’s cadence and attempted to ask how many times access had occurred. Operators closed the speech path before the question reached the room.

Memory sample preserved route geometry, Lio Senn’s duty location, and the convoy turn. Subject did not retrieve the order of his final two conversations.

Chain note: Third-party signature remains embedded in the heat record. Removal would require another destructive read.

RETURN 8

Timing: Six days after the seventh read
Facility: Same phase laboratory
Authorization: Continuation under prior disposal order
Embodiment: Constrained answer channel; 5 minutes 44 seconds

Operators limited output to yes, no, unknown, or repeat. The subject defeated the constraint by withholding breath after the question, “Could the convoy have rescued Eiren?” Clinical staff restored an open speech path to prevent distress.

He then said: “Possible for whom?” Follow-up pressure produced no answer. Route recognition remained intact. Facial recall for Lio failed twice, while her grid paths remained exact.

Risk decision: Executive review rejected further language conditioning. Asset scheduled for transfer beyond routine Crown access.

RETURN 9

Offset: Eiren loss + 40 years, 19 days
Facility: Crown transfer cradle, Callis approach
Authorization: Final integrity check before salvage conveyance
Embodiment: Auditory verification only; 3 minutes 09 seconds

Subject woke before the approved identity prompt and said: “You already tried me.” No interviewer acknowledged the statement. He named Callis after hearing its ring-balance tones, then asked whether the receiving archive knew what the bell contained.

The final control statement presented for confirmation read: “Convoy redirection followed unavoidable navigation instability; no recoverable record establishes deliberate choice.” Subject answered, “Your sentence knows more about recoverability than I do.”

Shutdown began when phase scatter exceeded the transfer limit. The subject attempted one further word. Audio resolution did not support transcription.

Transfer action: C9-ER-204 conveyed to Callis as mixed tuned mass, classified INERT. Interior access prohibited. Source attachments replaced with checksum stubs. Crown retained excursion telemetry through the emergency infrastructure channel.

LEDGER STATUS: Nine destructive reads recorded. No additional return authorized without chair-level directive. Public incident file unchanged.

SEAL AUDIT: RETURN 1 established intact autobiographical access, full route competence, and an unwillingness to adopt language supplied by counsel. RETURN 9 preserved route competence but showed advanced loss of faces, names, and personal sequence. Across the interval, every successful extraction narrowed the subject available for the next attempt. No entry documents informed agreement to a later read. Clinical stops limited immediate injury; they did not restore phase state already measured.

The sealed series contains no confirmed control statement from Iven Ko. Prepared language appears in five interviewer notes and once in the final verification prompt. In each instance, the subject corrected, refused, qualified, or exposed the premise. Any incident summary describing the record as inconclusive must disclose that Crown constrained the questions, withheld dispatch values, and repeatedly altered the only witness while seeking agreement.

Chapter 7 — Standing Hearing

Cal called the hearing while a clinical timer removed seconds from the witness.

The timer occupied no larger space than his thumb on the court rail. Its amber column descended whenever Iven’s vocal body remained awake and paused only after phase monitors confirmed full rest. Nia had refused to translate the column into fractions of a return. She had written beneath the display: CONSUMPTION VARIABLE. DURATION DOES NOT EQUAL COST.

Ada Neme read the warning twice before taking her place at Crown’s table.

The axial chamber had been built for freight appeals, not questions about whether evidence could decline to become evidence. Paper-thin acoustic baffles hung between the court rail and the public gallery. Callis rotation reached this close to the axis only as a slow drift in loose sleeves. Physical seals held the three case files to Cal’s desk: salvage ownership, emergency preservation, and the petition for standing in continuance.

Iven appeared behind a clear clinical partition to Cal’s right. The vocal body wore a gray linen shirt and no wrist strip. Nia sat within arm’s reach of the saline control. Osei attended from the archive table with bell C-9’s passive trace disconnected from every network except the court recorder.

Cal touched the timing lamp. “Continuation matter C-9-ER-204. Petition to recognize Iven Ko as a person for the limited duration of active coherence. Crown’s compulsory-analysis request remains stayed until disposition.”

Ada rose. “Crown objects to calling the pattern by a personal name before the test concludes.”

“The name forms part of the petition and the recovered registry.”

“It also forms part of the source packet. Repetition does not prove preference.”

“Neither does refusing the name prove property,” Cal said. “You may preserve your objection without making the court speak in inventory codes.”

Ada sat. She had arranged her files by operational consequence rather than legal issue. The top folder carried a red corridor-risk edge. Cal knew the habit from twelve years of Crown appeals. She would not deny a human cost if she could place another human cost beside it.

He turned toward the partition. “Iven Ko, do you understand why we are here?”

“I understand three descriptions of it,” Iven said. “Nia says you are deciding whether I may choose what happens to me. Crown says you are measuring whether my answers originate outside a record. Your clerk said this could take three days.”

A few people in the gallery shifted. Cal kept his attention on Iven. “Which description do you accept?”

“The one that ends before my body does.”

No laughter followed. The line carried too much clinical fact.

Cal stated the test in terms a person could contest. The Continuance Articles treated a transmitted remnant as property until it formed a preference unrecoverable from its source conditions. Recognition did not require continuous memory, an original body, or agreement with the court. It required evidence that the present mind could choose beyond the recorded purpose that created it.

“What did the signal that carried you ask for?” Cal said.

“Rescue first. Record the convoy choice if rescue failed. Preserve Eiren’s registry.”

“Do you still want those things?”

“Yes.”

“Then those wants cannot by themselves satisfy the Novel Preference Test.”

Iven considered that without looking toward Nia. “You need me to want something my dead self did not prepare.”

“We need evidence of a present preference not recoverable from the packet.”

“Your version uses more nouns.”

“Courts ration verbs when frightened.”

That drew a brief breath from Iven that might have become a laugh in a stronger body. Cal regretted the exchange at once. Familiarity could look like advocacy, and advocacy would weaken any protection he granted.

Ada stood again. “Crown requests a baseline examination before any preference demonstration. We must know whether the pattern can distinguish an external choice from a supplied one.”

Nia’s hand moved to the clinical control. “Define examination.”

“Counsel will address the court,” Cal said.

Ada did. “Three paired prompts drawn from the sealed distress packet. One altered prompt. No broad recall. No visual material until the court confirms recognition stability.”

Nia looked toward Iven. “Likely cost remains low, but not zero. Contradictory prompts may increase stress. We can stop after any question.”

“You said that before I agreed,” Iven replied.

“Yes.”

Cal recorded both the warning and the response. Nia’s promise from the memory check had entered practice before law required it.

Ada began with route work. She offered two statements about choir mass. One matched Iven’s deposition: three bells could create an Eiren rescue line if detuned together. The other changed the number to two. Iven rejected the alteration and explained that two masses could shift the shear boundary but could not hold a path through it.

Her second pair concerned Lio Senn’s duty. The packet placed her at the east transfer loop. Ada moved her to collector control. Iven corrected the location, then added that Lio could authorize access to collector control without working there.

“Does the source record contain that distinction?” Cal asked Osei.

“The accessible layer contains her duty code and two route permissions,” Osei said. “It does not contain a prose explanation.”

“Route permissions may encode the inference,” Ada said.

Cal marked it as competence, not novelty.

For the third pair, Ada read two accounts of the convoy’s final movement. One used the public phrase unavoidable navigation instability. The other stated that the convoy maintained formation while changing bearing toward the Meridian Mouth.

Iven’s pulse rose on the monitor. Nia lifted one finger, warning without interruption.

“The second account matches what I remember,” Iven said.

“Matches memory or matches the deposition?” Ada asked.

“I cannot separate those for you.”

“Then the answer proves no present witness.”

“It proves I know the limit of my answer.”

Ada faced Cal. “Self-description also appears throughout the packet. The remnant repeatedly distinguishes observation from motive. That discipline belonged to Ko while alive.”

Cal wrote the point because it deserved preservation. A witness could not prove novelty merely by exhibiting character. Yet a court that demanded discontinuity would recognize personhood only when damage made someone unlike themselves.

He recessed before the clinical timer reached Nia’s first limit.

During rest, the vocal body did not sleep in the ordinary sense. Nia closed the sensory channels and let phase coupling fall below the threshold for coherent response. Cal remained in the chamber while Crown clerks sent him eleven pages arguing that the Compulsory Evidence Clause authorized deeper comparison if the voluntary test failed.

Osei stayed at the archive table. He ran a cloth along the passive recorder’s edge though no dust had reached it.

“What would satisfy you?” he asked.

“I do not discuss a pending test with a witness technician.”

“I asked what would satisfy the law.”

“The law says a novel preference.”

“That sounds like you will know it when you hear it.”

Cal looked at the three sealed files. “That troubles me too.”

The Articles offered examples: a signal person choosing one advocate over another, refusing a proposed body form absent from the source, requesting destruction for reasons formed after transmission. Each example presumed the institution could present neutral options. After nine unauthorized reads, no option involving the archive felt neutral.

“Could you ask him what he wants?” Osei said.

“His recorded self wanted several things.”

“Ask about this room.”

Cal did not answer. The suggestion had merit, which made accepting it from a party dangerous. He wrote it in the margin as an objection to his own caution.

On Day 6, Ada called a Crown phase analyst by remote link. The analyst had mapped every declarative preference in the accessible Eiren packet: rescue, preservation, testimony, registry recovery, contact with Lio, and avoidance of nonessential medical intervention. A choice touching any of those categories could have grown from the original signal.

“So the more complete a person’s record, the harder personhood becomes?” Cal asked.

“The test measures novelty, not completeness.”

“A complete source predicts more choices.”

“Yes.”

“And destructive testing enlarges Crown’s knowledge of what the source predicts.”

Ada rose before the analyst could answer. “The prior reads remain under sealed dispute. They cannot expand the packet for this test.”

“They expanded Crown’s ability to challenge it.”

“Not mine. I have not reviewed semantic output from those activations.”

Cal believed her. Ada’s restraint could protect the process while preserving Crown’s institutional ignorance as an advantage. Both facts could coexist.

Nia reported that Iven could tolerate one short visual-choice sequence. She described the cost as uncertain and the stress boundary as strict. Iven agreed after asking which images, who had selected them, and whether refusal would count against him. Cal ordered the answers read into the record.

Ada proposed four views derived from the source: Eiren’s terminator line, the east transfer loop, choir bell C-9’s original bay, and a route map without outcomes. Iven could choose one to hold on the room window during the remaining examination. Crown argued that selecting among emotionally weighted memories might demonstrate coherent preference even if it did not alone establish novelty.

The first image appeared as a low-resolution reconstruction of Eiren from orbital survey. No ruins, no bodies, only the hard division between collector light and the inhabited dark. Iven watched without moving.

Next came the thermal grid. The simulation used maintenance plans to rebuild its handrails, pressure doors, and pipe braces. Nia had prohibited human figures. Empty duty stations extended into the distance.

The third view placed a modeled C-9 in its launch cradle. Crown’s reconstruction rendered the auric glass too bright. Osei entered an objection to the color calibration.

A route plane filled the final screen. Three mass marks held near the Mouth. Eiren waited beyond the shear boundary.

Ada stood beside her table. “You may select any view. You may request a neutral gray field. You may also decline to choose.”

Iven looked from the screen to the chamber’s clear partition. “Are those my only options?”

“For this sequence,” Ada said.

“Why?”

“They avoid introducing new visual material that might contaminate later memory comparisons.”

“Everything you offer comes from Eiren or the bell.”

“Those sources let us evaluate the choice.”

“Because you already know why I might choose them.”

Ada did not evade. “Yes.”

Iven turned toward Cal. “Then give me a window showing Callis.”

The public gallery remained quiet enough for Cal to hear the timing lamp advance.

Ada’s expression changed by less than a blink. “There is no exterior window in this chamber.”

“I can see a clear wall, three acoustic baffles, and twenty-one people who keep looking at the timer. That is Callis, but Nia says the partition must stay clear. Show me somewhere on the station that has nothing to do with Eiren.”

Cal asked, “What would you like to see?”

“I don’t know what exists here.”

“Crown objects,” Ada said. “An open request can be shaped after the fact to appear novel.”

“Then let him narrow it,” Cal replied.

Iven watched a court attendant secure a stack of paper under a magnetic strip. “Show me where people eat.”

Nia checked the monitor. “No clinical objection to a live, noninteractive feed.”

Ada opened the source index on her desk. “Crown needs ten minutes to verify that no Callis dining space appears in the packet.”

“Take five,” Cal said. “The witness pays for the other five.”

The remark did what he intended. Ada assigned two clerks, and Osei searched the court copy in parallel. They found route references to Callis in old Crown schedules but no image, description, or preference concerning the station. Iven had identified Callis from ring-balance tones during the ninth sealed read, yet no record showed interest in its ordinary life.

A court attendant placed a portable lens in the west concourse canteen. The feed opened on a long room under low artificial gravity. Cups sat in recessed trays. Two maintenance workers argued over a ration printer while a child pushed a bead of spilled water along the table with one finger. At the far wall, a service window closed for pressure equalization and reopened after its lamp turned green.

Iven leaned forward against the linen support.

“Why there?” Ada asked.

“The child is trying to make the water cross a seam.”

“That does not answer why you requested the canteen.”

“I asked for a place where people eat because no one died there for me.” His eyes stayed on the feed. “I want to look at something I was not built to remember.”

Ada approached the clinical partition but stopped at its marked boundary. “Your source asked rescuers to preserve life. Interest in living people may follow directly from that purpose.”

“Interest, perhaps,” Cal said. “Not this object, this place, or this reason.”

“The test cannot rest on poetic phrasing.”

“It will rest on evidence. The phrasing merely made you notice it.”

Ada returned to her table. “Crown requests examination of the choice. Ask whether he would retain the Callis view if offered Lio Senn’s image.”

Nia said, “No.”

The refusal crossed the chamber before Cal could rule. Nia’s hand covered the image control.

Ada faced her. “You do not hold standing in this matter.”

“I hold clinical authority over prompts during this return. A spouse comparison creates known stress.”

“Iven may choose to accept that cost.”

“After explanation,” Iven said. “I do not.”

Ada went back to her files and withdrew a blank court wafer. “Then Crown proposes a non-source comparison. The court will select another live Callis feed by chance. If novelty alone drives the request, any unencoded location should serve.”

Cal saw the distinction she wanted. A random view could test whether Iven preferred the canteen or merely wanted something outside his packet. It also risked turning the present mind into a series of challenges designed to eliminate every alternative explanation.

“What cost?” Iven asked.

Nia studied the monitor rather than guessing. “Low if the image contains no Eiren-linked material. Additional waking still spends coherence. I recommend no more than six minutes before rest.”

Iven nodded once. “I agree to one image.”

Cal directed the timing lamp to choose among court-approved public feeds. It selected a maintenance overlook on mass ring one. The attendant opened the view without commentary. Tuned ballast moved behind smoked shielding while two technicians crossed a gantry on toe loops. White schedule marks advanced around the ring in exact intervals.

Iven examined it for less than a minute. “Keep the canteen.”

“Why?” Ada asked.

“I understand what the ring does.”

“Understanding does not prevent preference.”

“No. The people prevent it.” He indicated the child, who had acquired the cloth at last and now twisted water into it. “The ring would let me work. The canteen lets me fail to be useful.”

Ada did not write that line down. One of her clerks did.

“Would you preserve the canteen view if no legal advantage followed?” she asked.

“I did not know it carried a legal advantage when I requested it.”

“You knew this hearing concerned choice.”

“I knew every answer might be used. That does not make every answer false.”

Cal intervened before the exchange became an endurance test. “The request preceded knowledge of its evidentiary effect. Crown may argue weight, not manufacture retrospection.”

Ada closed the wafer. “Then ask what duration he prefers.”

Iven looked to Nia first. “How long until you need to close the return?”

“Four minutes at the present load.”

“Leave the canteen until then. No more questions.”

That choice placed rest above further proof and kept an ordinary view over a technically familiar one. Cal entered both conditions in the record. Ada objected that a medically advised stop could not count as novelty. Cal sustained the objection in part; stopping followed known risk. The chosen image did not.

He watched the canteen feed. The child had succeeded in pushing the water across one table seam and failed at the next. A maintenance worker offered a folded cloth. The child ignored it.

Crown’s proposed comparison might strengthen the test, but it would also consume the preference while claiming to verify it. Delay no longer offered a reversible path. Every additional proof request altered the mind under examination.

Cal called a recess of four minutes and wrote the operative order by hand. He removed two sentences that reached beyond the case. He added an expiration condition, a review path, and a fraud reservation. Narrowness mattered. So did not making Iven earn the same protection again at each institutional door.

When court resumed, Iven had kept the Callis feed. He did not watch Cal. Ada stood with both hands on Crown’s red-edged risk folder.

“The court finds present evidence of a preference not recoverable from the Eiren distress packet,” Cal said. “Iven Ko requested novel visual access, selected an ordinary Callis location without encoded precedent, and declined a source-weighted comparison after receiving a cost warning. Those choices satisfy the Novel Preference Test for this proceeding.”

He pressed his seal into the paper order.

“Temporary standing in continuance is granted through the active coherence period and any court-approved rest interval necessary to complete this hearing. No destructive analysis may occur without Iven Ko’s informed agreement while that standing remains effective.”

Ada said, “Crown preserves appeal and requests clarity concerning the property petition.”

“Title remains undecided. Personhood does not require ownership to resolve first.”

“And compulsory analysis?”

Cal turned the order so the timing lamp could capture its physical seal.

“The Compulsory Evidence Clause no longer applies while standing lasts.”

Nia disconnected Crown’s proposed image set. Osei closed the route packet. On the west canteen feed, the pressure lamp changed from amber to green, and people carried their trays through the reopened service window while Iven watched a place no prior version of him had known to request.

Chapter 8 — The Price of a Quiet Settlement

Ada removed the word unavoidable from the settlement and watched Crown’s risk model turn red.

The deletion changed no mass schedule. No catalyst shipment missed a window. Yet the legal drafting wafer immediately marked four dependent clauses for executive review, as though one adjective held the Meridian Mouth in alignment.

She sat alone in a Callis conference room two chambers below the Court. The room had no networked wall and no hidden phase equipment; Nia Daro had inspected both conditions before agreeing to bring Iven. A physical air duct clicked whenever station rotation put its outer valve under load. On the table, Ada had arranged three copies of the proposed order, a public incident statement, and a supply map sealed beneath gray paper.

Jalen Orr appeared on her private operations link. His image carried none of the delay that would have made him seem remote.

“You changed approved language,” he said.

“Because Ko will reject a statement that repeats the official cause.”

“The official cause remains under dispute.”

“Then we cannot require him to affirm it as the price of recognition.”

Jalen’s eyes moved across a copy on his side. He read legal pages as if they contained schedules, locating every term that could slip. “The board authorized recognition of loss. Not intent.”

“The offer says rescue failure.”

“It also preserves the bell against further access.”

“Under Crown supervision.”

“Under joint seal.”

Ada touched the clause. “Crown selects two of three custodians. That will read as supervision because it is supervision.”

“Then draft it more gently.”

A decade earlier she might have obeyed the instruction without hearing its admission. She had defended the Eiren seal for twelve years by keeping claims narrow, disclosure slower than panic, and the Mouth’s dependents visible in rooms where old guilt produced simple proposals. She had not needed to lie about what Crown wanted. Precision could shelter control better than falsehood.

“Ko has standing,” she said. “Gentle control still requires his agreement.”

“Temporary status granted by a Callis examiner under an emergency article.”

“Effective status until appeal.”

Jalen leaned closer to his lens. Behind him, route arcs crossed an operations display. “Do not let a provisional witness negotiate corridor policy.”

“He is negotiating access to himself.”

“The distinction disappears if his refusal forces a public seizure dispute.”

Ada looked at the covered supply map. “No. It becomes inconvenient.”

Jalen ended the call after ordering her to return with a signed settlement or a written account of why Callis had chosen litigation over habitat safety.

The phrasing placed the future report before the meeting. Ada saved the instruction with its time mark.

Nia arrived first. She brought a passive phase meter, two sealed glucose packs, and no briefcase. Her archive jacket carried a fresh repair along one cuff where Crown quarantine staff had cut a closure tag. She examined the room again despite having inspected it that morning.

“You removed a transmitter,” Ada said.

“I removed a Crown transmitter from a room represented as private.”

“It managed the air valve.”

“The valve still works.”

“It no longer reports faults.”

“I put a paper indicator on it.” Nia pointed to a thin strip fluttering beside the duct. “A person can look.”

“You have an expansive view of privacy.”

“I have a recent education in undisclosed access.”

Ada accepted the point. “The settlement conference record remains local until all parties agree to release it.”

“Does Crown accept Iven as a party?”

“For this conference.”

“That qualification will matter to him.”

“It matters to me too.”

Orderlies brought the vocal body through the clinical door in a reclining chair. Iven wore the same gray linen as at the hearing. A court timing lamp hung from the chair rail under Nia’s seal. He inspected the blank walls, the paper air indicator, then the three documents.

“No window?” he asked.

“Not for this meeting,” Ada said. “You may request one.”

“I wanted to know whether you had learned anything.”

Nia checked the body’s pulse. “Ada has agreed to one hour maximum, with a rest decision at thirty minutes.”

“Likely cost?” Iven asked.

“Lower than route recall. Negotiation can raise stress. We stop if the phase margin widens past the line I showed you.”

He found that line on the meter before nodding. “Proceed.”

Ada slid the shortest document toward him but kept her hand on its edge. “This offer does four things. Crown will join Callis in recognizing Eiren as a rescue failure rather than a navigation loss. The remaining signal receives permanent preservation under joint custody. No party seeks further embodiment without your informed request. Crown funds a complete registry reconstruction and restitution process.”

“What does Crown receive?”

“No inquiry into individual or institutional intent. Existing corridor authority remains in place. The case closes after the incident statement and preservation order enter the record.”

Iven looked at the document without touching it. “You said four things.”

“The registry and restitution share one clause.”

“No. You counted what I receive and stopped counting when Crown appeared.”

Ada let go of the paper. “Crown receives finality.”

“Better.”

Nia sat beside him rather than behind the chair. “What does permanent preservation mean if the archive estimate changes?”

“C-9 remains sealed against destructive access. Non-destructive maintenance continues. If degradation threatens total loss, all custodians must agree before intervention.”

“All custodians except Iven?”

“Iven’s standing may not remain active during a future degradation event.”

“Then the seal expires where his voice does.”

“The order preserves his stated conditions during this proceeding.”

Nia tapped the relevant clause. “It preserves conditions ‘to the extent recognized by then-applicable continuance law.’ That gives the future custodian every argument Crown already made.”

Ada had written those words herself. They reflected current doctrine. They also placed the risk on the person least able to appear later.

“I can replace that phrase with a specific prohibition against voluntary Crown access,” she said.

“Why only Crown?” Iven asked.

“Because Crown controls the bell schedule and carries the strongest operational claim.”

“Callis could become curious.”

Nia did not defend her institution. “Yes.”

Iven pushed the preservation order back. “Then curiosity needs more than one name.”

Ada marked the clause for revision. Negotiation could improve the seal and still fail on intent. She had come prepared for that distinction.

“The incident statement matters beyond Crown,” she said. “Public records still call the convoy lost to navigation. This changes the legal classification to rescue failure and restores Eiren’s registry as a casualty record rather than salvage inventory.”

“Does it say why rescue failed?”

“It states that tuned mass remained committed to Mouth stabilization and did not open the Eiren route.”

“Who committed it?”

“The historical decision chain remains incomplete.”

“Does the statement say someone decided?”

“It says the convoy did not detune the required bells.”

Iven looked at Nia. “That describes a door staying closed.”

Nia kept her hands on her knees. “Yes.”

Ada unfolded the proposed public language.

Aster Crown Transit and Callis recognize that Eiren Reach suffered a rescue failure during Meridian instability. Available convoy mass remained aligned to corridor stabilization, preventing a viable Eiren approach before thermal-lattice collapse. All recovered persons and records shall receive protection under the Continuance Articles.

Iven read it twice. His eyes moved more slowly across the final sentence.

“You recognize failure,” he said. “You protect whatever reaches you. Between those actions, no one acts.”

“The statement does not exonerate anyone.”

“It does not accuse weather either. It leaves a space where choice should go.”

“Naming a chooser without admissible proof creates another false record.”

“You know more than you admit here.”

Ada had expected anger. His calm forced her to examine each answer before using it.

“I know Crown chose to hold the Mouth,” she said. “I do not yet have evidence I can place in an open file assigning that decision to a particular officer or executive body.”

Nia’s attention sharpened. “You just moved beyond the public record.”

“I moved exactly one step. The convoy mass did not remain aligned through accident.”

Iven’s pulse rose. Nia glanced at the phase margin but did not stop the meeting.

“Then put that sentence in the statement,” he said.

“Crown will not approve it.”

“Will not, or cannot?”

“Will not.”

The paper air indicator snapped once as the duct changed load. Ada thought of Morcant’s catalyst lines, where every delayed shipment became a choice among industrial heat, food culture, and reserve.
