She opened the room and gave the duty officer the sealed key. She dictated Osei’s temporary restrictions into the lamp, then added her own conflict: she remained his supervisor, a potential disciplinary witness, and a participant in the thermal evidence. Cal would have to appoint an independent investigator.

Before she finished, Ada requested a private channel.

Nia accepted from the integrity terminal with the duty officer present.

Ada appeared in a small frame, seated somewhere inside Crown’s Callis office. Behind her, staff moved hard copies into cases. “Did Marr release it?”

“You can ask through the investigation.”

“The extraction omitted signal fields. That choice looks technical.”

“So does opening an inert bell nine times.”

Ada let the answer pass. “Jalen wants C-9 moved before the crowd reaches the mass ring.”

“Your engineer signed that movement risks collapse.”

“He believes public disorder changes the risk balance.”

“The concourse does not change glass stability.”

“It changes station security. Someone will try to reach the bell.”

“Then protect the door.”

“Callis marshals are already refusing Crown support because they think we intend seizure.”

“You do intend seizure.”

Ada looked past the frame, listened to someone Nia could not hear, and muted her side for four seconds. When sound came back, her voice had lowered. “The quiet settlement cannot survive this.”

“It did not survive Iven’s refusal.”

“Private recognition remained possible. Public acknowledgment of the rescue decision, preservation of the bell, controlled restitution talks. That path has closed.”

“Because people learned a count?”

“Because the count now sets the scale of any concession. If Crown admits one unauthorized recall, every display will answer with nine. If we contest the wording, we look as if we dispute the burns. If we accept it, the merits hearing becomes public judgment before evidence completes.”

Nia watched staff cross behind Ada with sealed cases. “You are still describing harm to Crown.”

“I am describing the position from which Crown will react. Jalen has ordered full opposition, including challenge to Iven’s standing. I would prefer you understand that before the filing reaches Cal.”

“Why tell me?”

“Because sixty thousand requests to call a damaged person can become coercion without anyone signing an order.”

On that point, they agreed.

The public queue passed eighty thousand. Nia opened a sample, not to read sentiment but to classify demands. Some people asked for a transcript. Some asked the court to let Iven choose. Many requested an activation at a fixed civic hour so all six habitats could listen. A smaller group offered coherence donations, as if phase state could transfer from one nervous system to another. Several threatened archive staff. Two threatened Crown offices. One message contained a route diagram to the mass ring.

Nia sent the threats to station security and kept the rest in evidence order. She then drafted a public notice with one operative sentence:

No public request, audience count, or institutional need authorizes an activation without the signal person’s informed preference.

The communications clerk read it. “They will say we are hiding him.”

“We are keeping them from summoning him.”

“Can we state whether he will appear at the merits hearing?”

“No. That choice has not been made.”

“Can we confirm nine returns?”

“Confirm nine recorded activations. Correct the unit.”

The clerk revised the notice. Nia required a link to the raw, redacted ledger fields so the correction did not look like denial.

Before release, a marshal carried a paper envelope through the reception lock. An older woman outside had refused to submit it through the public queue because every electronic form required an Eiren civic number. Her envelope held a worn transit work chit, two repair-pay receipts, and a photograph of a man kneeling beside a thermal pipe with his sleeves tied above both elbows.

A note on the back gave his name and occupation: night-shift valve fitter, temporary contract, dayward loop three. The public Eiren registry returned no match.

Osei read over Nia’s shoulder. “Family record?”

“Work record.”

“He had family if someone kept this.”

“That does not tell us his legal residence.”

“It tells us he worked there when the loops failed.”

Nia examined the chit under a document lamp. Its corner bore Eiren port control, not the civic registry. The entry date fell six weeks before the disaster. No exit stamp appeared. A transient worker could leave without a matched stamp if the outbound office failed to close the permit. During the last thirty-one hours, that office would have had other work.

The woman waited beyond the glass with both hands around the railing. She did not ask them to call Iven. She wanted a name accepted by a form.

Nia opened a new custody category: Eiren registry reconstruction, public submissions, unverified. The archive system warned that no active case authorized it. She attached the Court’s preservation notice and limited the category to non-signal documents. Nothing submitted there could reach C-9, the route layer, or a vocal-body system.

“You are opening an archive outside order,” Osei said.

“I am receiving evidence the public queue was designed to reject.”

“That sounds familiar.”

“It should. Watch the difference.” Nia showed him the access list. Any clerk could view the catalog. No one could publish a name until two independent records placed the person at Eiren during the failure. Submitters retained copies and could withdraw private material. Every exclusion required a written reason.

Osei looked toward the woman. “And if there is only one record because Crown discarded the rest?”

“Then the reconstruction carries uncertainty instead of erasing the person.”

The duty officer signed as second custodian. Nia scanned the work chit, returned every original to its envelope, and sent the woman a receipt through the lock. Within three minutes, fourteen more paper packets appeared at the marshal desk. Some bore civic numbers. Several carried work permits from convoy crews whose names had never belonged to Eiren’s resident rolls.

Nia assigned two clerks to receive without deciding. The count could wait until the records had edges strong enough to survive challenge. For the first time that day, public pressure produced work that did not require touching Iven.

Osei stood two steps behind her under paired-access restriction. He offered no edits.

They released the notice to the concourse displays.

For a minute, the central wall showed the full text. The crowd read in uneven silence. Then comments rose across the lower margin. Some accepted the correction. Others wrote that Callis had replaced one euphemism with another. The largest public petition changed its demand from CALL HIM LIVE to LET HIM CHOOSE IN PUBLIC.

Nia did not find the second wording harmless. A choice made before an audience could still carry the audience’s hand on it.

Cal’s office called as the merits clock crossed thirty-one hours. His clerk requested confirmation that C-9 remained untouched, that Osei’s release excluded signal data, and that Nia could staff the preservation field through a public disturbance. She answered yes to all three. Then the clerk asked whether public pressure affected medical timing.

“It affects the conditions of consent,” Nia said. “Not the glass.”

“We will add that to the sealed conference.”

Outside reception, station marshals extended the service lane with low railings. People yielded without leaving. Food sellers brought insulated containers. Court reporters set up hand slates. Eiren descendants arrived carrying work cards and family registers, not signs. A freight departure posted late because the concourse could no longer clear fast enough.

The gathering had acquired logistics. It would not disperse after one notice.

Osei came to stand at Nia’s left, far enough from the terminal to honor his restriction. “You should take me off the team.”

“I told you the conditions.”

“Crown will use me against the archive.”

“They will use your dismissal as proof we agree the ledger should have remained hidden.”

“That is strategy.”

“It is also true.” Nia handed him a paper checklist for the next field watch. “Paired access. You do not touch a control alone.”

He took the sheet. “Understood.”

Across the glass, the Callis concourse display shifted from the return ledger to Cal’s scheduling order. FAST MERITS HEARING. PUBLIC FEED SUBJECT TO MEDICAL LIMITS. The words traveled through the crowd as people raised their slates.

Nia could no longer see the floor between them.

Behind her, Osei began reading the preservation checklist aloud for his second signer. Ahead, the station settled around a hearing no private settlement could contain.

Chapter 13 — Person for the Duration

Cal cut the phrase while actively coherent from the draft order and the gap closed around his knife.

The strip lay on a black drafting mat beneath the chamber timing lamp. Without those three words, the operative sentence granted Iven Ko provisional personhood from the moment of his Novel Preference finding until final disposition. With them, personhood existed only during embodiment. Every breath taken by the vocal body carried rights. Every medically necessary rest returned the same human pattern to disputed property.

Cal placed the words back into the sentence.

Sena Pell watched from the opposite side of the drafting table. She had arrived from the multi-polity circuit less than an hour earlier, still wearing the soft-soled travel shoes required in Callis’s axial chambers. Her judicial coat remained folded over one arm.

“You have moved that phrase four times,” she said.

“Five.”

“Has repetition improved the statute?”

“No.”

“Then stop asking the paper to change it.”

Cal sealed the draft in its current form and followed her into the hearing chamber.

The public gallery sat empty under medical restriction. A live feed carried the proceeding to the Callis concourse with a fixed delay that allowed the clerk to remove protected body data. Even so, sound from the gathering reached the court through structural vibration. Thousands of feet in the station’s central ring made a faint irregular pressure against the handrail.

Ada Neme occupied Crown’s table alone. Jalen Orr had removed her operations title from the electronic appearance line but had not replaced her as counsel. Nia Daro sat at the medical rail with three field summaries and no view into the vocal-body room. Iven remained at rest in bell C-9. No one had asked him to wake for a hearing about whether sleep ended his rights.

Sena took the central chair. Cal sat one level below as appellate custodian and author of the temporary order under review.

The clerk called the matter.

Ada rose. “Crown accepts the Novel Preference finding for the activation in which it occurred. We contest extension beyond the text of the Continuance Articles.”

Sena said, “State the boundary you propose.”

“Standing in continuance attaches to an actively coherent signal person. It does not convert every dormant phase record into a perpetual rights holder.”

“Does Crown consider Iven Ko a person now?”

Ada looked toward the empty medical rail beside Nia. “He is not now embodied.”

“That does not answer.”

“It answers under the statute.”

Sena let the silence remain until Ada added, “Crown considers him a person during valid active coherence.”

Cal wrote the sentence on a case strip. Its grammar performed the work Crown wanted. Person occupied a temporary condition, like pressurized or awake.

Nia received permission to give technical evidence from her seat. She explained that rest did not create a copy or a different source. The same nonrenewable phase structure remained in C-9 while the vocal body deactivated. Respeaking translated a portion into tissue and spent coherence. Rest reduced current expenditure but did not stop boundary drift.

Ada asked, “Can the phase layer form a new preference while no body operates?”

“No.”

“Can it receive legal advice?”

“No.”

“Can it understand an order?”

“Not in any way we can test without reconstruction.”

“Can it suffer pain?”

“Not as an embodied nervous system.”

“Then what present capacity grounds personhood during rest?”

Nia’s fingers settled on the edge of her field summary. “Continuity.”

“That is a conclusion.”

“It is a measurement. The resting pattern and the next embodied state draw from one diminishing structure. Damage during rest changes who can appear later.”

Ada turned to Sena. “Continuity of material does not settle continuity of legal person. A living citizen remains metabolically active during sleep. A phase record does not.”

Cal interrupted before analogy replaced the statute. “The Court granted standing because Iven formed a novel preference. Does Crown claim that finding becomes false when the body rests?”

“No. It becomes inoperative outside the condition the Articles recognize.”

“Would Crown permit destructive analysis during that interval?”

“The Compulsory Evidence Clause applies to non-person property, subject to preservation orders.”

The answer tightened every face in the room. Ada had not invented the consequence. She had stated it because concealment would make later litigation worse.

Sena leaned toward her microphone. “Under Crown’s reading, the Court must schedule a person into absence before exercising compulsory access.”

“Only where analysis can occur without defeating a valid preservation order.”

“And if analysis prevents the person from returning?”

“Then preservation principles should bar it.”

“Should. Not personhood.”

“Under present law, yes.”

Cal watched the feed indicator blink as the delayed broadcast carried that admission toward the concourse. Public anger would treat it as monstrous. Judicial discomfort did not make the reading implausible. The Articles had been drafted for emergency records that either passed the test during examination or returned to archive inventory. No drafter had built a category for a mind whose legal existence alternated with a medical switch.

Sena called Cal to explain his order.

He placed the operative strips on the display. His temporary ruling had granted standing from Iven’s request for a window showing Callis until the court could review duration. It blocked the Compulsory Evidence Clause, required informed consent for activation, and preserved C-9 in place.

“Textual basis?” Sena asked.

“The Articles say a remnant that forms a novel preference ‘shall receive standing in continuance for the duration of active coherence.’”

“What does duration modify?”

“Crown says standing. I read it as the period in which the Court must determine and protect the preference.”

“Why?”

“Because active coherence has no stable clock. A person may be embodied, resting, or distributed between recoverable phase bands during one return. If rights follow only audible speech, custodians gain power to end those rights by deactivation.”

Ada said, “Custodians already control medical activation under safety law.”

“Medical control does not usually change the patient into property.”

Sena raised a hand before Ada replied. “Custodian Orsen, are you asking us to recognize indefinite personhood in every remnant that once passed the Novel Preference Test?”

“No.”

“Where does it end?”

“When no recoverable coherence remains, or when a valid preference authorizes disposition, or when adjudication resolves the protected interests.”

“Those boundaries do not appear in the Articles.”

“They become necessary once the law encounters a person damaged by the law’s own process.”

Sena’s expression did not change. “Necessity can produce narrow orders. It can also produce doctrine no one knows how to contain.”

The court recessed for twenty minutes.

In the order room, Sena removed her judicial coat and hung it from a rail. Cal stood at the wall of case strips while she read Iven’s preference findings again. The request for a real Callis window had no encoded precedent. His later demand for cost disclosure showed continuing agency. Neither finding answered whether agency retained legal force while it could not act.

“You want to repair the statute,” Sena said.

“I want to prevent a switch from deciding status.”

“Those may amount to the same project.”

“Not today.”

She tapped the draft with one blunt fingernail. “Your first version grants personhood until all coherence ends. Your second grants it through litigation. Your third leaves duration undefined. Each one follows sympathy farther than text.”

“Not sympathy.”

“You believe this witness suffered an institutional wrong. So do I. That belief makes sympathy more dangerous, not less.”

Cal looked at her. “Should we ignore the wrong?”

“We should refuse to build permanence from it before adversarial argument.” Sena returned the strip to the wall. “A hard case does not excuse soft boundaries.”

The phrase carried none of the comfort of a maxim. Sena had spent decades watching emergency courts turn compelling facts into authorities that later reached people no one had imagined. Cal knew the caution. He had used it himself during the Pellin shortage when petitioners asked him to convert temporary ration priority into inherited entitlement.

“What can we protect without deciding permanence?” he asked.

“Actions already taken by a recognized person.”

“His preference to receive cost information.”

“Yes.”

“His selection of counsel.”

“If validly made.”

“He requested independent counsel during the standing hearing.”

“Then appoint counsel.”

“His medical choices while resting?”

Sena considered. “Choices made while active can direct immediate care through the same return. Do not pretend that answers future recalls.”

“There is no technical line between immediate care and deciding whether to wake him tomorrow.”

“Then write the legal line around what this record supports. Field adjustment, body stabilization, stopping an activation already begun. Future reconstruction remains open.”

Cal felt the contradiction sharpen. Rights would cover the medicine needed once Iven appeared, but not the decision that made him appear. Counsel could object in the interval, yet counsel would hold no durable instruction beyond the current return. The law protected a person from pain after the custodian had already crossed the boundary that restored personhood.

“That leaves the central harm intact,” he said.

“It leaves the central question for full argument.”

“Delay consumes him.”

“Then hear it quickly. Do not solve urgency by pretending you have solved doctrine.”

A clerk knocked and entered with a sealed medical message. During recess, C-9’s preservation field had required another correction. Nia reported no activation and no measurable event loss, but the drift envelope had widened at its upper edge.

Sena read the message. “How much time?”

“No one can answer without a destructive diagnostic.”

“Of course.” She returned it. “Every fact in this case charges admission.”

They went back to court.

Cal proposed a narrower order. Crown objected to every clause that carried authority through rest. Nia objected to any phrase equating rest with absence. Sena required each side to state practical consequences instead of preferred labels.

Under the proposal, Iven would receive independent continuance counsel immediately. Counsel could access the full sealed record, attend technical work, challenge any activation request, and speak during periods when Iven rested. Counsel could not consent to a new activation unless Iven had given a specific instruction during valid coherence.

Immediate medical choices would belong to Iven while embodied. Those choices could continue through temporary rest within the same clinical episode: field intensity, body support, pain control, who could observe, and when an activation already in progress must stop. The archive could make emergency adjustments needed to prevent unchosen loss, subject to later review.

Future recalls remained outside that authority. Existing law offered no settled method for a preference to govern after the active interval ended. The Court would not infer consent from prior cooperation, counsel’s judgment, public demand, or evidentiary need.

Ada read the final clause twice. “Crown can accept no inferred consent. We object to counsel blocking an application before active standing resumes.”

“Counsel may object,” Sena said. “The Court decides whether any application may proceed.”

“On whose rights?”

“On the basis of preservation and the prior valid standing finding.”

“That avoids the status question.”

“It postpones it without handing you the bell.”

Nia asked permission to speak outside her technical role. Sena denied it, then asked what technical consequence remained unaddressed.

“If no person exists during rest,” Nia said, “who owns the risk of waiting?”

Sena’s eyes narrowed slightly. “That sounds legal.”

“The field requires choices. Lower correction risks diffusion. Higher correction risks shear. Neutral settings do not exist.”

“Who chooses now?”

“I do under the preservation order.”

“Would counsel need notice?”

“Yes.”

“Would Iven’s prior tolerance choices guide you?”

“If he has made them with adequate information.”

“Then add them to immediate medical authority.”

The clerk revised the clause. Nia sat down, dissatisfied but no longer excluded from the mechanism.

Sena did not release her. “Give us an actual sequence, Resonantist. No abstractions.”

Nia brought up the clinical record from Iven’s last waking. The display omitted memory content and showed only body condition, field load, and recorded choices. Seven minutes after stabilization, his breathing had become shallow. Nia offered glucose, reduced light, or early deactivation. He chose glucose because he wanted enough time to ask about process cost. Twelve minutes later, he asked for the real Callis window instead of an Eiren simulation. Near the end, he agreed to rest after receiving a promise that no later activation would occur without explanation.

“Which choices continue now?” Sena asked.

Ada answered first. “The promise binds the custodian as a condition of any future consent discussion. It does not itself create standing during dormancy.”

Nia said, “His light tolerance and stabilization limits guide the next body. The request for a Callis window also remains in the room unless he changes it.”

“Would you treat the glucose choice as permanent?”

“No. The next body may be hungry differently.”

“Would you treat his agreement to rest as permission to activate later?”

“No.”

“Why does one instruction carry and another stop?”

Nia pointed to the trace. “Some choices govern a procedure already underway. Rest completes that procedure. A later respeaking begins another destructive procedure.”

Cal saw the boundary Sena had demanded. It came from action rather than metaphysics: continuation of an existing clinical episode versus initiation of a new one. The distinction would not answer every case. It could keep this order from pretending to answer future access.

Ada tested it. “Suppose the field destabilizes tonight. Nia can increase correction without waking him?”

“If delay risks unchosen loss, yes,” Cal said.

“Suppose increasing correction might preserve testimony while reducing bodily comfort at the next activation.”

“Counsel receives notice. The court resolves it if time permits. In emergency, Nia preserves the widest future choice, not the most evidence.”

“Suppose no adjustment can prevent loss without a brief reconstruction.”

No one answered immediately.

The public feed delay light moved once around its frame. Somewhere beyond the chamber, thousands of viewers waited to hear whether necessity could make a body without its owner’s consent.

Sena said, “This order does not authorize reconstruction as medical preservation.”

Ada kept her eyes on Cal. “Even if the alternative destroys all remaining coherence?”

“Especially then,” Nia said. “We cannot preserve a person by spending the person without permission.”

Sena turned to her. “That conclusion belongs to the question we have reserved.”

“It belongs to my hands if the alarm sounds tonight.”

Cal requested leave to add an emergency clause. He drafted it at the table while both sides watched. Technical custodians could take non-destructive action to maintain existing phase conditions. No emergency label could authorize embodiment, broad semantic access, or a diagnostic read that materially spent coherence. If all non-destructive options failed, the custodian had to seek the Court and appointed counsel, even when delay carried risk.

Ada objected that the clause might force preventable loss. Nia answered that an involuntary activation could cause the same loss while violating the preference conditions already on record. Sena narrowed the language to C-9 and the present case. Then she adopted it.

The independent advocate, still waiting beyond the rail, sent one written question through the clerk: could counsel demand deactivation during a future clinical episode if Iven became unable to speak? Nia said the body’s recorded stop thresholds already triggered automatic rest. Counsel could enforce those thresholds and ask for stricter ones while Iven remained able to choose. Counsel could not substitute a broader preference after capacity failed.

Cal added that limitation. The order now described hands, instruments, and decisions rather than claiming an answer about permanent identity. It still left the central gap exposed.

Cal asked how the Court would describe status in the caption. Ada proposed “conditional standing.” The clerk proposed “continuing preservation after standing.” Both phrases concealed the human consequence beneath a procedure.

Sena dictated the wording herself.

Iven Ko shall be recognized as a person for the duration of active coherence. Actions and immediate medical choices validly made during that coherence remain operative through medically necessary rest within the same return. No ruling is made today regarding authority over future recalls.

The sentence entered the public feed.

Noise reached them through the station structure seconds later, a broad vibration from the Callis concourse. Cal could not tell whether it carried approval or anger. The same words gave Iven a lawyer and preserved the category Crown had used to analyze him.

Ada requested clarification. “Does personhood end at deactivation?”

“For purposes not preserved by this order, the statute does not recognize active standing,” Sena said.

“Then Crown’s position prevailed.”

“No. Your Compulsory Evidence application remains barred pending merits.”

“Under preservation rather than personhood.”

“Under this Court’s authority to prevent an irreversible act before it decides who may authorize it.”

Ada sat. She had obtained the narrow duration language and lost immediate access.

Cal entered the independent counsel appointment. The advocate had been waiting in a side room with no access to C-9 until the ruling. A court officer brought them in, placed a sealed case copy in their hands, and read the restrictions aloud. Counsel could receive Iven’s instructions at his next medically approved activation. Until then, they could refuse nothing on his behalf permanently, but they could force every request into open review.

Nia handed counsel the current field summary. “Read the drift note before anything else.”

“Not the testimony plan?”

“The body has to arrive before it can give testimony.”

Counsel opened the field summary first.

Sena rose after the clerk sealed the order. As she passed Cal’s lower chair, she stopped long enough to speak without the microphones carrying it.

“You are going to ask this court for more,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Then bring a boundary, not only a wound.”

She left through the judges’ lock.

Cal remained until the chamber cleared. On the drafting table, the final order occupied two physical strips. One named Iven a person during active coherence. The second carried counsel, medical authority, and preservation through rest. A blank connector lay between them where the clerk had expected a cross-reference.

He did not fill it. Existing law supplied no words for the interval.

The new advocate carried the sealed case toward the Archive ring. Nia followed with the field summary. Cal watched both pass beneath the red timing lamp as it counted down to merits, bearing powers that reached Iven’s next waking but stopped at the choice to wake him again.

Interlude II: Eighteen Thousand Five Hundred Six

CALLIS ARCHIVE
EIREN REGISTRY RECONSTRUCTION
PUBLIC REVISION MEMORANDUM 4

Purpose of revision

This memorandum corrects the number of persons present at Eiren Reach during the final thermal failure. Earlier public records counted registered residents only. Port-control fragments, contractor payrolls, convoy berthing sheets, family submissions, and recovered clinic rosters establish an additional transient population.

No entry below derives from a conscious phase record. No clerk queried choir bell C-9. The reconstruction proceeds through ordinary documents so that one damaged witness does not carry the burden of naming a colony.

Prior certified figure: 18,204 registered residents.
Restored category: 312 transients present without completed civic registration.
Corrected figure: 18,516 persons lost.

The prior figure remains accurate as a resident subtotal. It may not be used as the total dead.

I. Reconstruction rules

  1. A name enters the confirmed register when two independent records place that person at Eiren during the thirty-one-hour failure window.
  2. A damaged name may remain partially printed. Clerks must not invent missing letters from frequency, family resemblance, occupation, or expected language.
  3. Occupation does not substitute for identity. “Valve fitter, unknown” remains a record of presence but not a recovered name.
  4. Departure requires affirmative evidence. An open work permit without an exit stamp does not prove continued presence by itself.
  5. Records created after the disaster may corroborate but cannot independently confirm. Family memory supplies search terms, not automatic certification.
  6. Resident and transient categories describe registry status. They do not grade attachment, responsibility, or loss.

Clerk annotation, Revision 2: The phrase unregistered worker caused repeated error. Many restored persons held valid port and payroll records. They lacked Eiren civic registration because their contracts were temporary, their applications remained pending, or their employers had failed to file. Revision 3 replaced the phrase with transient.

Review annotation, Revision 3: Temporary status did not mean temporary presence at the moment of death.

II. Resident file fragments

E-000214 | Nera Vos | dayward mirror scheduler
Civic ledger confirms residence in cavern district four. Shift exchange records show Vos transferring two collector fields to manual control during the first hour of thermal instability. Final checksum lacks the closing six characters. No reconstruction attempted beyond the surviving text.

E-001903 | Pel Anik | school pressure inspector
Education payroll and district maintenance sheets agree. A family submission included three inspection tags bearing Anik’s hand mark. The tags establish ordinary work, not location during the last hour. Confirmation rests on payroll continuity plus an emergency shelter roster.

E-004771 | Sio Maren | food-culture technician
Clinic supply record identifies Maren by civic number after a nutrient-vat burn eleven days before failure. The public disaster index had rendered the occupation as “culture stock.” Revision 4 restores the person separately from the inventory.

E-008092 | Jo Pellat | tram brake tender
Two route sheets preserve the name. A third carries only Jo P. No clerk may merge that fragment with Pellat’s file unless the vehicle number also agrees. Vehicle agreement confirmed in Revision 3.

E-011440 | [checksum break] Afi | thermal-loop apprentice
First name survives. Family name does not. Apprenticeship roll, tool issue, and shelter assignment place Afi at Eiren. Public display shall preserve the break: Afi [family name unrecovered].

E-015608 | Ralen Ti | sanitation diver
Civic registration confirms residence. Pump-duty logs continue through the twenty-sixth hour of failure. Later copies changed diver to driver. The original pressure certification resolves the occupation.

E-017999 | Meis Orra | night clinic porter
Hospital staffing and meal allotment agree. A Crown salvage summary listed Orra under movable medical equipment because the personnel column slipped one field during conversion. Revision 4 removes that classification.

Resident reconciliation result: 18,204 confirmed. No numerical change from the final civic subtotal. Changes in Revision 4 affect names, occupations, damaged fields, and the separation of persons from inventory lines.

III. Transient source set

Port control preserved four partial arrival rolls. The rolls listed 527 open entries during the quarter containing the disaster. Departure ledgers closed 189 of them before the thermal event. Independent records showed twenty-six additional people leaving on unsynchronized freight movements. The remaining candidates required individual review.

Contractor payrolls supplied presence marks for thermal crews, tunnel surveyors, medical installers, food-culture auditors, and convoy support staff. Berthing sheets added short-route pilots whose employers maintained no Eiren payroll. Clinic rosters identified dependents and injured travelers waiting for onward passage.

Family submissions opened 143 candidate files. Seventy-nine later matched port or payroll records. Forty-one described registered residents already counted. Eleven lacked any independent record and remain in the unresolved appendix. Twelve concerned people who had departed before failure.

No unresolved appendix entry contributes to the corrected total.

IV. Restored transient fragments

T-0001 | Haro Sen | night-shift valve fitter
Port work chit and dayward loop payroll agree. Arrival occurred six weeks before failure. No departure appears. A family photograph identifies the worksite but does not supply confirmation; the two institutional records do.

T-0027 | Kemi Ar | medical sterilizer installer
Contractor invoice abbreviates the name. Arden Coil certification preserves the full form. Clinic bed records place Ar under observation after solvent exposure during the failure window.

T-0068 | Daro Venn | freight galley worker
Berthing list and meal-credit ledger agree. The shared surname does not establish relation to any present Callis custodian. No kinship inference entered.

T-0104 | [checksum break] M | tunnel survey assistant
Survey payment survives under employee number. Port entry preserves only the first letter of the personal field. Confirmation attaches to the employee number and biometric work seal. Public form reads: M [name unrecovered], tunnel survey assistant.

T-0159 | Ilya Sorn | short-route pilot
Convoy berth assignment and fuel-account signature place Sorn on the surface apron. An automated departure estimate appeared in an old Crown summary. No launch record, pressure-cycle entry, or mass change supports it. The estimate cannot close the file.

T-0216 | Sera Val | food-culture auditor
Employer travel order planned a three-day visit. The disaster began on the second day. Eiren lodging and emergency ration records confirm continued presence.

T-0274 | Niko Tath | dependent, age nine
Clinic intake and guardian permit agree. Contractor payroll omitted dependents by design. Revision 2 excluded Tath. Revision 3 restored the entry after the guardian permit checksum matched port control.

T-0312 | Olan Re | mass-cradle cleaner
Two employers denied active contract. A shift token recovered from Callis family submissions matched Eiren cradle seventeen’s service log. Port control confirms entry. The final work mark occurred eleven hours before lattice failure.

Transient reconciliation result: 312 confirmed persons.

V. Count correction notice

The Eiren registry previously closed at 18,204 because the civic ledger counted residents and excluded open port files. That closure propagated into disaster education, insurance settlement, Crown salvage records, and Callis’s public index.

Revision 4 restores 312 transients through record-by-record corroboration. Arithmetic now reads:

18,204 residents + 312 transients = 18,516 persons lost.

Clerks shall not describe the additional 312 as included within the 18,204. Clerks shall not replace individual entries with the correction figure. The number supports reconciliation; names and preserved fragments support the register.

VI. Publication instruction

Display confirmed names in registry order, not by social rank, occupation, age, evidentiary completeness, or dramatic interest. Keep checksum breaks visible. Link every correction to its source class and reviewing clerk. Preserve unresolved files without adding them to the total.

The heading on future certified copies shall read:

EIREN REACH: 18,516 PERSONS LOST

Below that heading, the first entry begins with Nera Vos at the dayward mirrors. The final confirmed transient entry ends with Olan Re’s shift token. Between them, the Eiren registry retains each recoverable name, each honest break, and each occupation only once.

Chapter 14 — Ninety Days of Heat

Ada had four habitat reserve curves open when Latchkey’s pump architect refused her question.

“I asked how long you can preserve heat exchange after catalyst interruption,” Ada said.

Ema Orsen looked back through the remote engineering link. Transmission compression had flattened the color from her work coat, but it could not conceal the wet line at her collar or the grease pressed into one cheek. Machinery moved behind the acoustic baffle at her shoulder. Every fourteen seconds, the image trembled with the start of a large pump.

“No,” Ema said. “You asked how long we can preserve an acceptable heat-exchange rate. Define acceptable.”

The evidence room held Ada, two Crown analysts, Cal Orsen, and a court recorder whose timing lamp marked every pause. Cal had not spoken since his daughter appeared on the wall. He sat beyond the counsel rail with his hands folded around a paper schedule. Ada knew enough about estrangement not to mistake his stillness for neutrality.

“An internal temperature compatible with continued habitation,” Ada said.

“For whom?” Ema reached out of frame. A diagram replaced half her face: six circulation trunks, each divided into inhabited and industrial loops. “If we shed the foundry and agricultural bays, we preserve the residential core longer. Then food production ends. If we preserve agriculture, two lower decks cross the mold threshold on day eleven. Medical storage fails earlier because its exchangers require a tighter gradient.”

“I need the terminal bound.”

“You need a number that makes your choice look imposed by physics.”

The Crown analyst nearest Ada shifted in his chair. His slate carried the reserve table already filed with the Court: Latchkey, Morcant, Vesper Fold, Arden Coil, Sable, Near Orison. Six habitats. A combined population of 2.3 million. Four curves crossed their supply line before ninety days. Numbers should have disciplined the hearing. Instead, each question exposed another decision hidden inside the measure.

Ada kept her voice level. “If Meridian Mouth service stops in full on the present date, when does Latchkey first lose a critical system?”

“Day thirty-eight under rationing we have not approved.”

“Which system?”

“Lower-deck potable reclamation.”

“Can you bypass it?”

“For nine days, if no heat-sink jacket cracks and if the clinic gives up one sterile loop.”

“After that?”

“People begin leaving a deck that has nowhere to receive them.”

The recorder’s lamp advanced. Ada let the answer remain whole. A useful risk record did not soften a consequence, but neither did it pretend one consequence settled every other question.

Cal raised his eyes. “Architect Orsen, does Crown’s filed reserve table overstate Latchkey’s resilience?”

“It compresses different failures into one line. The catalyst quantity looks accurate within our reporting error.”

“Does the table understate it?”

“No.”

“Then four habitats have under ninety days of reserve?”

“Four have less than ninety days before ordinary replenishment becomes impossible. They do not all fail on day ninety. They fail by system, by deck, and by the choices made before then.”

Ada watched Cal receive the distinction without claiming it. He had spent years converting shortages into rulings. Ema had spent those years keeping pumps alive after rulings reached machinery. Whatever stood between them had learned to speak through exact units.

A court clerk opened the line for questions from provisional counsel. Cal asked only whether Latchkey had received any nonpublic Crown schedule showing an alternate service cadence. Ema said no. The word arrived without hesitation.

Ada’s analysts did not look at her. They had prepared the witness and knew the approved answer because there was no alternate schedule in the packet.

“Architect Orsen,” Ada said, “have you been asked by your father or any Callis officer to minimize the consequences of interruption?”

Ema’s expression changed by less than a breath. “No.”

“Have I asked you to exaggerate them?”

“You asked me for survivable bounds.”

“Did I direct your answer?”

“No.”

“Then give the Court the most favorable honest case.”

For the first time, Ema glanced toward something beyond her screen. Perhaps another engineer. Perhaps a pump alarm. “A short interruption with a guaranteed restart can be carried. We would empty industrial storage, stop new fabrication, and cut transit heat. A prolonged interruption without a credible restart date causes cascading failures that stores alone cannot repair.”

“How prolonged?”

“That depends on what remains in motion elsewhere. Anyone who gives you a single duration without a service plan offers false reassurance.”

Ada called the reserve worksheet onto the shared wall. “Run day thirty-eight.”

Ema studied the court copy. “Your worksheet assumes we isolate the lower decks at a fixed hour.”

“Change the assumption.”

“To what?”

“The sequence you would use.”

A pump started behind Ema. Compression tore the image into blocks, then restored it with her hand braced against the console. “First we lower habitat spin by four percent. That cuts circulation head and buys catalyst, but anyone with unstable bone repair needs transfer inward. Next we stop Foundry Two. Its coolant enters the residential reserve after six hours, provided the salts remain uncontaminated. On day nineteen we choose between seed storage and clinic sterilization. Your curve treats those as equal load.”

“They draw the same amount.”

“They do not carry the same future.”

The Crown table allowed Ada to select either branch. She chose seed storage. The clinic line crossed its lower limit on day twenty-six. She reset and preserved the clinic. Stored germination stock began warming on day twenty-eight.

“How much seed becomes unusable?” Cal asked.

“Between twelve and forty percent. We cannot know without opening the vaults, and opening them spends cold.”

Ada changed the restart field from guaranteed to uncertain. Red bands widened across the residential trunks. The worksheet had always contained uncertainty, but the filed summary displayed only median reserve. She had approved that choice because courts understood one line faster than a field of possible failures.

“Show the Court what happens if service returns on day sixty,” she said.

Ema did not touch her controls. “Which service?”

“One freight window at current rated mass.”

“Cargo?”

“Catalyst first.”

“What happens to the patients transferred during spin reduction?”

“They remain on Latchkey.”

“Then you have specified an impossible case. We cannot keep all of them through day sixty under reduced circulation.”

Cal leaned toward the wall. “How many require movement?”

“Current register gives one hundred twelve. That count changes with every surgery.”

Ada entered patient transport ahead of catalyst. The model pushed catalyst delivery nine days later and moved potable reclamation below safe output. She reduced freight packaging. The mass saved did not recover enough margin.

“Try a shared window with Arden Coil,” Ema said.

“Crown does not schedule shared clinical and catalyst loads through an unstable corridor.”

“You asked for the sequence I would use.”

Ada built it. Arden’s membranes occupied the thermal envelope left by the patient carriers. Catalyst canisters filled the remaining mass to tolerance. The Latchkey curve survived to day seventy-three, then narrowed into another uncertain band.

One analyst whispered, “That violates three handling standards.”

Ema heard him. “Standards written for ordinary transit. If you want an emergency answer, stop marking ordinary practice as a physical limit.”

Ada saved the run into the evidence record. “Does this prove Latchkey can survive a full interruption?”

“No. It proves our survival depends on coordination your filed table does not describe.”

“Does it make an immediate shutdown safe?”

“No.”

“Does any honest forecast make it safe?”

“Not one I have seen.”

The phrase landed where Ada intended and cut more widely than she wanted. Crown could prove danger. It could not use Ema to prove that only Crown’s preferred schedule could contain it. Worse for schedule exclusivity, the improvised run showed that shared control could stretch a failing reserve farther than six isolated projections admitted.

After the engineering link closed, the wall held six reserve curves and a small gray square where Ema’s image had been. Cal stayed seated while the recorder sealed the session copy.

“You knew she would not provide the terminal number,” he said.

“I knew she would not provide a dishonest one.”

“You called her because she would contradict your own table.”

“She corrected its interpretation. The quantities remain uncontested.”

His thumb pressed the paper schedule flat. “You wanted the risk admitted by a witness I could not dismiss as Crown.”

“I wanted the Court to hear from someone who operates a pump.”

“You also wanted me to hear my daughter describe decks she cannot save.”

Ada closed the Morcant curve before answering. “I did not choose your relationship.”

“No. You chose its evidentiary use.”

The analysts had begun packing their slates. Both moved with excessive care. Ada dismissed them, waited for the pressure door to close, and faced Cal across the empty evidence room.

“If I had called another architect, would the reserve become less real?”

“No.”

“Would you have trusted the estimate as much?”

His refusal to answer supplied its own quantity.

Ada gathered the filed sheets into order. “A full shutdown does not punish Crown alone. Morcant’s catalytic stores support eighteen days of full clinical demand after industrial diversion. Arden Coil can manufacture substitute membranes but lacks the Mouth windows to move them. Vesper Fold can feed itself for a season and cannot replace its heat-sink cores. These dependencies do not excuse what happened at Eiren. They limit which remedy remains lawful now.”

Cal stood. In the axial chambers his movement carried him farther than intended, and his hand closed around the rail before his shoulder reached the acoustic baffle. “You have stopped denying the old choice.”

“There is no useful denial left.”

“Then stop presenting present dependence as though it arrived without authors.”

He left with the court copy. Ada remained beside the blank witness square until the timing lamp signaled that the room had reverted to counsel access.

Present dependence had authors. Some had died. Some chaired Crown. Some had spent twelve years maintaining a schedule because every month of successful operation made change more dangerous. Ada had defended that maintenance. She could name the lives attached to it and still recognize the convenience of calling a designed trap an inherited one.

Her slate chimed with an internal priority request from Jalen Orr.

Jalen appeared from Crown’s schedule chamber rather than his office. Three nested route arcs glowed on the wall behind him, each line broken into service windows and thermal recovery intervals. Operators moved across the lower floor. None came close enough to enter the channel.

“You let Orsen qualify the reserve table,” he said.

“Ema Orsen qualified it. The court admitted the underlying quantities.”

“You gave Cal a public distinction between interruption and service design.”

“He already understands that distinction.”

“Understanding does not require us to put it in the record.”

Ada looked at the inner arc. Two Mouth windows had moved since morning, both minor freight corrections. “The record would have failed an adversarial engineering review without her testimony.”

“Then use an engineer who knows which fact needs defending.”

“She defended the fact.”

Jalen leaned toward his camera. At sixty-eight, he had learned to make quiet attention feel like an escalation. “Four habitats face reserve exhaustion inside ninety days. That statement needs no ornament.”

“It needs failure sequences. Otherwise opposing counsel will show one store lasting ninety-one days and call the entire projection inflated.”

“You sound eager to help them.”

“I am preserving the part of our argument that survives examination.”

A route operator crossed behind him with a sealed wafer. Jalen waited until the operator passed. “The Court confirmed personhood for the duration. It did not acquire authority over the Mouth schedule. Keep remedy outside the standing case.”

“The merits hearing will join them whether we approve or not.”

“Then we make the joining look dangerous.”

“It is dangerous.”

His gaze narrowed. “You have become difficult to reassure.”

“I was not hired for reassurance.”

Jalen ended the link without dismissal. The six curves returned to Ada’s wall. At the bottom of each filed chart, a Crown model identifier linked reserve consumption to schedule assumptions. Ada had reviewed those identifiers for twelve years. This set began with MSX, the current exclusivity model, followed by habitat and revision number.

Latchkey’s identifier did not.

The difference occupied two characters near the line’s checksum: MX-ALT/4. Someone had copied a reserve output from another model family into the court table, then normalized its heading to the approved schedule. Ema’s testimony had forced Ada to inspect the failure layers. Without that inspection, the altered identifier would have remained beneath a chart everyone considered settled.

Ada touched the line. Counsel access permitted her to inspect provenance, not to open Crown operational systems from a court terminal. The model reference offered only an origin date, a schedule division, and a retention class marked superseded before executive review.

Before executive review. Not rejected. Not unsafe. A schedule model had existed long enough to generate habitat reserves and had then disappeared before anyone with decision authority could be said to know it.

She copied the retention string to paper. Digital notes from the evidence room synchronized with Crown compliance. Paper did not, which explained why Callis still kept it.

The model family code bothered her more than the concealment marker. Crown used ALT for changes inside an exclusive schedule: bell substitution, delayed freight, temporary load shedding. No alternative could relinquish exclusivity without a different legal class and executive approval. Yet MX referred to Mouth transition architecture, a category Ada had encountered only twice, both times during acquisitions that changed who controlled a bell.

Someone had modeled a slower transition and hidden it beneath an ordinary alternate-run label.

She did not have the model. She had an index entry pointing toward its burial.

Ada folded the paper once and slid it beneath the court schedule. The act felt theatrical, so she unfolded it and wrote the identifier into the margin of Ema’s admitted diagram instead. Evidence rooms scanned margins at closure, but counsel working copies left under seal. She needed a path that neither vanished into Crown compliance nor entered the public record before she understood what it carried.

The pressure door opened. One of her analysts returned for a forgotten seal stylus.

Ada covered the margin with her hand.

“Director?”

“Take the stylus.”

He retrieved it from beside the recorder and glanced at the six curves. “Do you want the Latchkey qualification added to tomorrow’s risk summary?”

“Yes. Separate catalyst exhaustion from first critical-system loss. Do the same for all six habitats.”

“That will widen the ranges.”

“It will make them defensible.”

After he left, Ada compared the alternate model’s origin date to the Crown filing calendar. The run had begun eleven months earlier, three days after a mass engineer submitted a warning about cumulative Mouth shear. Ada remembered the warning. Her office had classified it as speculative because no operational remedy accompanied it.

A remedy may have existed in another division while she signed the classification.

She opened Crown’s retention directory through the limited discovery window. Searching the full identifier would create an audit alert. She instead searched the public prefix, then narrowed by date, authoring division, and output type. Twenty-one records appeared. Nineteen belonged to ordinary freight alternates. One held no title and no accessible summary. The last had been converted into a legal-risk memorandum stating that any departure from schedule exclusivity would produce unacceptable liability.

The untitled record carried the same checksum family as MX-ALT/4.

Ada requested its metadata under pending-litigation authority. The directory asked whether the request concerned a filed exhibit, anticipated evidence, or privileged defense preparation. Every answer notified a different Crown office. She chose anticipated evidence. Jalen’s staff would see the request at the next compliance sweep, perhaps in six hours.

Access opened on a single page.

MODEL INDEX: MOUTH TRANSITION SERIES, RUNS 1–4.

Below it, four rows listed creation dates, computational mass, scenario owners, and disposition. Run 1 had failed stability bounds. Run 2 had been stopped for patient-transfer assumptions. Run 3 remained under technical review. Run 4 had completed, then moved to restricted schedule strategy by chair authority.

No numerical outputs appeared. No bell assignments. No duration. The index proved work, not feasibility. It also proved that Jalen’s instruction to keep remedy outside the case protected more than a legal boundary.

Ada read the access footer twice. Export prohibited. Screen capture logged. Local print unavailable.

She took the timing lamp from the evidence table and set it beside her slate. The lamp could certify spoken counsel notes, but any recording entered Callis custody. She did not need the content admitted yet. She needed to keep it from disappearing.

Ada memorized the four run dates, then closed the page. She reopened it to test herself. Dates correct. Scenario owners correct. Run 4 disposition exact. She wrote them in longhand across the reverse of the reserve table, using pump annotations between each field so an automated scan would not reconstruct the index as a Crown record.

That concealment implicated her as soon as the ink dried. Twelve years spent arguing that operational fear justified controlled disclosure had left her skilled at the mechanics of suppression. Intent did not alter the action. Only what she did next might alter its use.

The directory chimed. A compliance marker had attached to her metadata request sooner than expected.

Ada closed every reserve curve except Latchkey’s. On Ema’s diagram, lower-deck reclamation branched from the same trunk that served the clinic. One valve choice gave nine days to one system by taking sterility from another. The engineering did not offer innocence. It offered a map of who would pay.

She sealed the working copy under her personal litigation key and entered a retrieval request for the untitled run’s physical audit wafer. The system warned that chair approval would be required.

“Request it,” Ada said.

The directory accepted her voice and sent the notice to Jalen.

Before the confirmation could be recalled, she tore the handwritten model index from the reserve table, folded it around Ema Orsen’s pump diagram, and placed both inside the court’s pending-evidence envelope. The envelope’s physical seal closed beneath her thumb. Crown still possessed the model. Ada had stolen the fact that it existed.

Chapter 15 — The Cost of Calling

Nia lowered the ninth heat bloom into alignment and watched the public estimate lose half its value.

The thermal map hung above choir bell C-9 as a stack of translucent sections. Each section carried a pale oval where a destructive read had driven heat through the auric glass. Osei had numbered the blooms by depth rather than date. Their sequence could not be known from temperature alone. Nia had spent two days matching each scar to old maintenance tolerances, Crown activation fragments, and the changes in Iven’s responses.

The result on her slate read 2.14.

Osei looked past her shoulder. “Run the uncertainty again.”

“I did.”

“With the outer-lamination loss at the high bound?”

“That gives 1.96.”

“Low bound?”

“2.27.”

He rested both hands on the cradle rail. C-9 lay beneath them in its preservation field, smoked amber under the vault lamps. The bell held no visible movement. Its phase trace pulsed against the passive meter with long rests between each rise.

The public filing still showed 4.6 returns. That figure assumed an intact interior, then subtracted the cost of the Callis activations. It treated the nine blooms as superficial calibration damage because Crown’s sealed transfer record had classified them that way. Nia had once accepted the classification. She had recognized her own instrument signature in the seventh scar and understood that acceptance could not survive another unexamined number.

“Use 2.1 returns,” she said.

Osei did not enter it. “The corrected mean gives 2.14.”

“Returns do not support hundredths at this damage level. Two point one states what we know without pretending we know more.”

“And the lower bound?”

“Put it in the medical note.”

“The Court will quote the headline.”

“Yes.”

“So will Crown.”

“Yes.”

Osei keyed the number. The reserve display contracted. A band that had once held four ordinary activations and a margin now held two, perhaps less if distress widened Iven’s recall. The word return made coherence sound renewable. Nothing in C-9 came back after use.

Nia placed the original estimate beside the correction. “Now price the merits protocol.”

They had costed the hearing before, using the public reserve and a standard signal-person load: ninety minutes of structured testimony, visual exhibits, route recall, two recesses, and controlled cross-examination. The protocol projected 0.62 return. That calculation assumed coherent retrieval and stable recognition. Iven had neither.

Osei entered the latest route-response delay. He added the stress spike from the standing hearing, then the deeper retrieval needed to distinguish encoded deposition from present testimony. The number climbed to 0.68.

“Add face recognition,” Nia said.

“That is preparation, not merits.”

“Crown’s witness list includes identity foundation. They will put Lio Senn’s record to him.”

Osei’s fingers stopped above the slate. “Why?”

“To test whether he can connect the route memory to a person.”

“They know faces are expensive.”

“They will call it reliability.”

He entered the task. The upper projection reached 0.74.

Nia set the filed hearing cost beside the revised reserve. About 0.7 return. Roughly one third of what remained. If questioning broadened into autobiographical recall, the fraction could rise. If the body destabilized, stopping it would preserve less than stopping before the damage began.

She did not trust one calculation merely because it condemned the earlier one. “Blind the bloom labels.”

Osei copied the thermal sections into a clean workspace and removed every date, instrument code, and Crown entry reference. He left only heat depth, affected glass volume, local stress orientation, and the passive response measured that morning. Nia turned away while he changed the section order.

“Ready.”

The first scar had spread along a manufacturing seam. Nia classified it as a shallow read with uncertain thermal loss. The second penetrated three phase layers and curved toward the route packet. She assigned a higher cost. By the fifth section, the shape in the glass had begun to repeat: narrow access near the encoded statement, wide bloom where the process searched for context, then a sharp edge where someone stopped the activation before coherent descent.

“Show me the public Callis reads separately,” she said.

Osei marked two sections with a gray border. Their heat paths remained compact because Nia had used current descent controls and limited sensory load. Neither resembled the older damage.

“Crown drove the prior bodies harder,” he said.

“The marks support broader access. They do not tell us why.”

“What benign reason needs nine attempts?”

“Keep motive out of the reserve note.”

“It keeps finding a way in.”

Nia classified the remaining scars, then let the slate restore sequence. Her blind estimate reached 2.08 returns. The difference from the first calculation fell within measurement error.

Osei rotated the seventh section. Her old calibration cadence appeared along its edge as four equal warming points and one longer descent mark. Seven years earlier, she had accepted a maintenance order describing an inert route model that needed phase-response calibration before transfer. She remembered the tool’s drag. She remembered the Crown supervisor asking her to repeat the final pulse because the model had failed to settle. No vocal body had occupied the service room. Nothing in the task had looked like respeaking from the gantry.

Yet the bloom widened after her pulse. Someone had used her calibration to open a deeper path elsewhere.

“Subtract my event as maintenance only,” she said.

Osei stared at her. “Why?”

“Challenge the conclusion. Assume my pulse spent no conscious coherence and carried only thermal cost.”

He changed the classification. The reserve rose to 2.23.

“Now remove the shallowest unknown scar.”

“On what basis?”

“Assume manufacturing damage.”

The result reached 2.31.

“Still nowhere near 4.6,” he said.

“No.”

“Then stop trying to rescue their number.”

“I am trying to learn whether an unfriendly examiner can.” Nia restored both events. “Export all three cases. Supported estimate, conservative damage estimate, and lowest plausible reserve.”

A medical alert appeared beside the hearing protocol. The vocal body team had priced a second factor that the phase model omitted: cumulative tissue strain. Iven’s reconstructed pattern might retain coherence while the neutral body lost the ability to carry it safely. His prior glucose response had slowed. Breath support now engaged three seconds earlier during descent. Another long hearing could end from tissue failure before phase exhaustion, leaving testimony interrupted without preserving the expected reserve.

Nia opened the body report. “Add a motor-loss threshold to the stop protocol.”

“There is no patient-controlled stop protocol yet.”

“Then cost one.”

Osei built a branch using existing medical cutoff controls. Spoken command required less motor effort but could be disputed if speech broke. A hand signal offered independent confirmation but demanded stable proprioception. The safest design used both and permitted either to begin descent.

“You are designing consent before we ask him,” Osei said.

“I am identifying whether consent can be withdrawn. Without that, asking him to proceed offers only entry.”

The branch reduced expected hearing cost by eight hundredths if the stop came at the first overload marker. It also reduced useful testimony time.

“Crown will oppose it,” Osei said.

“They can oppose after he knows the price.”

Osei left the two estimates side by side: 2.1 returns and a hearing centered near 0.7. “Send the correction to Cal.”

“Not yet.”

“He set the hearing schedule from the wrong number.”

“Iven receives it first.”

“We need authorization to bring him up.”

“His medical choices belong to him now.”

“That means he can authorize. It does not let us ask an inactive person.”

Nia removed the slate from the shared mount. “The current rest order includes one low-load medical interval before the merits hearing.”

“Tomorrow morning.”

“We use it now.”

Osei looked toward the vault door. A court seal covered the activation console, its physical filament threaded through the send control. Breaking it without a custodian would end her access before she could explain the number. “Cal will come if we call.”

“Then call him. Do not send the calculation.”

“He will ask why.”

“Tell him Iven needs to receive a material medical correction before counsel.”

Osei’s jaw tightened. “You are separating the patient from the case as though the Court has ever done that.”

“For the next ten minutes, it will.”

Cal arrived in seven. He carried the temporary-personhood order on a sealed wafer and read Nia’s request while standing outside the vault. The pressure door remained closed between them.

“You recalculated the reserve,” he said through the intercom.

Nia held the slate facedown. “I have information that changes his consent.”

“Does it affect the hearing?”

“Yes.”

“Then counsel must know.”

“He must know before counsel decides what to ask him.”

“I am his counsel.”

“You are also the examiner who scheduled the hearing.”

Cal looked through the door’s narrow glass at C-9. “That conflict existed yesterday.”

“The quantity did not.”

“How large a correction?”

“Large enough that hearing it after you would make his choice look managed.”

The intercom carried the low fan noise from his side. Nia watched him weigh reversible order against damage already accumulating in delay. He finally pressed the wafer into the seal reader.

“I will authorize the medical interval,” he said. “I remain present as counsel.”

“You remain outside the vocal-body room until Iven invites you.”

“That exceeds your authority.”

“It protects the choice you claim to represent.”

Cal’s face changed slightly. Not concession. Recognition. “Record my objection.”

Osei raised the custody lamp. “Already running.”

Cal broke the seal.

The vocal body opened its eyes on the sixth breath.

Nia kept one hand around the saline regulator and the other against the warm cuff at Iven’s wrist. The body had lost weight since the last activation. Its tendons showed beneath pale skin, and the pulse arrived too quickly for the low metabolic setting. Linen restraints lay loose across the forearms. Iven had asked that they remain unfastened unless his movements threatened the phase leads.

His gaze found the ceiling, the glucose cup, then Nia. Recognition took two breaths longer than before.

“You moved the lamp,” he said.

The timing lamp stood on the opposite shelf. During his prior interval it had rested near the door.

“Yes.”

“Why?”

“So you can see the reserve display without turning your head.”

“Generous.” His voice rasped. “Or ominous.”

Nia raised the bed by five degrees. “Can you state your name?”

“Iven Ko.”

“Place?”

“Callis. Vocal-body room three.”

“Last event you remember?”

He closed his eyes. The pulse in his wrist rose beneath her fingers. “A judge gave me medical choices that end when I stop being able to make them.”

“Do you remember the judge’s name?”

“Sena Pell spoke. Cal Orsen argued. I do not know which answer you want.”

“Either establishes sequence.”

“Then both cost less than choosing.”

Nia held the glucose cup to his mouth. He swallowed twice and turned away.

“I recalculated your remaining coherence,” she said.

His fingers shifted under the cuff. “Before the Court?”

“Yes.”

“Does Cal know the number?”

“No.”

That answer steadied him more than the glucose had. He looked toward the observation glass, which remained opaque. “Tell me.”

“The public estimate assumed 4.6 returns. It classified nine interior heat blooms as calibration damage. We matched those blooms to destructive access. The best supported estimate now gives 2.1 returns. The plausible range extends below two.”

Iven watched her mouth as though route meaning might arrive through its movement before the words settled. “How much did this call use?”

“Expected cost, less than five hundredths. Your stress response may raise it.”

“Then stop measuring my stress as if the answer can improve it.”

Nia released his wrist. The cuff continued sending pulse data, but her hand no longer made the measurement feel personal. “You can end this interval at any time.”

“What does the hearing cost?”

“A standard merits protocol now projects about 0.7 return.”

He breathed in through his nose. “One third.”

“Roughly one third of the corrected estimate. More if recall broadens. Less if questioning stays narrow and you stop before overload.”

“And what did Crown take?”

“We cannot assign exact cost to each prior read. Together, the evidence supports a loss of at least half the reserve reflected in the public filing.”

“I asked what they took.”

“So did I.” Nia kept her voice even. “The map shows consumption. It does not show which memory left with each use.”

His gaze moved away from her and stopped on the blank wall. The room offered no window. During the standing hearing he had chosen a view of Callis over a reconstruction of Eiren because the choice belonged to the present. Nia had wondered whether that preference marked recovery. Now it looked like conservation. He had selected something that demanded no damaged face.

“Bring Cal in,” Iven said.

“Do you want the reserve display first?”

“No. Numbers behave when I am not looking at them.”

Nia opened the observation glass. Cal stood beyond it with his hand on the rail. She unlocked the room door, but she did not move from beside the bed.

Cal entered carrying no files. “Iven.”

“Did you know?”

“I knew Nia had a material correction. I did not know its size.”

“Would you have brought me up if she sent it first?”

“Yes.”

“For the medical interval or for preparation?”

Cal took the chair at the foot of the bed. He did not answer quickly. “Both, if you agreed after hearing the number.”

Iven gave a dry sound that did not become a laugh. “Agreement after counsel explains why testimony matters.”

“I would explain what the hearing can decide.”

“You would be good at it.”

“That does not make the explanation coercive.”

“No. The remaining time does.”

Nia checked the phase-load bar. It had reached sixty-two percent of the interval limit. Iven noticed her glance.

“How much to decide?” he asked.

“Decision itself has no fixed cost. Remaining awake does.”

“Then give me the choices without argument.”

Cal looked at Nia. She nodded once.

“First,” he said, “we postpone while challenging Crown’s other records. Delay also carries preservation risk. Second, we proceed with the scheduled merits hearing and seek strict limits on scope. Third, you decline testimony. Existing telemetry and physical evidence remain, but the direct allegation may lose force.”

“Fourth,” Iven said. “You call me later because the case becomes difficult.”

“Only with your consent while you hold standing.”

“And after?”

Existing law gave Cal no answer that could survive Iven’s absence. His eyes lowered to the unfastened linen across Iven’s arm.

“The Court has not decided that,” he said.

“Then those are not four choices. They are three choices followed by someone else’s.”

The phase bar reached sixty-seven percent. Nia reduced the visual feed and warmed the saline by one degree. Iven’s breathing eased.

Cal leaned forward. “You do not owe the Court your destruction.”

“Does the Court know that?”

“I do.”

“You are not the whole Court.”

“No.”

Iven turned to Nia. “Can you make the hearing stop when I say?”

“The activation can be stepped down within twelve seconds if the leads remain stable. A sudden cutoff risks shear.”

“Not can the machine stop. Can I stop the people?”

Cal answered. “I can ask for an order limiting examination.”

“That means a judge chooses whether my limit counts.”

“Yes.”

“Then ask for something else.”

Nia understood before Cal did. “A stopping point controlled from the bed.”

Iven’s eyes returned to her. “Not the bed. Me.”

She pulled the activation protocol onto the wall. The current design assigned stop authority to the supervising resonantist after a judicial recess command. Iven could request recess, but the body did not control the phase descent. Nia could insert a patient command into the medical cutoff layer. Any spoken phrase risked accidental activation during testimony. A deliberate hand sequence could fail if motor control deteriorated.

“You need two paths,” she said. “A spoken command and a physical one.”

“What words?”

“Your choice.”

“Stop now.”

Cal shook his head. “That could occur in quoted testimony.”

“Then the full sentence.” Iven looked at him. “This examination stops now.”

Nia entered it into the local protocol but did not authorize the change. “Physical command?”

Iven lifted his right hand. Tremor moved through the fingers. He pressed thumb to the first joint of his middle finger, held for two breaths, and released.

“That movement appears nowhere in the route checks,” Nia said. “We can use it.”

Cal read the proposed control. “Crown will argue that counsel or medical staff could coach either signal.”

“Then record the choice now,” Iven said.

The timing lamp stood within his view. Osei activated its identity thread from the console. Nia stated the corrected reserve, hearing projection, and control design. Cal stated that Iven had received the information before counsel knew the quantity. The lamp asked Iven to confirm.

He looked tired enough that the room seemed to move around his effort to remain still.

“I authorize one final evidentiary activation,” he said. “It proceeds only if my stopping point controls the descent. Spoken or physical. No examiner gets to spend the interval after I withdraw it.”

Cal asked, “Do you authorize preparation before that activation?”

“No rehearsal that enters broad recall. Documents may be described to me while I am at medical baseline. I choose whether to see them.”

“Do you understand that stopping may leave cross-examination unfinished?”

“Yes.”

“That may reduce the force of your testimony.”

“It may.”

Cal’s next question waited behind his teeth. Nia saw him abandon it.

The phase-load bar crossed seventy percent.

“Iven,” she said, “this interval has reached its safe descent range.”

He pressed thumb to finger and held.

Nia acknowledged the physical command. Osei began the twelve-second taper. Warm saline moved through the body cuff. The pulse slowed first, then the eyes lost focus.

Before breath support took over, Iven looked at the protocol on the wall. “Make them sign it.”

“I will,” Cal said.

“No.” His voice had thinned to air. “Make them sign before they hear me.”

The body released its next breath and did not draw another on its own. Nia watched the phase trace settle into C-9. Cal remained at the foot of the bed.

On the wall, the new control waited for three signatures: medical custodian, counsel, and presiding justice. Nia placed her hand against the first field. Her activation license made the instrument accept her identity.

She signed before anyone could ask whether she would still hold that license at the hearing.

Chapter 16 — Archive of Complicity

The license reader rejected Nia’s thumb before she could submit Iven’s stopping protocol.

A red bar crossed the seal field. ACTIVATION AUTHORITY UNDER AUTOMATED REVIEW. SOURCE ORDER CONFLICT. The reader released the protocol wafer but kept a gray copy of its signature trace. Nia tried no second time. Repeated attempts could turn a contested credential into an access violation.

Osei stood beside the vault console with the custody lamp looped around his wrist. “That review did not exist an hour ago.”

“It did. We gave it something to match.”

“The corrected reserve?”

“My signature.” Nia withdrew her thumb and studied the reader’s dull contact plate. “The protocol linked my current license to the instrument cadence in bloom seven.”

He looked toward C-9. The preservation field drew a faint amber line along the bell’s service lip. “Crown’s old transfer file called that work external calibration.”

“So did I.”

“Then why flag it now?”

“Because the reader found an activation-order family beneath the maintenance number.”

Nia opened the conflict notice without dismissing the red bar. A credential system preserved its first result more reliably than any screen opened afterward. The notice identified a seven-year-old work order: CROWN ROUTE MODEL CONDITIONING / C9-ER-204 / SERVICE TASK 7C. Her completion mark sat below three remote authorization stubs. The visible order described inert geometry and prohibited semantic access. A concealed dependency field pointed to a retention ledger the Callis Archive had never received.

Osei read the stub names. “They are all closed offices.”

“Offices close. Keys migrate.”

“Can we follow them?”

“Not from an activation console.” She sealed Iven’s protocol in a passive sleeve. “We use the acquisition terminal and record every query.”

“The hearing begins in two days.”

“Then we do not spend today pretending my authority remains intact.”

They left C-9 under the court field. At the vault door, Nia’s license still opened preservation access. It would permit her to regulate temperature, inspect seals, and respond to a physical fault. It would not permit a pulse. The distinction felt accurate enough to hurt.

The acquisition terminal occupied a narrow room beneath mass ring two. Its keys had worn smooth under decades of gloved hands. Nia seated the custody lamp where it could see the screen, the door, and both technicians. Osei pulled the original Crown transfer packet from offline storage.

Eleven public lines appeared, the same ones they had read on the first day. Mixed mass transfer. No recoverable emergency telemetry. Outer phase glass cleared under salvage protocol. Interior access prohibited. Origin blank.

Nia opened the service-task index and entered 7C.

The terminal returned no record.

“Search the parent family,” she said.

Osei removed the final character. Seven tasks appeared. Six concerned inert route models held at Crown depots. The seventh showed no title, only her license number and a completion date.

“Order them by creation, not completion.”

The blank task moved from last to first.

Someone had created it before the six harmless jobs, then used the later orders to define its apparent family. A reviewer searching by number would see a routine series. A reviewer asking when the series began would find the concealed dependency.

Osei bent close to the checksum. “Creation predates transfer by twelve days.”

“And my work?”

“Nine days before transfer.”

Nia remembered a Crown supervisor telling her the bell could not enter Callis custody until its model layer answered a standard cadence.
