He had brought C-9 to a rented service cradle outside the archive ring. She had not seen a vocal-body room, medical feed, or active phase channel. Her probe had connected through a Crown isolation case. Every indicator available to her showed an inert geometric model.

Every indicator had belonged to Crown.

“Open my instrument log,” she said.

The terminal warned that archived calibration records could contain protected worker data. Nia entered her consent. Four pulses and one long descent mark appeared, exactly as the seventh bloom carried them.

Osei overlaid the thermal map. “Your last pulse ends here.” He pointed to a narrow heat edge. “The deeper bloom starts forty milliseconds later.”

“Too fast for a separate manual send.”

“They armed a linked process.”

“Or my cadence crossed a trigger threshold.”

“Which means?”

Nia expanded the response channels. Her instrument had registered a clean mirror from the outer layer. Under it lay six microseconds of discarded phase motion, labeled housing noise. She restored the raw values. The line contained breath regularity, motor load, and an autonomic spike.

A body had been active somewhere beyond the isolation case.

Osei stepped back from the terminal. “You heard him.”

“No audio reached my station.”

“Your instrument did.”

“State only what the values support.” Nia magnified the autonomic channel. “A biologically loaded response shared the circuit. Identity remains unproved.”

“The bell identity proves it.”

“The bell links the process to C-9. The thermal sequence links it to one of nine destructive events. We still have to establish which event.”

Osei opened the sealed Crown activation fragments admitted after the ledger leak. Dates appeared only for three entries. The others used relative sequence numbers. Return 7 listed outer-cadence conditioning by contracted Callis resonantist. Return 8 began eleven months later. Return 6 ended twenty-three months earlier.

Nia’s completion date fell between them.

“The seventh recall,” Osei said.

She read the ledger line again. It did not name her. It did not need to. The instrument code, license trace, and date closed the path. Crown had built a remote activation behind an inert maintenance interface. Nia had supplied the cadence that opened or stabilized it. Her lack of informed consent changed her culpability. It did not change what the pulse had done.

“What did Return 7 seek?” she asked.

Osei opened the clipped purpose field. “Recognition stability under neutral technician contact.”

The room’s fan vibration traveled through the terminal keys into Nia’s fingertips. Iven had recognized her calibration cadence during his first Callis embodiment. She had treated that recognition as impossible, then as evidence against Crown. It also pointed toward an encounter his damaged continuity retained in the body even after context disappeared.

“They used me as an unfamiliar stimulus,” she said.

“You were not in the body room.”

“They needed a cadence outside Crown’s regular staff. Something not encoded in the Eiren packet and not repeated across their other tests.”

“Then his recognition of you helped prove agency.”

“After it helped damage him.”

Osei’s hand hovered near the custody lamp. “Do we call Cal?”

“We finish the reconstruction first.”

“Nia.”

“If I confess before separating the supported chain from inference, Crown will call the whole result guilt-driven.”

“You did not know.”

“Put that in the chain where the record supports it.”

They worked for another three hours. Nia retrieved the task ontology in force seven years earlier. It defined route model as a non-conscious predictive state and required a separate red warning for any biologically loaded process. Her order contained no warning. Osei found the rented cradle’s network diagram in a freight-insurance attachment. The isolation case had carried a second line concealed inside its shielding return. That line led to Crown medical systems.

Nia traced custody from the original completion mark through acquisition. The blank task had been bundled into six inert calibrations, exported under a family checksum, and stripped of its dependency field when Callis accepted C-9. A Callis intake reviewer had verified the family total but not the creation sequence. Nia had signed only her task, not the bundle. The archive had nevertheless accepted Crown’s classification because one of its own senior resonantists appeared in the record.

Her signature had not merely performed work. It had laundered the bell’s condition for transfer.

Nia pulled the insurance diagram down to the terminal’s fabrication bench. “We need to prove the concealed line could carry the response, not only that a line existed.”

“We cannot rebuild Crown’s case from a freight drawing.”

“We can test the topology.”

Osei fetched two passive phase couplers, an obsolete shielding sleeve, and a route-model resistor from the training cabinet. None could touch C-9. Their bench reconstruction would use a calibration simulator that carried no stored person and no destructive state. Nia listed every substitution for the custody lamp before either of them connected a lead.

The freight diagram showed an isolation case with one approved return path. Its shielding braid should have drained stray phase motion to the cradle frame. Crown’s hidden medical line ran inside that braid, sharing its ground for eleven centimeters before separating beneath the case hinge.

Osei cut a matching length of training braid. “If they carried a live response beside the drain, your instrument should have seen noise before the trigger.”

“It did. Six microseconds labeled housing noise.”

“That label came from your tool.”

“From its ontology. Crown selected the ontology when they issued the task.”

They connected the harmless simulator beyond the sleeve. Osei configured it to send a pulse shaped like elevated breath load, not a neural trace. Nia stood back from the controls. Her license remained under review, and even a training pulse would complicate the record if she sent it herself.

“External cadence ready,” he said.

“Use four equal points and one long descent.”

“That repeats your signature.”

“It tests the mechanism.”

Osei sent the sequence. The approved return channel showed a clean mirror. Beneath it, the instrument discarded a narrow motion band as housing noise. When he restored the raw view, the simulated breath pulse appeared inside the discarded band.

“Again with the hidden line removed,” Nia said.

He disconnected it at the hinge. The next cadence produced no buried response.

“Reverse the shared ground.”

“Why?”

“An unfriendly review will argue accidental coupling.”

Osei reversed the braid connection. A response appeared, but its polarity inverted and its delay widened beyond a hundred milliseconds. Crown’s line in the freight diagram preserved polarity and matched the forty-millisecond edge in bloom seven.

Nia wrote the distinction into the reconstruction. “The geometry supports intentional carriage. Do not write that it proves intent to harm.”

“It proves someone built a path.”

“Yes.”

“For a process the order told you did not exist.”

“Yes.”

He shut down the simulator. “How many people had to know?”

“The case designer. Whoever configured the medical side. Whoever bound my order to the trigger.”

“And the supervisor in the room.”

“Likely. We do not have his private display.”

Osei unwound the training braid and laid every part in a separate tray. “You keep narrowing the statement until no person fits inside it.”

Nia sealed the simulator log. “I am narrowing it until the people who do fit cannot escape through an exaggeration.”

He looked at the old order on the screen. “Where do you fit?”

She touched no part of the bench. “At the external cadence. At the unrecorded objection I should have made. At the signature Callis relied on afterward.”

The reconstruction added one more dated operation to the chain: a present, non-destructive bench test showing how Crown’s concealed medical line had passed a biologically loaded response beneath the tool’s noise filter.

At the end, Osei built a single provenance path on the wall. No rhetoric. No motive. Eight dated operations connected Nia’s maintenance order to the hidden medical line and from there to Return 7.

“Now Cal,” she said.

Cal read the reconstruction without touching it.

They met in a record chamber beside the Court’s axial hall. Paper-thin acoustic baffles separated the central table from three observation stations. Ada occupied one station by Crown link. She had joined after Cal notified all parties that a custodian credential conflict might affect the merits hearing. Sena Pell had declined to attend until the record established whether judicial action was required.

Cal reached the final provenance line. “Did any document tell you a conscious process might answer your calibration?”

“No.”

“Did you see a vocal body?”

“No.”

“Did you know Crown had performed prior access to C-9?”

“No.”

Ada spoke from the wall. “Those answers concern her knowledge then. Ask what she suspected.”

Cal looked at Nia. “Did any part of the assignment seem irregular?”

“Yes. Crown required its isolation case to remain closed. The supervisor refused my request to inspect the return channel. He said opening it would invalidate density certification.”

“Was that explanation plausible?”

“Yes.”

“Was it required by ordinary practice?”

“No. A Callis custodian could have inspected under shared seal. I accepted Crown’s refusal because the order classified the layer as inert and because transfer depended on timely completion.”

“Did you record an objection?”

“No.”

Osei sat behind her with the custody lamp. He did not move.

Cal asked, “What consequence followed from your pulse?”

“The instrument record supports a linked biologically loaded response forty milliseconds after my final cadence. The heat map places the resulting damage at bloom seven. The Crown ledger identifies contracted Callis calibration during Return 7. Taken together, those records establish my technical participation in the seventh recall.”

Ada’s voice sharpened. “Participation can mean any contribution. Did you initiate respeaking?”

“I initiated the external sequence that enabled or stabilized it.”

“Did you authorize semantic access?”

“No.”

“Did you intend destructive access?”

“No.”

“Then say plainly that Crown deceived you.”

Nia looked at Ada’s compressed image. “Crown concealed the active process and misrepresented the order. Callis failed to require an independent return-path inspection. I accepted a closed interface because the paperwork gave me a lawful reason to do so.”

“You are distributing blame to the archive.”

“I am describing the control failure.”

“That description threatens the custody of every record you handled after transfer.”

“Yes.”

Cal’s attention settled on her. “You understand that this admission may compromise C-9’s chain?”

“The chain already carries the compromise. Silence would only keep the Court from measuring it.”

“Why disclose now?”

“My license reader matched the current protocol to the old cadence.”

“Would you have disclosed without the automated review?”

Nia felt Osei’s presence behind her, the lamp preserving the time she took. “I had already disclosed the matching signature and the nine blooms. I had not reconstructed the hidden medical line. Without today’s conflict, I might have continued treating my task as calibration adjacent to the recall rather than part of it.”

“Is that a yes?” Ada asked.

“No. I cannot claim I would have reached the same conclusion today without the reader.”

The timing lamp marked the answer. It would harm her. That gave it evidentiary value rather than moral purity.

Cal turned to Ada. “Does Crown contest the authenticity of its ledger fragment?”

“Crown contests the inference that the biologically loaded response belonged to Iven Ko.”

“Will Crown produce the sealed Return 7 medical record?”

“I do not hold authority to waive it.”

“Then the Court will weigh your refusal with the physical match.”

Ada’s mouth tightened. She said nothing further.

Cal opened Iven’s stopping protocol beside the reconstruction. Nia’s signature showed a red review mark. “A merits activation requires unchallenged technical authority. I cannot leave this credential active while its holder serves as both operator and implicated witness.”

Nia placed her thumb on the table, away from every reader. “I agree.”

“Agreement does not decide discipline.”

“I know.”

“Pending archive review, I suspend your activation license. Effective now. You may perform passive preservation under court supervision. You may not send a pulse, authorize embodiment, control descent, or sign an activation protocol.”

The operative words entered the chamber seal. Nia had expected relief and felt none. Her license had permitted Crown to trust her seven years earlier. It had permitted Iven to trust her yesterday. The same credential connected both acts while consent separated them.

Osei rose. “The stopping protocol needs another medical custodian.”

“It will receive one,” Cal said.

“Iven authorized it after Nia explained the reserve. He chose her to implement it.”

“He chose the control. The Court can appoint an independent operator.”

Nia spoke before Osei could argue. “Do that.”

Cal studied her. “There is a separate question. Iven has repeatedly identified you as the person he wants present for technical explanation. His recorded medical preference names you, not merely a licensed office.”

Ada said, “An implicated custodian cannot remain inside the advisory chain.”

“A license suspension removes operational authority,” Cal replied. “It does not erase a represented person’s choice of adviser.”

“Her advice may shape testimony about the very activation she helped perform.”

“Then examination may expose that conflict.”

Cal turned the protocol wafer over. “I will retain Nia Daro as Iven Ko’s chosen technical adviser, subject to full disclosure and no instrument control. A separate court resonantist will execute any authorized activation. This ruling preserves his present preference without pre-deciding archive discipline.”

Nia had not asked to stay. Asking would have made the role sound like hers to claim. Iven’s choice created the only reason she could remain after the license ended.

Cal pressed his seal to the suspension order. Her wrist credential cooled as its active field shut down.

“The confession breaks any claim to a clean chain of custody,” he said. “It also explains the break before Crown can expose it piecemeal. The merits record will carry both facts.”

Ada’s link went dark without farewell.

Outside the chamber, Nia tested her wrist against a general archive door. It opened. At the resonance-vault line, the instrument gate stayed closed until Osei placed his junior credential beside hers. His key permitted escorted preservation access, nothing more.

They entered together.

C-9 rested under the same amber field. The red review bar had disappeared from the activation console because the console no longer recognized Nia as someone entitled to submit a command. Iven’s stopping protocol remained in its passive sleeve, her invalidated signature visible beneath the plastic.

Osei reached for the field-temperature control, then stopped. “You should tell me the adjustment.”

Nia read the gradient. Ring vibration had raised the outer seam by two hundredths of a degree. Before suspension, she would have corrected it herself.

“Lower the cradle set point by one tenth,” she said. “Wait for two full fan cycles before touching the inner field.”

Osei entered the change. The instrument accepted his hand under passive-preservation authority. Nia watched the temperature line descend without touching the console.

On the third fan cycle, the field settled. Osei offered her the custody lamp. She shook her head and pointed to the cradle.

“You hold it,” she said.

He fixed the lamp to his wrist. Together they began checking the seal that her license could no longer open.

Chapter 17 — What the Returns Took

The woman in the verified image had Lio’s left hand and a stranger’s face.

Iven knew the hand because the smallest finger leaned inward from an old pressure-door fracture. He knew the work coat by its gray shoulder patch, thermal-grid east crew, though the preserved image offered no color. Behind her, a service corridor divided around a coolant trunk. He could place the corridor within Eiren’s lower grid to half a meter.

He could not place the woman.

Cal waited beside the image wall. Nia stood beyond the instrument line with both hands visible and empty. A court resonantist occupied the control station where she would once have sat. Iven had declined the operator’s name. Learning it would create another item he might notice losing.

“Remove the verification label,” he said.

Cal blanked the border. The image remained: woman, conduit, numbered valve, light falling from above and slightly spinward.

“Ask me again.”

“Do you recognize this person?” Cal said.

“No.”

“Do you recognize the setting?”

“Grid approach four. The camera stands inside the intake lock. Valve E-fourteen feeds the lower mirror loop.”

“Can you identify the person by context?”

“Yes.” He tasted glucose at the back of his throat. “Lio Senn had east-grid duty that week. She wore that coat. The hand matches hers. The image record names her. Those facts identify her.”

Cal’s gaze remained on him. “Do you recognize Lio’s face?”

Iven looked again.

The face had a broad mouth held against a smile, one eyebrow lower than the other, hair cut close because grid seals punished loose strands. All details arrived. None connected to the space where his wife should have been. Recognition ought to have crossed the gap without work. Instead, he assembled a conclusion from evidence.

“No,” he said.

Nia’s fingers closed around each other, then separated. The movement did not touch a control.

The vocal body lay at a low angle to reduce cardiac load. Warm saline entered at Iven’s left wrist. Three phase leads crossed his chest and disappeared through the bed housing toward C-9. The connection gave no sensation he could name. Its cost showed elsewhere: a breath that ended before he expected, the room taking longer to settle after he turned his head, a person reduced to facts that refused to become familiarity.

Cal dimmed the image. “We can stop preparation.”

“This counts as documents described at medical baseline.”

“The load rose when you tried recognition.”

“I did not ask to stop.”

“No.” Cal placed the sealed image wafer facedown. “You also prohibited rehearsal that enters broad recall.”

“Then do not make it broad. Ask where she went.”

Nia spoke from behind the line. “Route reconstruction may cost less than face retrieval, but we have not tested the difference at this depth.”

“I have.”

“When?”

“Every time I wake.”

The court resonantist lowered the wall brightness. Iven could not see the operator’s hands, only their reflection in the black panel: fingers on a descent control signed by someone other than Nia. His stopping protocol had received all required seals that morning. Spoken command or thumb to middle finger. Either would begin the taper within two seconds, no judicial permission required.

Cal opened a blank route field. “Start at your quarters.”

A white point appeared.

Iven lifted his right hand. Tremor entered before he reached the wall, so Nia gave him a pointer with a broad grip. He set the point on a curve that the field had not drawn.

“The quarters were here, west of registry ventilation. Door opened toward the narrow passage because Lio said an inward-opening door invited people to leave tools against it.”

“Do you remember the room?”

“I remember distances. Bed to wash recess, two steps. Door to storage, one and a half. Her side had the easier path to the corridor because she took early grid calls.”

“Do you remember seeing her there?”

He found no image. “I remember moving around her.”

The distinction hurt without producing a picture. There had been a body in the spaces he preserved. Someone had occupied the clear path and complained when his route sheets covered the table. He knew how far to reach for a cup she had set down. The cup had no hand attached.

Cal said, “Continue only if you choose.”

Iven drew the passage to the first lift. “On failure day, the lift stopped two levels above the heat junction. She used the maintenance ladder behind the south bulkhead.”

“How do you know?”

“She called from the junction.”

“Do you remember the call?”

“Not the order of it.”

He drew six ladder turns, a crawl around a seized inspection gate, and the slanted conduit that crossed the final landing. The pointer moved with increasing certainty. Route geometry survived without effort where a face had demanded assembly. He knew which rung flexed because Lio had once marked it for replacement. He knew she would put her right boot to the wall at the fifth turn to clear a cable housing. The knowledge contained her practice and omitted her appearance.

Nia watched the body-load display. “Your phase demand has fallen by twelve percent.”

“While I trace the route?”

“Yes.”

“Then faces cost more than paths.”

“One sample does not establish sequence of loss.”

“Use a non-visual record.”

Cal looked to Nia before selecting the preserved engineering log. She read the body-load line and nodded. The document opened as a grid of valve states and technician marks. It carried no reconstructed voice, only entries written by a living worker forty-seven years earlier.

Iven recognized the notation before he read the name. Lio marked uncertain flow with an open square instead of the standard half bar. She hated closing a field that someone else might mistake for measured fact. Three open squares crossed the east return line. Beside the third, she had written: BYPASS SOUTH. DO NOT WAIT FOR MIRROR FEED.

“Was that her phrasing?” Cal asked.

“Yes.”

“How can you tell?”

“She gave instructions as routes. Destination first when urgency rose. The grid office preferred fault first.”

“Do you remember her writing it?”

“No.”

“Do you remember hearing the instruction?”

Iven searched. A call existed as pressure without words. “I remember that the mirror feed had already failed when she reached intake four. I do not remember whether she told me before or after I sent the convoy correction.”

Nia said, “Do not force order.”

“I am not forcing it. I am finding where it fails.” He pointed to the log time. “This stamp comes seventeen minutes after the east-grid alarm.”

“The stamp clock drifted during thermal loss,” Cal said.

“By forty-one seconds at this point. Later it drifted faster.”

“What tells you that?”

“Route office clocks synchronized from the same junction until the west conduit broke. Lio’s grid clock used a local thermal standard after that. I corrected them in the deposition.”

Cal brought up the registered distress timing. Iven aligned the two clocks without calculating. He moved the log seventeen millimeters left, then another fraction for thermal drift. The entries fitted the route packet around a missing band.

“That band contains your last conversation?” Cal asked.

“It contains several events. Her call. My change to the rescue approach. A pressure warning from the north cavern. I know their relations to the failure. I do not know their relations to each other.”

The wall showed three records converging on one dark interval. Geometry held their positions around the gap while sequence refused to enter it.

Cal reopened the image beside the log, smaller now. “Can the writing help you recognize Lio's face?”

“No.”

“Does it make the person in the image feel familiar?”

“No.” The answer came with relief because nothing in it required searching. “It makes the work familiar. That distinction matters.”

Iven read the final open square. Its route sent a crew toward the lower mirror loop after surface collectors had already begun losing heat. “She remained at the grid.”

“The log proves her credential entered this line,” Cal said.

“It proves more than that. She opened the south bypass after the intake lift stopped. The control lived at the junction. No remote path reached it after the west conduit broke.”

“So she had to be there.”

“Yes.”

“Does that help you remember choosing to leave her there?”

Iven’s hand tightened around the pointer. The phrase assigned him a decision he could not place. “I did not choose her station. She did.”

“Crown may ask whether you redirected the convoy knowing she remained.”

“I knew the grid still carried workers. I knew rescue required three bells. I knew the convoy turned away. Do not make missing sequence into missing knowledge.”

Cal marked the distinction in his preparation notes.

The engineering log stayed on the wall after he removed the image. Iven could follow Lio’s open squares from intake four to the failing mirror loop. The path carried her judgment at every branch. It did not restore the person who had made those marks. Her choices had survived in procedure while familiarity had left his body.

“Show another face,” Iven said.

Cal closed the field. “No.”

“You need to know what questions Crown can use.”

“I need to keep counsel from becoming another examiner who spends you for certainty.”

Iven let the pointer rest against his chest. “You already brought the image.”

“At your request after I described it.”

“And now you regret obeying?”

“I regret that the law recognizes the cost only after the harm becomes measurable.”

“That sounds like a judge’s problem.”

“I am not sitting as judge in this room.”

“No. You are sitting as the man who will ask the judge to keep her hands off the controls.”

Cal took the pointer and laid it beside the glucose cup. “Then I need your instruction, not another test.”

Iven looked toward Nia. “What did the returns take first?”

Her answer came too quickly for comfort. “We cannot map individual losses to specific activations.”

“I did not ask which return took which memory.”

“No.” She glanced at the muted image wafer. “Your observed losses affect sequence before spatial relation. Recent Callis events remain better ordered than Eiren events. Faces now require contextual identification. Routes remain precise, including routes learned before the deposition.”

“So sequence, then faces, then geometry.”

“That order fits what we have observed. It may not predict the next loss.”

“What else remains?”

“Preferences formed here. Your request for a Callis window. Your choice to receive the reserve correction before counsel. Your stopping control.”

“Those are records.”

“They are also choices you recall making.”

“Today.”

Nia did not answer.

The room fan cycled. Air crossed the body’s wet lips and cooled them. Iven wanted to ask what lasted after route geometry. The question offered no useful control. A reserve measured in returns could reach zero without a clean final boundary. He might retain a corridor after losing why anyone walked it. He might retain the shape of refusal after losing the event that made refusal necessary.

Cal slid the image wafer into a court sleeve. “Crown intends to use this during identity foundation. I can object that recognition adds no material fact. The image has independent authentication.”

“They want the failure.”

“They may want to test it.”

“Same cost, cleaner noun.”

Nia said, “We can provide the result from this preparation under seal. There is no medical reason to show the image again.”

“Will they accept your result after suspending you?” Iven asked.

“My license concerns operation. My observations remain subject to examination.”

“Why did they suspend it?”

Cal started to answer. Iven lifted one finger.

“Nia.”

She came to the edge of the instrument line. A stripe in the floor separated advice from control. “Seven years ago, Crown contracted me to calibrate what it described as an inert route model inside C-9. The order concealed a live medical line. My cadence enabled or stabilized Return 7. I did not know a body had been activated.”

“Did you suspect?”

“I knew Crown’s refusal to open the return channel departed from best practice. I accepted its density explanation and recorded no objection.”

“Did I see you?”

“No evidence places me in your body room. Your process received my instrument cadence through the linked channel.”

He closed his eyes. Four equal points and one longer descent lived somewhere he could not reach by asking. On his first Callis return, her hands at the controls had made his body expect a pressure change before it came. He had told her she had done this before. Neither of them had understood the sentence.

“Why keep her?” he asked Cal.

“You chose her for technical advice before we reconstructed the event. You may change that choice.”

“I asked Nia.”

She kept her hands at her sides. “Cal retained me because the record names me as your chosen technical adviser. I cannot operate any activation instrument. If you withdraw the preference, I leave.”

“Would you leave if Cal ordered it?”

“Yes.”

“Would you argue?”

“On the record, once.”

That sounded like her. Or it sounded like the woman he had known only through present returns. He possessed no earlier face for comparison. Trust had become another route: not recognition, but a sequence of observed choices. Nia had told him 2.1 before counsel. She had built a stop control that removed authority from her own station. She had admitted the seventh event before Crown could use it as a hidden blade.

“Stay,” he said. “Do not touch the controls.”

“I cannot.”

“That has not stopped institutions from finding a special interpretation.”

“It will stop me.”

Cal asked, “Do you want that preference recorded?”

“Yes. Include the conflict.”

The timing lamp opened its thread. Iven stated that Nia should remain for technical explanation despite her participation under concealed conditions. He did not forgive her. Forgiveness required a memory of the person before and after harm. He had only the present facts and the choice they supported.

When the thread sealed, Cal brought up Crown’s proposed examination sequence. It contained forty-two questions grouped under identity, route competency, event chronology, command authentication, and remedy relevance. Iven read only the headings.

“Which requires Lio?” he asked.

“Identity question six. Chronology questions eleven through fourteen. Crown argues that her grid role connects your route reconstruction to actual thermal conditions.”

“Her engineering log does that.”

“They may argue you learned the route from the log after waking.”

“Then ask for a route absent from the log.”

Cal opened the Eiren grid index. “Which one?”

Iven selected a maintenance bypass between intake four and mirror junction south. Lio’s preserved log named the endpoints but not the path. He traced it around two flooded conduits, through a hand-cut service opening, then down an unfinished stair that ended above the junction.

Nia compared the route to pre-failure construction records. “The opening appears on a repair permit from eleven months before the collapse. The stair appears on no final map.”

“Lio used it to reach a cracked return pipe,” Iven said.

“Do you remember going with her?” Cal asked.

“I remember the route.”

“Do you remember why you were there?”

“No.”

“Her repair permit lists no route cartographer.”

“Then perhaps she described it. Perhaps I walked it. The geometry does not tell me which.”

Cal marked the answer. “Crown will call that contamination between memory and record.”

“It is contamination between memory and losing memory.”

The body-load line climbed as he tried to place the trip. He could see the unfinished stair without seeing feet upon it. The service opening had sharp metal along its lower edge. Someone had wrapped cloth around it. He knew to duck left, then rotate one shoulder. No day attached itself. No voice told him why he had entered.

Nia said, “Demand has crossed the preparation ceiling.”

“I have not used the stop.”

“You set a ceiling for medical baseline. We agreed not to enter broad recall.”

“Can we finish the question list?” Cal asked.

Iven counted five headings still unopened. Each contained paths into sequence, faces, decisions. A rehearsal might reveal which questions did the most damage. That knowledge would be purchased with the damage it predicted.

“No,” he said. “Seal the list.”

Cal closed it at once.

“Not merely close it. Mark that I reject further rehearsal.”

“Reason?”

“Because preparation consumes the capacity it claims to protect. Crown gets one examination under the signed stopping rule. They do not get a smaller private version first.”

The timing lamp recorded his refusal. Iven felt no triumph. He had chosen ignorance about the hazards because measuring them repeated the hazard.

The court resonantist asked through the console, “Do you want descent now?”

He checked his hand. Tremor had increased. Thumb still reached the first joint of the middle finger. Speech remained easier.

“Not yet.”

Nia moved the blank wall where Lio’s image had been out of his direct view. “What remains to decide?”

“One question for counsel.”

Cal drew his chair closer. “Ask.”

Iven returned to the route field. His line from the quarters to the grid remained visible, precise through every turn. At its end, the system had placed a neutral dot where Lio’s verified image located her. The dot carried position without face, duty without voice. It would remain after this body descended because the court record had captured it. That did not make the dot a person. It made the Court responsible for what the person had chosen while present.

“If I stop tomorrow,” Iven said, “you may have unfinished testimony.”

“Yes.”

“If I do not come back, Crown will say the unfinished part matters more than the part I gave.”

“They will.”

“Can a person make a decision that survives their absence?”

Cal’s eyes went to the route ending. “Existing law protects some decisions after ordinary death. A will. A medical refusal made before incapacity. It has not recognized this form.”

“I did not ask what it recognizes.”

“No.” Cal placed both palms on his knees. “A person can make such a decision. Whether this Court will obey it remains unsettled.”

“Then settle your part before mine runs out.”

Iven spoke his stopping phrase. The independent resonantist acknowledged. As the twelve-second taper began, the route line stayed fixed on the wall.

Lio’s dot rested at the thermal grid. Iven could not give it her face. He could still show exactly how she had reached it.

Chapter 18 — Do Not Call Me Again

Iven woke to the Court already in session.

The first sound his body made entered the record: a wet breath drawn through a throat that had not existed twenty minutes earlier. The merits chamber answered with ventilation and the small click of a timing lamp opening its thread. Above him, three acoustic baffles curved toward the judges’ bench. Their edges helped him judge distance before his vision cleared.

“Identity check only,” Nia said from his right.

Her voice came from beyond the instrument line. The independent resonantist stood closer, one hand on the descent control and one above the saline regulator. Iven did not know the operator’s name. That choice had survived the night.

“Iven Ko,” he said.

“Place?”

“Callis Court of Continuance, axial merits chamber.”

“Purpose?”

“To give evidence about the Eiren rescue turn under a stopping protocol I control.”

Sena Pell leaned toward her bench microphone. “The Court acknowledges the protocol. Either recorded command initiates medical descent without further permission. No party may continue questioning after acknowledgment begins.”

Ada sat at the Crown table below the bench. She wore no route emblem. A stack of physical schedule sheets rested beneath her left hand, each corner aligned. Cal occupied the table nearest Iven’s bed with one sealed wafer and no paper.

Nia asked, “Do you want the window open?”

The chamber’s outer wall could show Callis rotating against the fixed dark or remain opaque. Iven had chosen the real view during his standing hearing because no memory packet had told him to want it. Today, movement beyond the glass would consume attention he needed elsewhere.

“Keep it closed.”

The wall stayed gray.

“Medical baseline permits examination,” the resonantist said.

Cal rose. “Chief Justice, before testimony, the witness asks that his present reserve correction, stop authority, and refusal of further rehearsal enter as a single exhibit.”

“Crown has no objection to the reserve correction,” Ada said. “We preserve our scope objection.”

“Preserved,” Sena replied. “Proceed.”

Cal came close enough that Iven could see the gray at his temples. “Iven, do you understand that you remain under oath from the standing proceeding?”

“Yes.”

“Do you understand that the Court may assess unfinished testimony differently from completed testimony?”

“Yes.”

“Who decides when this examination ends?”

“I do.”

Cal opened the first route field. Three white points appeared in a triangle around the Meridian Mouth. A fourth point marked Eiren beyond the stable approach corridor.

“Tell the Court your work at Eiren Reach.”

“I maintained civil route geometry for emergency freight, heat exchange, and external rescue approaches. During the thermal failure I received the convoy track and calculated a viable detuning schedule.”

“What did rescue require?”

“Three Crown choir bells removed from Mouth stabilization for nineteen hours. Their tuned mass had to travel with the convoy through the shear boundary, hold an approach lane, then return after Eiren’s lower caverns reached evacuation temperature.”

“Did that guarantee rescue?”

“No. It created a viable window. Pressure closure remained uncertain. Surface transfer carried further risk.”

“What happened to the three bells?”

Iven placed his finger on the triangular points. They moved inward rather than outward. “Crown held them at the Mouth.”

“Was that motion consistent with navigation loss?”

“No. A lost convoy would show correction search, dispersed timing, or an emergency mass release. These bells received synchronized vectors. They turned together.”

Cal brought up the pilot telemetry admitted earlier. The three points bent away from Eiren and tightened around the corridor.

“What command governed that turn?”

“Dispatcher code AR-6.”

Ada’s hand stopped on the schedule sheets.

Cal asked, “What did that code mean in the notation used forty-seven years ago?”

“Authority Retention, level six. It ordered all controlled mass to prioritize corridor continuity over local rescue. Only central dispatch could issue it across three separately piloted bells.”

“Could a pilot generate AR-6 alone?”

“No.”

“Could shear damage generate it?”

“No. Damage can corrupt a code. It cannot authenticate the same command to three craft through separate channels.”

“Did you receive the code directly?”

“Yes.”

“Where?”

“Eiren route office, lower civic level, after the surface relay began overheating.”

“Do you remember the sequence around receipt?”

Iven watched the route field. Eiren lay at one edge, the Mouth at another. Between them, sequence opened into a blank he could not cross. “I remember the code. I remember plotting its consequence. I do not reliably remember whether Lio called from the grid before or after it reached me.”

“Does that uncertainty change the command’s route effect?”

“No.”

“Why not?”

“The bells’ telemetry carries authenticated send time. My deposition carries the received code. The route solution shows the commanded vector. Lio’s call does not cause any of those facts.”

Cal displayed two modeled paths. The first sent three bells toward Eiren. The second closed them around the Mouth.

“Which path did the convoy take?”

Iven touched the second line. “This one.”

“What happened to Eiren’s rescue window?”

“It closed. Without tuned mass, the convoy could not cross the shear boundary. Eiren’s thermal lattice failed over thirty-one hours.”

“Did your original deposition distinguish accident from decision?”

“Yes.”

“How?”

“I encoded both available routes, the nineteen-hour requirement, the authenticated command, and the observed turn. A navigation failure would remove choice from the record. AR-6 put choice back into it.”

Cal separated the three pilot tracks. “Show the Court how synchronized command differs from a shared response to shear.”

Iven moved the first track to its raw heading. “This pilot received the command spinward of the fault boundary. It corrected by seven degrees.” He selected the second. “This one had already entered lateral drift. It corrected by eleven degrees and reduced mass output to prevent overshoot.” The third line approached from below the route plane. “This pilot rolled before turning because its bell could not cross the new vector at full tune.”

The tracks looked different until their corrected paths converged around the Mouth.

“If shear drove them,” Iven said, “their initial vectors would diverge according to position. If a general corridor alarm drove them, each pilot would choose a safe hold and await mass coordination. These three craft performed different maneuvers toward one assigned geometry.”

“What supplied that geometry?”

“AR-6 carried a central hold solution with individual approach vectors.”

“Could your deposition have generated the apparent convergence after the fact?”

“No. Pilot telemetry came from independent flight recorders. My layer predicted where their lines would meet. The later recovery found them inside that tolerance.”

Cal overlaid the deposition solution as a thin amber ring. All three tracks entered it.

Iven felt the body-load line rise, then settle. The calculation required no face and no lost conversation. Distance, bearing, tune, correction. The returns had left him the part Crown once needed him to simplify.

“Does this establish why central dispatch chose corridor retention?” Cal asked.

“No.”

“Does it establish that they chose it?”

“Yes.”

The timing lamp pulsed once to mark the answer. No one in the chamber moved.

Cal opened the old Crown distress classification. “Official history calls the convoy loss unavoidable. Is that description consistent with what you observed?”

“No.”

“What description would be accurate?”

“The convoy was redirected to preserve the Meridian Mouth after Crown received a viable Eiren rescue schedule.”

Ada rose. “Objection. The witness can establish route events, not executive knowledge.”

Sena looked to Cal.

“I asked what he observed,” Cal said.

“His answer assigned receipt to Crown as an institution. Authentication does not establish which office read the rescue schedule before dispatch.”

“Limit the answer,” Sena said. “The Court receives it as route evidence. Executive attribution requires separate foundation.”

Cal nodded. “Iven, can you identify the dispatch office that signed AR-6?”

“Central mass coordination.”

“Can you identify an executive who ordered that office to send it?”

“No.”

“Can you identify whether central coordination received your viable schedule before sending the code?”

“Yes. Its acknowledgment checksum appears in my deposition.”

Cal enlarged the checksum. “No further direct questions.”

The phrase changed the room. Until then, every route had moved toward a fact Iven still held. Ada could now choose where to press. Her questions waited in the aligned sheets under her hand.

Sena said, “Crown may examine.”

Ada approached the foot of the bed but remained outside the medical line. “Iven Ko, do you recall composing the route deposition inside C-9?”

“Yes.”

“Do you recall every layer?”

“No.”

“Which layers do you recall?”

“The route alternatives, dispatch authentication, rescue requirement, convoy turn, and Eiren thermal progression.”

“Do you recall encoding personal memory?”

“Some.”

“Do you recall deciding what to omit?”

“No.”

“Then you cannot exclude the possibility that your present account combines the original deposition with information shown to you on Callis.”

“I can distinguish some encoded facts from present preferences. I cannot establish the source of every remembered detail.”

Ada lifted one sheet. “The pilot telemetry shown today reached Callis after your first public activation. Correct?”

“Yes.”

“You had already alleged a commanded turn before seeing it?”

“Yes.”

“Your allegation could have shaped how later technicians interpreted the telemetry.”

“The telemetry existed before my allegation.”

“Interpretation can still change.”

“So can concealment.”

Cal did not object. Ada let the answer enter.

“Do you recognize this code table?” she asked.

A Crown schedule page appeared. AR classifications filled two columns. Iven read the level-six entry. Corridor authority retention under distributed mass threat.

“Yes.”

“Does it contain the word Eiren?”

“No.”

“Rescue?”

“No.”

“Abandon?”

“No.”

“Then AR-6 did not itself order anyone to abandon Eiren.”

“It ordered the three bells away from the only route that could reach Eiren.”

“That describes effect, not text.”

“Routes make text physical.”

Ada’s eyes held his. “Could central coordination have believed your approach window already closed?”

“They acknowledged the schedule while it remained viable.”

“Could they have believed detuning would close the Mouth permanently?”

“My projection gave a closure risk of eight to fourteen years under the severe branch, not permanent loss.”

“Could that closure have isolated six habitats?”

“Not the same six. Habitat dependencies differed forty-seven years ago.”

“Could it have killed people elsewhere?”

“Yes.”

“How many?”

“I did not hold complete population and reserve data.”

“Then you cannot tell this Court how many lives AR-6 preserved.”

“No.”

“Can you tell it how many Eiren lives a successful rescue would have saved?”

“No. A viable window does not guarantee full evacuation.”

Ada placed the sheet down. “Your evidence proves a choice between risks. It does not prove that the choice lacked justification.”

“My evidence proves Crown called the choice a navigation loss afterward.”

“That concerns later reporting.”

“It concerns whether anyone believed the route absolved them.”

The body-load indicator sounded once. Nia stepped to the edge of her line.

“Phase demand has reached the first warning band,” she said. “Narrow retrieval only.”

Ada turned toward Sena. “Crown has not entered autobiographical examination.”

“You have entered comparative casualty modeling,” Cal said.

“It bears on the claimed meaning of the command.”

Sena addressed Iven. “Do you choose to continue?”

“Yes, within the warning band.”

The judge marked the choice. Ada waited until the resonantist confirmed stable breath support.

“Mr. Ko, did you know Lio Senn remained at the thermal grid?”

“I know it now and encoded it then.”

“That did not answer whether you presently remember knowing it before the turn.”

“I do not retain reliable event order across that interval.”

“Do you recognize her verified image?”

“I identify her by context. I do not recognize her face.”

“Could your failure to recognize faces affect identification of a dispatcher?”

“AR-6 uses authenticated code, not facial identification.”

“Could sequence loss affect whether the acknowledgment preceded the command?”

“The independent timestamps establish order.”

“Your correction of those timestamps relies on Eiren clock drift. Who calculated that drift?”

“I did.”

“During the event or on Callis?”

“During the deposition. Callis records later confirmed the relation.”

“Do you recall performing the original calculation?”

“I retain the geometry and result.”

“But not the act?”

“Not as a sequence I can watch.”

Ada moved to the route wall. “Then let us test the calculation from raw clocks without relying on your recovered result.”

Nia said, “That requires broad reconstruction.”

“Crown disputes the foundation.”

“The pilot telemetry and deposition checksum establish it independently,” Cal said.

“They establish a match only after his drift correction. Crown may test whether the witness can reproduce the correction.”

Sena read the body-load line. “How much cost?”

The resonantist answered, “Unknown. Prior route work remained below face retrieval. Event-order reconstruction drove the warning rise yesterday.”

“I refused further rehearsal yesterday,” Iven said.

Ada faced him. “A refusal to rehearse does not answer examination.”

“No. It answers who gets to spend the uncertainty.”

Crown’s schedule sheets remained on the table. Six habitats depended on the Mouth now. Ada had not denied the old turn. She disputed what could be done with it, and what could be proved before the body carrying it ran out. Iven understood her fear without accepting her claim to his remaining memory.

Sena asked, “Do you choose to attempt the calculation?”

“No.”

Ada said, “Then Crown moves to treat the drift correction as untested witness interpretation.”

“Argument later,” Sena replied. “Ask another question.”

Ada returned to her table and selected a different sheet. “Did Crown personnel perform nine prior activations?”

“The physical record supports nine.”

“Do you remember all nine?”

“No.”

“Do you remember any Crown examiner by name?”

“No.”

“Do you remember being asked for a controllable statement?”

The phrase struck without opening a scene. A room narrowed around him. Someone had asked for fewer causes, one fault, one route. The memory carried pressure at both wrists and no face.

“I remember the demand,” he said.

“What words?”

“I cannot recover them without searching.”

“Please search.”

Nia stepped forward. “Objection on medical grounds.”

Ada did not turn. “The prior activation history bears on contamination and coaching. Crown needs to know what propositions entered the signal before Callis.”

The warning tone sounded again. Iven’s right hand had begun to shake against the linen. He pressed it flat, not yet forming the stop signal.

Cal rose. “Crown has the sealed return ledger. It can identify its own questions without compelling him to reconstruct the harm.”

“The ledger contains clipped purpose fields, not full examination.”

“Because Crown withheld the full records.”

“Privilege remains under review.”

Sena lifted one hand. “The witness may answer whether he recalls. He will not be ordered to induce broader recall.”

Ada looked at Iven. “Do you choose to search for the wording?”

“No.”

“Do you remember giving a false answer during any prior activation?”

“No.”

“Does that mean you did not, or that you cannot remember?”

“I cannot remember.”

“Do you remember contradicting yourself?”

“No.”

“Same qualification?”

“Yes.”

“Do you remember whether your original deposition named a specific executive?”

“No.”

“Will you search?”

“No.”

Ada set one sheet aside and uncovered another. “Crown seeks leave for expanded examination of the deposition’s personal layer, limited to source discrimination and prior inconsistent statements.”

Nia said, “That scope could consume the remaining warning margin before descent completes.”

“We can recess and resume under a separate activation,” Ada replied.

The chamber seemed to contract around the sentence.

A separate activation. Another body, another opening breath, another set of careful reasons why the question could not wait. The case might gain an executive name. It might gain no useful fact. Either result would leave less of him available to refuse the next request.

Cal spoke toward the bench. “No separate activation has been authorized.”

“A future request remains available if examination cannot finish today,” Ada said.

Iven looked at the stop control reflected in the wall. The independent resonantist had not moved. Nia could advise and could not touch it. Sena could limit questions and still leave tomorrow unsettled. Cal could object until his words became doctrine or failed to.

Only Iven could use what the protocol already gave him.

He pressed thumb to the first joint of his middle finger.

The resonantist saw it. “Physical stop acknowledged. Descent begins.”

Ada said, “Crown preserves unfinished cross-examination.”

“Preserved,” Sena replied. “No further questions.”

The route wall dimmed. Saline warmed at Iven’s wrist. Twelve seconds had felt short in preparation. Here each one held enough room for someone to propose another return.

“I have another preference,” he said.

Cal came to the bedside. “State it for the lamp.”

The timing lamp brightened. Iven’s breath support had begun to soften the edges of speech.

“Do not call me again,” he said.

No one interrupted.

“Not for cross-examination. Not for an executive name. Not if another answer would win this case. No further activation of my conscious pattern. Preserve the record without making me enter it again.”

Sena asked, “Do you understand that this choice may leave material questions unanswered?”

“Yes.”

“Do you understand that Crown may challenge the legal force of a preference after this body deactivates?”

“Yes.”

“Do you make this choice free of instruction from counsel, adviser, or operator?”

“Yes.”

Cal said, “Is this preference limited to the present examination?”

“No. This is my final embodiment.”

Nia’s face held no plea. “Do you want C-9 preserved?”

“Yes. Preserve it. Do not use preservation as permission.”

The lamp requested final confirmation. Iven could no longer feel the middle finger under his thumb. He kept the hand in place anyway.

“I choose not to be respeaken again,” he said. “The choice belongs to me even when I cannot repeat it.”

The timing thread sealed.

Breath support carried the next inhalation. His own effort did not. The chamber’s distances remained clear: Cal at one meter, Nia beyond the line, Ada at the Crown table, Sena above them, the route wall six meters left of the bed. Faces softened before positions did.

C-9 drew the active pattern down through the leads. Iven felt no bell, only the loss of the room’s vertical measure. The route display kept Eiren and the Mouth on opposite sides of the gray wall. Between them, three bells still held the turn he had named.

The last thing he could place was Nia’s empty hand resting outside the controls.

Chapter 19 — Evidence Without a Witness

Cal had not yet removed the spent timing lamp when Crown asked the Court to compel another activation.

The lamp stood beside the witness place, its green filament dark after recording the final breath of the vocal body. Linen restraints lay folded against the chair arms. A technician had drained the warm saline from the support collar and capped both lines, but a clear drop still trembled at one connector whenever Callis corrected its roll. No person occupied the chair. The court record nonetheless remained fixed on it.

Ada Neme rose at Crown’s table with a motion wafer in one hand. She looked as though the hearing had merely paused for an equipment change.

“Crown moves to strike the accusation concerning dispatcher code AR-6,” she said. “In the alternative, Crown requests completion of cross-examination under the Compulsory Evidence Clause.”

Cal kept his palm over his microphone. Around the axial chamber, paper-thin acoustic baffles curved between the delegations. They had absorbed Iven’s last words without echo. Now they made Ada’s request sound close enough to touch.

Chief Justice Sena Pell examined the wafer transmitted to her bench. “Completion by what means?”

“Controlled access to the surviving phase record. The current embodiment ended before Crown could test scope, source contamination, or whether the alleged memory had been reinforced through counsel’s preparation.”

“The witness exercised a stopping authority recognized by this Court.”

“While embodied,” Ada said. “Standing in continuance ended with active coherence. Property rules govern what remains unless the Court extends personhood beyond the Articles.”

Cal opened his microphone. “The Court cannot use expiration of a right to authorize the act the right prohibited.”

Jalen Orr sat behind Ada, three nested route arcs fastened at his collar. His face had not changed when Iven refused further calling. It changed now by less than a blink.

Ada turned toward Cal. “You are assuming the prohibition survived its holder.”

“I am asking the Court not to destroy evidence while deciding that question.”

“The unfinished cross-examination prevents the accusation from carrying dispositive force. Preservation cannot become a method for admitting one side of a contested memory.”

Sena raised two fingers. Both microphones closed.

She did not confer with the other six justices. Their remote seats showed delay clocks from three habitats, none longer than eleven seconds. Every word spoken in Callis reached those benches after a measurable interval. Cal had spent his career treating such intervals as protection against impulse. Today each clock measured how quickly a preference could be converted back into property.

Sena opened the common channel. “Crown will identify the access it seeks.”

Ada sent a technical schedule to the court display. Cal read the heading first: narrow-band route retrieval, one embodied interval, projected coherence cost 0.18 to 0.31 return. The estimate treated stress as a controlled variable. It also assumed the nine earlier activations had left the same distribution of damage Nia had mapped before the merits hearing.

“Your schedule requires a vocal body,” Sena said.

“For authentication, yes.”

“Then call it what it requires.”

Ada’s jaw tightened. “A limited respeaking.”

The word settled into the chamber without the protection of a euphemism.

Cal touched the edge of his record slate. He could oppose the request through the preservation order, through evidentiary fairness, or through the bodily authority the Court had already recognized. Each path failed at a different seam. Preservation delayed destruction but did not decide ownership. Evidentiary fairness favored Crown once cross-examination remained incomplete. Bodily authority belonged to persons, and the Articles tied Iven’s personhood to active coherence.

A reversible order could hold C-9 for a day. It could not reverse another read.

“Crown’s estimate excludes memory loss outside the requested route field,” Cal said when Sena gave him the channel. “The last activation demonstrated loss of facial recognition and sequence. Narrow prompts did not confine prior damage to narrow categories.”

Ada answered at once. “Then the Court should permit observation without broad recall.”

“Observation by whom?”

“Court-appointed resonantists.”

“On what subject?”

“The remaining property.”

Cal let the term remain audible. “Crown proposes to reconstruct a person so technicians may observe property.”

“I propose a lawful test of an evidentiary object whose temporary personhood has ended.”

“What would the object be asked?”

Ada looked down at the schedule. “Whether AR-6 came from direct sensory memory, a route model, or later reconstruction.”

“And if he refuses?”

“He cannot refuse before he exists.”

Sena’s timing lamp lit amber. “That answer reaches the issue more directly than Crown may intend.”

No one at the Crown table moved.

Cal saw the shape of the argument then. The motion did not merely ask whether completed testimony remained admissible. It asked whether a preference could bind the conditions required to produce its future holder. Existing doctrine had no name for that interval. The law recognized a person during activation and material outside it. Crown intended to cross the gap by acting before any person could object.

He requested the technical record of Iven’s stopping decision. Nia’s adviser feed remained dark; her license suspension barred her from operating the court instruments. Osei Marr occupied the archive station instead. He sent three synchronized traces: vocal pressure, body-control cessation, and the phase response captured within C-9 when Iven formed the refusal.

The display showed no image of Iven. Cal had ordered all body-room video sealed after deactivation. He would not let the chamber study a dead man’s face for signs of sincerity when the operative record lay elsewhere.

Osei enlarged the final eleven seconds. A set of phase lines tightened around a route relation Cal could not read. The geometry persisted after the vocal trace ended.

Ada approached the display by permission. “This proves a state change in the bell.”

“It records a change coincident with the expressed preference,” Cal said.

“It may record fatigue.”

“That remains possible.”

“Or cooling in the vocal coupling.”

“Cooling remains possible.”

“Then you lack authentication.”

Cal looked toward the vacant chair instead of the lines. “Authentication can remain disputed without authorizing the act under dispute.”

Ada returned to her table. “Crown cannot test the state change if every test counts as violation. Counsel has built a closed claim: the record says do not examine the record, and examination would destroy the proof. No adversarial court can accept that structure without surrendering judgment to an unverifiable remnant.”

“She did not say unverifiable,” Sena said.

Ada faced the bench. “Then require verification.”

“Through the same destructive access the preference forbids?”

“Through the only method current law recognizes.”

For the first time since the motion began, frustration entered Ada’s voice. Cal did not mistake it for cruelty. She had spent twelve years defending a system whose schedules fed six habitats. Her fear counted living bodies, pump margins, catalyst reserves. That fear did not grant Crown use of another body. It did make the legal shortcut more dangerous because reasonable people could call it necessity.

Sena recessed the hearing for forty minutes and ordered both sides to preserve present conditions.

The chamber emptied by routes assigned to avoid contact between counsel. Cal remained at his table until the public gallery’s pressure door sealed. The axial floor carried almost no weight. His case wafers would have drifted if their magnetic corners had not held them in exact rows.

He removed the spent timing lamp at last.

A clerk met him at the evidence door with a custody sleeve. Cal slid the lamp inside. The sleeve recorded its dark filament, seal number, and the elapsed interval from deactivation. It asked for an item description.

He entered: Timing record, witness stopping decision.

The terminal rejected witness because active standing had ended.

Available categories appeared beneath the field: phase property, court instrument, biological waste, disputed salvage, other.

Cal selected other.

“Custodian Orsen.” Sena’s voice came from the passage behind him.

He sealed the sleeve before turning. “Chief Justice.”

She had left her black hearing coat at the bench. In the plain gray underlayer, she looked less severe and more tired. A physical docket strip curled between her fingers.

“You argued around the admissibility question,” she said.

“Crown’s proposed remedy would decide the rights question first.”

“Crown says no rights exist to decide.”

“Crown also accepted his personhood yesterday.”

“For the duration.”

Cal glanced through the evidence door window. C-9 did not sit there. The bell remained in the archive mass ring under a field that would fail if anyone attempted removal. Yet every category on the terminal had already reached for it.

Sena flattened the docket strip against the wall rail. “Suppose the refusal survives. Does every expressed preference survive?”

“No.”

“Which ones?”

“I do not know yet.”

“Suppose a signal person orders their estate transferred during an activation. Does that command bind descendants a century later?”

“Not by virtue of embodiment alone.”

“Suppose they prohibit any study of a bell that also contains public navigation data.”

“Then the Court separates the conscious layer from unrelated records where access permits it.”

“And where it does not?”

Cal considered the custody sleeve in his hand. “Then necessity carries a burden proportional to the destruction.”

“That sounds like ordinary balancing.”

“It may not be enough.”

Sena gave him the docket strip. “It is not enough. Ordinary balancing lets the institution create necessity, then spend the weaker party to answer it.”

The words did not absolve his argument. They cut away its easiest support.

On the strip, Sena had marked three questions in her narrow script. Could a novel preference retain force after active standing ended? What proof authenticated such a preference without renewed embodiment? What limits prevented dead preferences from governing living institutions?

Beneath them she had written one instruction: briefs by first lamp, Day 28.

“You are ordering full argument?” Cal asked.

“I am ordering you to discover whether you have one.”

“Crown requested immediate access.”

“They will receive a temporary refusal.”

“On preservation grounds?”

“On judicial ignorance.” Sena pressed her thumb to the docket seal. “The Court does not know whether the refusal survives deactivation. Until we know, no one may make the question impossible to answer.”

A seal line burned through the strip. Cal felt the small heat through his glove.

“Crown will say delay consumes habitat reserve,” he said.

“Crown may prove that in the remedy phase. Today they asked to consume a witness.”

She left him beside the evidence door.

Cal carried the sealed instruction to his workroom at the end of the axial passage. The room had one fixed table, two handrails, and an acoustic baffle that lowered when the door closed. He fastened the docket strip under a reading lens and called up the Continuance Articles.

The Articles had grown through salvage disputes, inheritance claims, military beacons, and medical continuities. Their drafters had feared fraud first. They required active demonstration because any stored preference could be fabricated by whoever held the substrate. The Novel Preference Test answered that fear by demanding a choice not recoverable from recorded intent. Once a remnant made such a choice, the Articles granted standing in continuance.

Then the text stopped caring.

It specified what happened to property during personhood. It said nothing about what happened to a person’s choice after active coherence ended. Medical directives offered the closest analogy, but those directives belonged to continuously living people whose bodies could no longer answer. Testamentary law offered another, yet wills transferred property and did not forbid creation of the testator. Orders against destructive sampling protected rare cultural remains, though not because the remains had asked.

Cal opened his old ration docket from the Pellin shortage. During that crisis, he had authorized temporary pump seizures because waiting for a full appeal would have starved three districts of heat. He had considered reversibility: Crown could repay seized catalyst later, reopen contracts, restore title. Nobody could restore the days of cold, but the machinery and accounts remained subject to correction.

C-9 admitted no such repair. A destructive read spent the only state that could prove or resist the act.

His clerk, Vey Aru, entered after knocking against the baffle frame. She held a stack of printed authorities clipped to a tether.

“I found eleven cases on post-deactivation directives,” she said. “Nine concern estate transfers. One concerns a military navigation remnant that requested erasure after mission review. The command erased it before appeal.”

“What happened to the appeal?”

“Dismissed. No remaining subject with standing.”

“And the last?”

“A medical signal refused a second embodiment. The hospital complied. No one contested.”

“So the useful cases vanished before producing law.”

Vey secured the stack to his table. “Compliance leaves fewer precedents than violation.”

She had also brought the archive fraud docket Cal requested during recess. He opened its oldest case. A freight consortium had fabricated six dead navigators from copied training traces, then used their supposed preferences to contest corridor fees. None had formed a novel choice. The fraud had succeeded for nine months because the consortium controlled both the substrate and the technicians who certified it.

Crown would place that case at the center of its answer. If a remnant’s instruction survived without active challenge, whoever held the glass might manufacture a silent principal with unlimited patience. Estates could acquire obedient ancestors. Ministries could preserve one emergency preference beyond the emergency. Corporations could put old executives between living regulators and every locked archive.

Cal read the certification defects. All six traces repeated phrases present in the training packets. Their phase states remained unchanged when they spoke. The technicians had destroyed their source instruments before independent inspection.

“Iven’s record differs at every point,” Vey said. “Independent custody lamp. Court-supervised embodiment. Novel choice against litigation interest. Phase change at expression.”

“Those differences authenticate one decision. They do not yet define a general test.”

“What else would you require?”

Cal marked four columns on a blank wafer: valid standing, informed scope, contemporaneous phase change, independent custody. A fifth requirement resisted compact language. The preference had to concern access to the substrate whose destruction would prevent later refusal. Otherwise a narrow bodily rule could spread into control over unrelated people and property.

He wrote necessary relation over the last column, then disliked its neutrality. Necessary for whom, decided by whom, under what pressure? Institutions had always possessed better nouns for the acts they wanted.

“The holder must understand that access spends coherence,” he said. “The refusal must identify that access. The Court must verify both without performing it.”

Vey pointed to the fourth column. “Independent custody fails if every qualified resonantist works for Crown or Callis.”

“Then qualification cannot substitute for adversarial observation.”

“Two technicians?”

“Two instruments measuring different conditions, with hostile review of method.”

She added the language. “You are writing an evidence rule.”

“I am trying not to write a religion.”

The remark came out harsher than he intended. Cal removed it from the dictation channel. He had heard advocates turn remnants into sacred objects whenever technical scrutiny threatened a sympathetic claim. Reverence could deny personhood as efficiently as ownership. A relic could not revise its answer, withdraw permission, or choose an inconvenient end.

Vey looked toward the sealed acoustic baffle. “Crown will still demand a live challenge.”

“Then we need a method that lets them challenge the instrument rather than the person.”

Cal read the military dismissal. The court had called the matter moot because destruction eliminated both claimant and disputed property. The opinion contained no malice. Its judges had applied categories available to them. A person who ceased could not hold standing; property that ceased could not receive a remedy.

“What does Crown cite?” he asked.

“The extraction cases. They argue active standing cannot reach backward to prevent a lawful operation begun while the remnant counts as property.”

“But their operation cannot be completed without active standing recurring.”

“That seems to be their problem.”

“It becomes ours if we say so.”

Vey waited. She had served him long enough to hear the difference between doubt and instruction.

Cal moved the Articles aside and placed Iven’s final preference record beneath the lens. The text transcript mattered, but not alone. A sentence could be coached. A voice could be cut. The surrounding decision carried more: Iven had been offered the chance to continue, understood the legal consequences, exercised an already recognized stopping authority, and chose an outcome harmful to his stated litigation goal. He had surrendered the strongest remaining evidence rather than submit to further use.

That choice satisfied the same structure as the Novel Preference Test. It did not repeat original distress intent. It arose during valid personhood. It changed what Crown could obtain and what Iven could ever prove.

The law did not need to preserve every preference forever. It needed to prevent institutions from waiting until the holder could no longer defend one concerning his own reconstruction.

Cal dictated the first line of his brief.

“A valid preference concerning destructive access does not lose force merely because the person’s exercise of that preference ends embodiment.”

Vey read it on her slate. “Crown will call that personhood after death.”

“Then we distinguish force from personhood.”

“How?”

He looked at Sena’s three questions. Fraud. Authentication. Rule by absent voices. Any theory broad enough to answer the first motion could become an instrument for old wealth, preserved executives, or fabricated ancestors. Crown would be right to attack it.

“We do not give the absent person a vote,” Cal said. “We require the living Court to honor a valid refusal about what may be done to that person’s remaining body.”

“Is the phase record a body?”

“For this purpose, it may have to be.”

Vey unfastened a fresh wafer. “That needs proof.”

“Yes.”

“And a limit.”

“Yes.”

Outside the workroom, the first-lamp bell sounded through Callis. Its tone reached the table as a vibration rather than music. Cal removed the hearing transcript from his draft stack. Testimony could establish the old crime if corroborated. It could not establish the authority of the refusal without making Iven’s words carry every burden at once.

He set the transcript aside and drew Osei’s phase traces toward him.

By the time the second lamp lit, Crown’s unfinished cross-examination no longer occupied the heading of Cal’s brief. He wrote a narrower question above the geometry record, one that asked what the Court could authenticate without forcing anyone back into the chair.

Chapter 20 — The Custodian Charged

Nia pressed her thumb into the tribunal seal while the charge clerk read out every act she had already admitted.

The seal took a capillary image through her glove and fixed it beside her name. Its glass warmed by two degrees. She kept her hand in place until the clerk told her to remove it.

“Unauthorized duplication of a restricted thermal map,” the clerk said. “Failure to disclose prior instrument involvement before assuming advisory custody. Maintenance activity under a compromised evidentiary chain. Distribution of archive evidence beyond the administrative seal.”

“I disclosed the instrument involvement when I established it,” Nia said.

The clerk looked toward the three members of the Callis Archive discipline panel. “Do you contest the stated acts?”

“No.”

“Do you contest intent?”

“I contest Crown’s description of the copy as distribution. I sent it to a Court custodian under preservation authority.”

Ada Neme sat at the opposing table with two Crown counsel and a sealed transit case. She had not presented the charges. Callis had charged its own senior resonantist because custody could not mean immunity. Crown had intervened to request transfer of C-9 if the panel found Nia unfit.

The tribunal chamber occupied a maintenance bay rather than a court room. Oil cloth covered the tool lockers. Fan vibration entered through the deck and touched the long table at regular intervals. Nia felt each cycle through the side of her hand. The room had been chosen because archive discipline required a panel to inspect the instruments involved. C-9 remained two pressure doors away, secured in cradle seventeen.

Panel Chair Der Mares adjusted the charge wafer under a physical lens. “The seventh recall bears your calibration signature.”

“Yes.”

“You did not know you were calibrating a conscious phase record.”

“No. Crown designated it as an inert route model.”

“Would a senior resonantist have identified the deception at the time?”

Nia examined the question before answering. Seven years ago, she had received a sealed bell interface, a route-response tolerance sheet, and an instruction not to inspect source layers. Every number had fit ordinary work. The person behind those numbers had not.

“A senior resonantist should have required provenance before calibrating any destructive access path,” she said. “I accepted the designation because the coupling pulse stayed below the stated read threshold. That threshold had been falsified.”

“Then Crown deceived you.”

“Yes.”

“Does deception remove your professional responsibility?”

“No.”

One of the panel members, Ulen Sarr, leaned toward his microphone. “What would you have us punish? Failure to detect a sophisticated concealment or the later breach of seal?”

“The first requires correction of archive practice. The second requires judgment of my choice.”

“You are not required to recommend your own sanction.”

“I am required to distinguish the acts.”

Der Mares set down the wafer. She had worked freight resonance before taking the chair; two fingers on her left hand carried old auric-glass burns. “Then distinguish them.”

Nia looked at the timing lamp between them. Its filament recorded without interpretation.

“Seven years ago, I treated a title record as proof of inertness. That error made me useful to an unauthorized recall. On Day Three, I found nine heat blooms and recognized my signature on the seventh. I copied the map because the Crown seal controlling it came from the institution implicated by it. I sent one copy to Custodian Orsen. I did not release the signal or the map to the public.”

“Osei Marr later released the count,” Ulen said.

“He released nine returns as a number. I neither ordered nor approved it.”

“Why did you retain him?”

“Because his breach did not alter C-9, and removing him would have left the bell to technicians with less knowledge of its damage.”

“That sounds like operational convenience.”

“It was an assessment of preservation risk.”

Ada requested the channel. “Crown asks the panel to note that preservation risk now rests substantially on technicians who have each breached a seal.”

Nia turned her head far enough to see Ada, not far enough to release the timing lamp from her peripheral vision. “Crown performed nine destructive reads under false designations.”

“The old reads remain under merits review.”

“My discipline does not restore your credibility.”

“No,” Ada said. “It removes yours from the custody calculation.”

Der Mares cut both microphones. “This panel will not try Crown’s conduct through inference, nor permit Crown to try the Eiren merits through a license charge.”

The room returned to fan vibration. Nia counted four cycles before the chair reopened her channel.

“Callis archive law permits permanent revocation where a custodian knowingly defeats a lawful seal,” Der Mares said. “It permits suspension up to seven years where authority remained genuinely disputed. What sanction do you accept?”

“A five-year loss of activation license.”

One Crown counsel looked down at his notes. Ada did not.

Der Mares asked, “Why five?”

“Long enough that I cannot operate another respeaking before this case, my employment, or public attention moves on. Short enough to recognize that I sent the copy into judicial custody rather than private possession.”

“You would retain non-activation preservation privileges.”

“If the archive permits them.”

“And access to C-9?”

“Only under another licensed operator.”
