“I believed disclosure without an alternative would force a false remedy,” she said. “A full shutdown could kill people whose dependence Crown helped create. I treated concealment as the price of preventing that response.”

“Did you disclose the existence of a slower alternative when you found its index?”

“No.”

“Why?”

“First, I did not have the model. Later, I had reason to believe it worked and still withheld the index while I tried to obtain it inside Crown.”

“How long?”

“Nineteen days.”

Cal let the number remain where she put it. “Do you ask the Court to excuse that?”

“No.”

Jalen entered through the Crown access door before the next question. He wore no operations key either. His counsel made room, but he remained standing behind the rail.

Sena addressed him. “Chair Orr, do you contest the authenticity of the executive minute?”

“I have not inspected the object since its removal.”

“Your system digest identifies it.”

“I contest unauthorized context, not the existence of a record.”

“Do you contest that Crown officers directed the convoy mass toward Mouth stabilization?”

Jalen’s attention rested on the sealed bar. “The company has already acknowledged that stabilization occurred.”

“That did not answer the question.”

“No,” he said. “I do not contest the direction.”

The answer offered no confession and no grief. It removed a factual dispute because the evidence made continued denial costlier than concession.

Sena ordered the old minute opened to all parties and the model confined to the engineering panel. Each polity would receive a complete non-executable account of burdens by the next civic morning. Crown’s control claims would be heard after feasibility testing, not before.

The clerk broke the bar’s outer cloth. Two record lights appeared through the sleeve, one aged amber and one recent blue.

Cal returned to his table. The chair beside him stayed empty. No channel opened toward the archive ring. No one asked a silent person to prove what Crown’s own minute now established.

Ada watched the panel technicians carry the blue record toward the sealed engineering room. Historic intent had entered evidence behind them. Ahead waited pumps, patients, ration ledgers, and twenty-six months of work.

At the sealed engineering-room door, Ada surrendered her Crown access wafer to the clerk and followed the model inside.

Chapter 24 — Twenty-Six Months

Nia held the temporary mass key against the test bridge while Osei cut power to the second Callis ring.

Nothing moved. The bridge carried no tuned mass, only a reduced phase model built from the suppressed Crown schedule. Even so, the instrument resisted the change through its ceramic handle. A faint pressure gathered under Nia’s fingers, traveled into her wrist, and stopped when the simulated ring dropped out of alignment.

“Loss at the outer dependency,” Osei said.

Across the engineering room, six habitat tracks narrowed on the wall. Latchkey went amber first. Morcant followed. Vesper Fold’s agricultural window held for another cycle before its line began to thin.

Ada stood beside the sealed Crown reader with her sleeves rolled to the elbow. Without her operations key, she looked less altered than Nia had expected. Authority had left only a pale strip around her wrist where the device had covered the skin.

“Restore ring two,” Ada said.

Osei did. The habitat lines recovered.

“That confirms the objection,” said Crown’s senior scheduler from the observation rail. “Callis cannot convert both contributions without exposing three dependents during the transfer.”

“Not both at once,” Nia said.

“The filed plan shows two ceremonial mass rings.”

“It shows two conversions. Your challenge assumes simultaneous release.”

The scheduler tapped the public timing lamp. Every exchange entered the Court’s engineering record as it occurred. Behind the acoustic baffles, representatives from six habitats watched with their own technical advisers. Sena Pell had given the panel one civic day to turn a hidden model into a plan any polity could inspect without receiving executable bell timing.

Nia shifted the key one notch clockwise. “Run the first conversion against Crown schedule relinquished in stages.”

Ada entered the condition. Crown’s exclusive priority line broke into six witnessed allocations. The test bridge changed pitch under Nia’s hand.

Osei withdrew the second ring from the model again.

Latchkey stayed green. Morcant dipped toward amber, then recovered as Near Orison’s industrial freight moved out of the first ration block. Vesper Fold held its heat-sink window. The simulated corridor narrowed but did not close.

The scheduler leaned forward. “You have transferred the failure to inner fabrication.”

“Yes,” Ada said.

Near Orison’s representative spoke from behind the baffle. “Transferred or shared?”

“No rhetorical answers,” the panel chair said. “State the material effect.”

Ada enlarged the inner-habitat track. “Near Orison loses priority freight and catalyst volume throughout the first stage. Medical supply holds. Nonessential fabrication does not.”

“Define nonessential,” the representative said.

“You define it under a published cap,” Ada replied. “Crown no longer decides in a private schedule.”

Nia watched the test bridge rather than the gallery. The suppressed model carried more than phase timing. It carried choices in fields labeled reserve protection, customer continuity, and operator discretion. Each neutral heading concealed a person who would wait. Making those choices public did not reduce their pressure on the instrument.

“Latchkey pump load is wrong,” Ema Orsen said over the remote engineering link.

Her image occupied a narrow panel beside the habitat tracks. Transmission delay from Latchkey clipped the first fraction of each reply, so the chair had instructed everyone to wait for her channel marker before speaking. Ema wore a pump hood pushed back from her forehead. Condensation had dried in a line across one cheek.

Ada checked the source table. “The model uses your filed maintenance reserve.”

“My filed reserve assumes normal heat-sink replacement. Your first stage moves that replacement by one window.”

“Crown’s table classifies the delay as tolerable.”

“Crown’s table does not operate our pumps.”

The scheduler began, “Latchkey certified those limits.”

Ema’s marker remained lit. He stopped because the record would have shown the interruption.

“We certified a maximum under ordinary redundancy,” she said. “Take one sink bank out for the longer window and the remaining pumps cannot carry the stated load. Reduce throughput before you delay the shipment, or you will force an emergency shutdown halfway through the stage.”

Nia felt Osei’s attention move from the wall to her hands. He had learned, during four months of disciplinary interviews and custody work, when she had found a fault but not its boundary.

“Can we reduce Latchkey throughput without losing Morcant catalyst?” he asked.

Ada brought the transport dependencies forward. “Not under Crown priority.”

“Under the public allocation?”

A different path opened when she removed the operator-discretion field. Morcant’s catalyst shipment shifted earlier. Latchkey’s heat-sink mass followed through the next narrow window. Near Orison lost another fabrication block.

Its representative pressed for recognition. “That brings us below our industrial reserve agreement.”

“The agreement depends on Crown exclusivity,” Cal said from the legal table. He had no engineering vote, but the Court had assigned him to identify obligations that disappeared with the judgment. “If exclusive control falls, the reserve preference falls with it.”

“People work in those plants.”

“No one has suggested otherwise.”

“Then put their losses in the plan.”

Nia looked toward the representative. “We will.”

The clerk entered Near Orison’s fabrication loss beside Latchkey’s pump limit before Osei started the next run.

Osei added the throughput reduction and reran the stage. The test bridge pressed back more evenly. Latchkey’s track remained above its pump floor. Morcant received catalyst before the deeper ration period. Near Orison’s industrial line contracted into a band that would be politically difficult and physically survivable.

The panel chair said, “First transfer conditionally sustained. Proceed.”

By the sixth hour, the model had covered every available surface.

Osei worked at the test bridge. Nia occupied a lower table with phase diagrams, custody copies, and two cups of glucose tea gone cold. Ada translated Crown schedule assumptions into statements the habitat engineers could challenge. Ema remained on the link from Latchkey, leaving twice to answer pump alarms and coming back with fresh stains on her hood.

The original schedule treated the Mouth as a sequence of protected openings. The phased retuning plan treated it as a system that would remain imperfect for twenty-six months. Every stage borrowed stability from the next and paid it back through rationing, converted mass, or slower transit.

Callis had called the two outer structures ceremonial because their daily work had become easy to ignore. Civic processions crossed them at accession years. Families fixed memorial tags to the sheltered rails. The rings also balanced the Court, carried archive shielding, and gave the station enough reserve mass to trim without suspending hearings. Conversion would close whole public sectors and force the Archive into a narrower thermal allowance. Nia traced the service routes on the model. C-9’s cradle remained stable, but three conservation bays would lose simultaneous cooling.

“We need a preservation rotation,” she said.

Osei pulled up the archive load. “One bay warm at a time.”

“Not warm. Reduced cooling. Anything near stress limit moves before conversion begins.”

The Callis representative asked, “How long will public access stay closed?”

Ada checked the stage sequence. “Most of the first year. Partial access after that, subject to mass recovery.”

A murmur passed behind the local baffle. Callis had offered rings in the abstract. The schedule now translated that offer into sealed memorial walks, delayed court sessions, and archive crews moving fragile glass by hand.

“Put every closure in the civic instrument,” Nia said. “If Callis claims credit for the mass, it records what residents lose with it.”

The clerk added a public-access appendix. Another hidden cost became a schedule someone could dispute.

Nia crossed out a proposed safeguard. “This requires active access to C-9.”

“It requires an Eiren-bell baseline,” Ada said.

“Then use the non-conscious geometry Osei authenticated. No destructive read. No activation channel.”

The Crown scheduler objected from the rail. “A fixed geometry cannot report drift.”

“It can set a boundary,” Osei said. “Current bells report their own drift. C-9 contributes a verified route relation and nothing else.”

“What happens if the relation falls outside tolerance?”

“We stop that stage and use the published fallback.”

“The fallback costs another trade window.”

Nia met his eyes. “Then write the cost beside it.”

He did not like the answer. He entered it anyway.

Ada moved the stage marker to the patient-transfer gap. A column of medical dependencies expanded across Arden Coil, Latchkey, and Near Orison. The total had survived every version of the model: 1,900 patients whose ventilation, thermal regulation, or drug production could not tolerate the narrowest corridor interval.

Arden Coil’s clinician appeared on a second remote link. “Your transfer field treats these people as one movement.”

“It is one protected window,” Ada said.

“It contains different tolerances. Some can travel before the ring conversion. Others cannot leave their current support until replacement beds come online.”

“How many groups?” the panel chair asked.

“Five clinical dependency classes. We can publish the classes without publishing individual records.”

Nia brought up the mass table. “Does splitting them change the required transit load?”

“Not total load. It changes preparation and receiving capacity.”

“Then the bell schedule can retain one protected window while the medical plan uses multiple departures around it.”

The clinician shook her head. “Not if Crown can cancel feeder transit to protect freight recovery.”

Ada’s fingers stopped above the reader.

Under the old schedule, Crown could downgrade a feeder line whenever corridor stability threatened contracted priority freight. The hidden model preserved that power in an appendix even while distributing the main bell schedule. A single retained clause could leave the patient plan technically present and operationally disposable.

“Delete operator downgrade,” Ada said.

The Crown scheduler stood. “You cannot remove emergency discretion.”

“We can identify who holds it.”

“During an unstable window, a committee cannot tune a corridor.”

“No one proposed tuning by committee. The active controller keeps safety authority. It cannot use that authority to replace a protected medical movement with commercial recovery unless the movement itself exceeds a published safety boundary.”

“You are asking operators to carry liability without judgment.”

“I am asking them to exercise technical judgment inside a visible rule.”

The scheduler looked toward the Court’s counsel. “And when the boundary proves incomplete?”

Cal answered. “Emergency action remains available. Its reasons become reviewable afterward.”

Nia remembered how many harms had entered the archive under the word emergency. Review after damage could not restore a phase state or a body. Yet hidden discretion had not prevented harm. It had only decided who could name it.

She took the patient field off the schedule and laid its load against the bridge. “Run all five dependency classes through a single protected designation. Stagger feeder departures. Lock commercial replacement behind the medical load.”

Osei built the test. The bridge’s response roughened at the transition into the narrow window.

“Too sharp,” Nia said.

He extended the feeder taper. The roughness moved rather than vanished.

Ema’s marker lit. “You are solving the transit edge. Latchkey’s receiving pumps will see the arrivals as a heat edge.”

“Can you absorb it?”

“Not without temporary sink mass.”

Morcant’s allocator spoke. “We have idle refinery sink during our ration block.”

Ada checked the geometry. “Moving that mass costs freight capacity.”

“Our freight capacity already falls under the plan.”

“Your catalyst reserve also falls.”

“It falls because everyone else needs what we refine. Use the idle sink before the next run or it helps no one.”

No one praised the offer. The clerk entered it as a mass contribution with an obligation to return or replace the equipment after the retuning. Shared sacrifice required better records than generosity.

With Morcant’s temporary sink mass, the patient edge flattened. Osei ran the stage three times under different delay assumptions. None crossed a safety floor.

The panel chair marked the medical window sustained.

Nia rubbed the ceramic key with her thumb. The material had warmed through hours of handling. In another room C-9 remained under a seal built from a refusal. Here, living people had supplied every correction the hidden model lacked. No absent voice had been made to solve their work.

The live technical challenge began at the ninth hour.

Court channels carried a public view of the dependency tracks, the test bridge, and every non-executable assumption. Crown’s scheduler received the challenger’s position because Jalen had withdrawn the company’s cooperation after the vault seizure. He did not repeat Jalen’s politics. He attacked the plan where a schedule could actually fail.

“Stage fourteen,” he said. “Second Callis conversion. Apply a delayed thermal recovery on the outer Crown bell and loss of the Near Orison contribution for one window.”

Ada entered both faults.

The bridge kicked against Nia’s palm. Morcant crossed amber. Latchkey’s pump line stayed above floor by less than the panel’s approved margin. The Mouth trace split into two unstable bands.

Observers spoke behind the baffles until the chair cut their channels.

“The filed schedule fails its live reserve test,” the scheduler said.

Nia kept the key in place. A bridge model could not injure her, but habit made her hold it as if a sudden movement might score glass. “Why did you combine those faults?”

“Because both remain plausible.”

“Plausible together?”

“The model does not prohibit coincidence.”

Ada opened Crown’s historic recovery table. “Your own standard applies coupled faults only when they share a cause or each exceeds the independent trigger.”

“I am challenging a public plan, not certifying Crown practice.”

“Then state a standard the plan can meet.”

He looked at the timing lamp. “No dependent habitat crosses its failure floor under one major bell delay plus one discretionary mass withdrawal.”

“Near Orison’s contribution cannot remain discretionary after adoption,” Cal said.

“The polity could breach.”

“Then the breach has consequences, not neutrality.”

Near Orison’s representative requested the channel. “We will bind the mass contribution if every other signatory binds its ration share.”

Morcant followed. Latchkey’s marker lit from the remote link. Vesper Fold, Arden Coil, and Sable entered the same condition. Their statements did not create mass. They changed a modeled withdrawal into a breach the schedule could treat as exceptional rather than ordinary operator choice.

The scheduler did not yield. “Run the bell delay alone.”

Osei cleared the discretionary withdrawal and retained the delayed thermal recovery. The Mouth trace continued split.

Nia felt the defect in the bridge before she saw its source. Pressure arrived from two simulated bells at nearly the same interval, then fell away together. The retuning stages had inherited Crown’s assumption that synchronized recovery minimized lost capacity. Under the slower schedule, synchronized recovery created a common edge.

“Separate the recovery intervals,” she said.

Ada examined the stage. “That extends the second conversion.”

“By taking time from where?” Osei asked.

“Freight recovery after the protected medical window.”

The Crown scheduler said, “You will deepen rationing.”

“Yes,” Nia replied.

Near Orison’s representative asked for the industrial effect. Ada displayed it. Morcant requested its catalyst curve. Ema asked for the revised Latchkey sink interval. Each cost appeared before anyone consented.

Osei separated the bell recoveries and extended the stage. The first unstable band narrowed. He shifted the outer bell by another increment. The second band joined it without crossing.

“Run delay,” Nia said.

The bridge pressed hard, then settled.

“Add one feeder loss,” the scheduler said.

Osei added it. Latchkey remained above floor. Morcant touched amber but did not enter failure. Sable lost a freight window. Vesper Fold’s agricultural buffer shortened.

“Reverse the order of bell recovery.”

The model survived.

“Move the patient preparation late.”

Arden Coil’s clinician objected. “That violates the clinical plan.”

“Test it,” Nia said.

They did. The medical field crossed its boundary, and the public display marked the schedule invalid before the corridor model could treat those people as available load.

For the first time, the Crown scheduler nodded. “The boundary reports correctly.”

The panel chair asked whether he had another coupled challenge under the stated standard.

He studied the tracks. “Not without adding a second major failure.”

“Then stage fourteen survives.”

The room did not celebrate. The surviving plan required two converted Callis rings, binding ration shares, public allocation, medical relocation, slower freight, and the end of Crown’s schedule monopoly. Success appeared as a set of losses no longer assigned in secret.

Near the end of the civic day, the panel compiled the public instrument.

Its title read PHASED RETUNING: TWENTY-SIX-MONTH MULTI-POLITY SCHEDULE. Below it, the obligations began with verbs. Crown relinquishes exclusive priority. Callis converts two ceremonial mass rings. Inner habitats ration. Six signatories publish reserve use. Medical authorities relocate 1,900 patients under protected transit. Operators retain immediate safety control and disclose every substitution.

Ada read the Crown clause twice before signing as a technical witness. Her signature did not bind the company. The Court’s order would do that if judgment adopted the plan.

Ema signed for Latchkey’s pump assumptions through the remote seal. “Do not use my name to call this painless,” she said.

“No one will,” Nia answered.

“You do not control what they call it.”

“No. Only what the record shows.”

Osei carried the test key to Nia for the archive witness mark, then stopped. Her license loss barred her from certifying activation work. This instrument contained no respeaking, yet its phase procedures still crossed a category the disciplinary order had reserved for licensed staff.

He looked at her rather than the form.

“You checked every run,” he said.

“You performed them.”

“The method came from you.”

“The mark belongs to the person who can lawfully answer for the instrument.”

Osei set his seal into the field. His hand remained steady through the acceptance pulse.

The panel doors opened. Court clerks took the public schedule toward the judgment chamber. Engineers began disassembling the test bridge because Callis needed the table for actual conversion work as soon as the order issued.

Nia removed the temporary key. A pale pressure line remained across her fingers, then faded while Osei locked the instrument under his own name.

Chapter 25 — Judgment in Silence

Sena Pell began reading while the engineers were still sealing the retuning schedule into the Court register.

The last clerk crossed behind the bench with an amber custody band in both hands. Cal heard its clasps meet through the paper-thin acoustic baffles. On the public wall, six habitat seals waited in a vertical column beside Callis and Aster Crown Transit. None had yet changed color. Agreement on engineering did not bind anyone until the judgment gave the plan an authority willing to survive appeal.

Sena set one palm on the printed opinion. She preferred paper for operative orders because a page could not revise itself after a vote.

“This Court addresses two questions argued together and decided separately,” she said. “First, whether a valid preference concerning destructive access retains force when the person who formed it no longer occupies an active embodiment. Second, whether Crown’s historic choice and present concealment create liability that can be remedied without imposing an immediate corridor closure.”

Crown’s counsel sat beside Jalen Orr at the lower rail. Ada occupied no Crown seat. She had taken a place among technical witnesses, three rows behind the advocates, where her bare wrist rested on the folded model summary.

Nia Daro sat beside Osei. Her license-loss order had removed every formal role she once held in activation work, but Iven’s valid selection of her as technical adviser had not been erased by discipline. Cal had insisted the seating clerk preserve that distinction. Institutions often turned a punishment into a deletion because deletion required less careful recordkeeping.

At the center of the witness rail stood an empty chair.

The Court had not moved it after argument. No device faced it. No channel waited beneath its arm. A clerk had sealed the floor socket. Cal had checked both before the chamber opened.

Sena continued. “The Continuance Articles do not make duration a measure of personhood. They make novel preference the threshold. Once a signal person passes that threshold, the state may not convert the limits of embodiment into permission to destroy the remaining state.”

She paused while the public transcript fixed the sentence.

Cal knew the next paragraph. He had written its first version at a narrow desk during the thirty-first night, crossed out half of it after Sena’s objections, and removed every claim broader than the case required. The wider Court had changed it again. That process mattered. A rule created to honor one person had to survive judges who did not share Cal’s attachment to the facts.

“We therefore recognize silent standing,” Sena said. “A preference formed during valid standing in continuance may retain legal force after deactivation when the preference concerns renewed embodiment, destructive measurement, or other access that would consume the person’s remaining substrate. Such force requires authentication independent of reconstruction, contemporaneous capacity, a defined protected interest, and review for fraud or material contradiction.”

The phrase entered the chamber without ceremony. No light changed above the empty chair.

Crown’s counsel wrote something for appeal. Cal expected him to. A durable right needed an adversary precise enough to expose its weak boundaries.

Sena turned one page. “Silent standing does not grant remnants authority over public policy, property distribution, inheritance beyond existing law, or the choices of living persons. It protects bodily autonomy where the body exists as a nonrenewable state. It does not establish governance by the dead.”

Jalen’s head lifted slightly. The judgment had answered his strongest objection rather than dismissing it. That narrowing had cost Cal three days and much of his pride. He had wanted a principle that named every indignity the case revealed. Sena had required one the Court could enforce.

“Applied here,” she said, “the refusal geometry authenticated by the Callis Archive confirms a novel preference formed by Iven Ko while he held standing in continuance. The record states his stopping decision. It also establishes his request against renewed activation. Crown may not compel another respeaking, and no custodian may authorize one absent evidence that the geometry itself resulted from fraud.”

Osei did not look toward C-9’s remote custody indicator. He kept his eyes on the opinion. Nia’s hands lay open on the rail, motionless except when her right thumb crossed the place where an activation key once rested.

Cal felt relief arrive as a procedural fact. The request now bound everyone in the chamber, including those who had never heard its voice.

A justice elected by Sable lifted his copy. “Chief Justice, I join the result and request clarification on duration. Does the protected preference expire when technology changes?”

Sena turned to the remedies page. “Not automatically. Review attaches to the access request, not to age alone. A custodian must show why new methods no longer threaten the substrate or contradict the protected interest.”

“And if a later method claims to read without destruction?”

“Then the applicant proves that claim through independent testing. The remnant does not bear the cost of demonstrating safety.”

Crown’s counsel requested the lamp. “May a custodian refuse testing forever by citing uncertain damage?”

“No. The order permits non-contact examination, external modeling, and comparison against unrelated archives. It forbids spending this person’s state to establish whether spending it would be safe.”

The Sable justice marked his page. “That satisfies my concern.”

Cal had resisted that paragraph in conference. He feared any review language could become a tunnel back to compulsion. Osei’s method had changed his position. A right did not require ignorance around it; it required placing the risk on whoever sought access. The distinction would demand competent custodians, funded laboratories, and judges willing to reject convenient certainty.

Sena named the custody chain. Callis would hold C-9 in its preservation field. Two external custodians would review every annual integrity report. Any fraud petition required particular evidence and could not rely on the remnant’s refusal as proof of incapacity. Nia could advise on preservation but could not conduct licensed activation work during her five-year loss. Osei could certify non-conscious measurements once his license reached full standing.

Each limit gave the order somewhere to fail in practice. Each review rule also gave a future court a place to catch that failure before a silent preference became property again.

The evidence ruling came next.

Crown had argued that unfinished cross-examination barred the dispatcher allegation. Under ordinary hearing rules, a party could not present an accusation and then remove the witness before the opposing side tested the full account. The rule protected courts from crafted fragments. It had never contemplated a witness whose continued appearance consumed the witness.

Sena read the distinction without softening it.

“A person’s lawful exercise of bodily autonomy does not become evidentiary misconduct because it prevents further examination. The resulting record may carry less weight. It does not vanish. Courts must ask what portion reached completion, what opportunity for testing occurred, and what independent material confirms the claim.”

Cal watched the timing lamp beside Crown’s table. It marked each operative sentence with a narrow green pulse.

“The completed testimony identified dispatcher code AR-6, a three-bell rescue requirement, a nineteen-hour interval, and a commanded turn away from Eiren Reach. Crown tested memory sequence, personal knowledge, route competence, prior activation damage, and the distinction between modeled routes and witnessed operations before the witness stopped the examination.”

Crown’s counsel stood. “Chief Justice, the order understates our unasked causation questions.”

Sena looked over the printed page. “Your objection remains preserved. Sit down.”

He did.

“The unasked questions limit unsupported inference,” she continued. “They do not erase facts independently confirmed by pilot telemetry, physical scoring in bell C-9, Crown’s activation ledger, non-conscious preference geometry, and the executive minute authenticated from Crown’s own vault.”

The minute appeared on the public wall. Its operative AR-6 entry occupied one plain block. Preserve corridor continuity. Redirect available convoy mass to primary stabilization. Eiren recovery proceeds only if no further corridor degradation results.

No voice read it aloud.

“The Court admits the completed testimony for the purposes supported by that corroboration,” Sena said. “It finds that Crown officers knowingly reassigned the three bells required for rescue and used them to stabilize the Meridian Mouth before Eiren’s available rescue interval closed.”

A sound crossed the gallery, too dispersed to identify as grief or anger. The baffles caught it and reduced it to cloth movement.

“The Court does not find that preservation of the Mouth lacked legitimate public purpose. The corridor supported inhabited polities then and supports 2.3 million people across six dependent habitats now. Legitimate purpose does not convert concealment into accident. Crown chose between lives, preserved the profitable system, then represented the choice as navigation loss.”

Jalen remained still. The opinion did not assign him his grandfather’s act. It assigned him what the records proved about his own tenure: obstruction, continued suppression, and defense of exclusive control after a feasible alternative existed.

Sena named those findings one by one. She did not call him cruel. Courts reached conduct more reliably than souls.

Cal looked at Ada. She listened without turning away. The judgment credited her production of the minute and model as cooperation after breach. It also found that twelve years of defending concealment caused continuing institutional harm. Her nineteen-day withholding of the model index remained in the findings. Assistance reduced no fact to absolution.

The registry clerk brought in two sealed volumes.

One carried Eiren’s historic resident register. The second contained the reconstruction completed from broken transit manifests, shift rolls, docking claims, and private duty lists. Cal had reviewed their count until the arithmetic entered his sleep: 18,204 registered residents plus 312 transient workers excluded from the original casualty field.

Sena waited until both volumes rested beneath the public timing lamp.

“The old record classified Eiren’s population loss through categories inherited from property, residence, and compensated cargo,” she said. “Those categories omitted people whose temporary work status did not reduce their capacity to die.”

The clerk opened the second volume to its certification page.

“The Court orders the Orison civic disaster register corrected to 18,516 persons lost at Eiren Reach. Each recovered name shall enter the register as a person. Where a name remains unrecovered, the corresponding verified occupational or transit identity shall retain a place pending correction. The 312 transients shall not remain an appendix to resident loss.”

On the public wall, the total changed.

No list began scrolling. The Court had rejected that display after registry clerks argued that a ceremonial cascade would turn damaged entries into spectacle and make broken records appear less human than complete ones. The wall showed only 18,516, the two source volumes, and a route to inspect each record with its uncertainty intact.

Nia bowed her head once toward the number. Osei touched two fingers to the rail and withdrew them.

Cal remembered ration hearings during the Pellin shortage, when people became units in a column because a magistrate could not distribute heat by learning every name. Counting had practical mercy in it. Counting also offered institutions a place to hide the difference between a person omitted and a unit never entered. The corrected registry did not recover anyone. It prevented the old omission from continuing as administrative truth.

Crown’s counsel rose when Sena reached liability. “We renew our objection to class restitution absent individual estate claims.”

“Noted,” Sena said.

“The company cannot compensate unidentified estates.”

“The order does not require fiction. It requires a fund administered under verified claims, public memorial maintenance, surviving community institutions where lawful successors exist, and civic services connected to the corrected registry.”

“Forty-seven years of successor disputes will consume the award.”

“Then Crown will not control administration.”

The word restitution appeared beside the corrected count. Its details occupied pages rather than a single number. Crown owed payments into an independently governed Eiren claims fund. It owed the costs of registry reconstruction and C-9’s permanent preservation. It owed corridor credits to the six habitats whose dependence had been used to preserve concealment. Executives who inherited no personal guilt still faced corporate obligations carried through the same continuity that preserved Crown’s contracts.

A registry advocate requested the timing lamp. “The fund language still favors people who left complete records. Transient workers often have no lawful successors recognized by residence codes.”

Sena checked the operative schedule. “Unclaimed portions do not revert to Crown.”

“Where do they go?”

“To restoration of names, preservation of Eiren civic records, and services available to verified work communities connected to the lost transit crews. The administrator may not manufacture an heir to close an account.”

“Will occupational fragments qualify?”

“For identity restoration, yes. For private estate transfer, existing evidence rules remain.”

The advocate did not sit immediately. “Then some people receive a name in the public book and no private award.”

“Yes,” Sena said. “A court cannot repair missing kinship by inventing it. It can keep the institution that caused the loss from profiting by the missing evidence.”

Cal wrote the exchange into his copy beside the restitution clause. Courts often weakened honest orders by promising completeness. The fund could rebuild records, compensate successors, and support communities carrying identifiable loss. It could not create a family history where Crown’s own secrecy had allowed one to disappear.

The administrator’s first report would fall due during the retuning’s opening stage. That timing linked liability to current scarcity, which Crown had opposed. Sena retained it. A remedy postponed until abundance would never arrive because every institution could name a more urgent present use for the money.

Jalen requested standing.

Sena allowed it.

“You treat institutional continuity as liability when it burdens Crown,” he said, “and deny continuity when the same institution claims authority earned by keeping the corridor open.”

“The Court recognizes both,” Sena replied. “Crown’s operational service weighs against punitive closure. Its continuous benefit and control support restitution. Continuity does not permit selecting assets without obligations.”

“The award will reduce retuning capacity.”

“Operational payments follow the protected schedule. Registry and preservation costs do not.”

“You believe a distinction on paper will survive scarcity.”

“No,” Sena said. “The Court orders review because paper alone will not.”

Jalen sat. He had received an answer shaped like work rather than moral victory.

The corridor remedy required every habitat seal.

Sena adopted the twenty-six-month schedule under temporary multi-polity supervision. Crown lost exclusive control of choir-bell priority and retained a technical operating role subject to published allocations. Two Callis mass rings entered conversion. Inner-habitat rationing began with the first stage. Medical authorities received protected transit for all 1,900 high-risk patients. Independent engineers would publish stage failures and substitutions without releasing executable bell timing.

Then she reached Callis.

“A polity cannot demand visible sacrifice from an operator while treating its own contribution as symbolic,” she said. “Callis accepts the closure and conversion obligations stated in the public instrument. It also accepts catalyst rationing, reduced civic access, archive preservation rotations, and the consequent limits on Court scheduling.”

The Callis delegate rose and set her seal against the register.

Near Orison followed after its counsel reread the industrial cap. Morcant entered its catalyst obligation and temporary sink contribution. Latchkey’s remote seal arrived with Ema Orsen’s pump conditions attached. Vesper Fold, Arden Coil, and Sable bound their shares.

Crown’s seal remained dark.

“The Court cannot compel corporate agreement to every allocation,” Crown’s counsel said.

“It can condition continued operation of a public corridor under multi-polity law,” Cal replied from the advocate rail. “Crown may decline the operating role and surrender its bells to supervised tuning.”

Jalen turned toward him. “You know surrender would interrupt the first stages.”

“Yes.”

“You are using present dependence as leverage.”

“Crown has used it as immunity.”

Cal regretted the symmetry as soon as he heard it. A judgment chamber rewarded phrases that made opposing harms look equal. They were not equal. The six habitats needed Crown’s technicians, and Crown had a right to resist uncompensated seizure. The order worked because it preserved payment and technical authority while ending secrecy, not because one side had acquired better leverage.

Sena corrected the record more precisely. “Continued operation remains compensated. Exclusive control does not. Chair Orr, the Court requires a choice between the supervised role and an orderly transfer under the emergency corridor charter.”

Jalen requested a private conference with counsel. The timing lamp granted eight minutes.

During the recess, no one approached the empty chair. Clerks checked seals. Habitat engineers compared the effective date against reserve tables. Nia spoke quietly to Osei about cooling rotations on the archive ring. The law had not interrupted ordinary work long enough for anyone to mistake judgment for completion.

Jalen returned before the lamp expired.

“Aster Crown Transit accepts the supervised operator role under protest and preserves every appellate claim,” he said.

Its seal changed from dark to amber.

The six habitat marks turned green in sequence after it. Callis followed last because its conversion obligation required two internal civic countersignatures.

Sena gathered the opinion pages. “The majority judgment takes effect upon filing. Separate views will follow. Preservation orders remain active. No access to C-9 may occur except under the authenticated preference and the narrow fraud review stated here.”

She pressed the physical filing seal through the paper.

The chamber did not erupt. People read. Crown’s counsel began marking appeal points. Engineers carried authorized copies toward the retuning rooms. Registry clerks closed the two Eiren volumes and placed them together on one cart.

Cal remained at the rail until the public wall cleared everything except the corrected count and the first scheduled obligation.

18,516 persons lost. Callis ring conversion begins at second shift.

Osei left with Nia for the Archive. No channel opened behind them. The empty chair stayed empty while the Court’s order went to work.

Chapter 26 — The Voice Not Called

Nia found the stress line because Osei’s torque stopped one mark before the ring clamp seated.

He held the long ceramic key with both hands. The converted ceremonial ring moved beneath them in a slow, massive rotation, carrying its new tuning blocks past the service gantry. Through the soles of Nia’s boots, each block arrived as a low pressure rise and left as a fading pull. The clamp in front of Osei should have accepted the key’s full turn between those rises. It had refused.

“Do not force it,” she said.

“I am under limit.”

“The tool is under limit. The collar is not moving.”

Osei eased pressure without letting the key spring back. Four months ago, he would have asked her to take the handle. Now his license seal occupied the key socket, and her hands remained outside the work.

Callis had stripped the public walk from ring three and filled its sheltered curve with phase couplers, heat shunts, and service rails. Memorial tags once fixed to the inner rail had gone into temporary archive cases. Their empty fastener holes traveled past Nia every seventeen seconds. The station smelled of oil cloth and hot ceramic instead of procession incense.

The first retuning window would begin in forty-three minutes.

Osei locked the key at its current position. “Surface check?”

“Start with the witness strip.”

He pressed a passive strip along the clamp edge. One end darkened where pressure exceeded the seating model. The mark lay two millimeters spinward of the latch, narrow enough to miss under ordinary inspection.

“Block distortion,” he said.

“Or collar distortion.”

“We measured both after thermal soak.”

“Then measure what changed.”

Osei released the key into its brace and photographed the collar from four fixed angles. He placed a scale strip beside the latch, then asked Nia to witness the service temperature without touching the phase meter. The distinction felt absurd at close range. She could read the instrument aloud but not certify what its phase field implied. It also prevented the old habit by which a senior worker performed every meaningful step and called the junior worker trained.

“Service temperature within expected range,” she said.

“Visual scoring?”

“One bright contact line spinward. No glass exposure.”

“Prior condition?”

Nia opened the conversion record on a passive slate. The collar photograph from installation showed no bright line. Its measured diameter appeared within tolerance, but the record carried a note from Osei himself: coefficient accepted from Crown materials table pending installed confirmation.

“You left the dependency visible,” she said.

“I should have demanded confirmation before load.”

“Could the ring have waited?”

He considered the conversion sequence rather than answering from shame. “Not without losing the first protected medical preparation window.”

“Then your note preserved the risk for this inspection. That differs from pretending it was resolved.”

Osei looked at the refusing clamp. “A visible risk can still fail.”

“Yes.”

“That is not comforting.”

“It should not be.”

A ring worker approached with an emergency torque extension. “Control says the schedule can accept a field waiver if you seat within redundant capture.”

Osei did not take the extension. “Who assessed the collar distortion?”

“No one yet. The tool is for the time limit.”

“Put it back.”

The worker hesitated. Nia had seen that pause directed at her for years: the fraction of time in which a crew decided whether authority lived in the named license or the older person standing beside it.

Osei pointed to his seal in the key socket. “My hold. My decision.”

The extension went back into its case.

He set three pickups around the clamp and called the ring crew for a hold on local adjustments. Nia watched his order appear on the window ledger under his own name. She could advise, observe, and handle non-activation preservation. She could not authorize phase work for five years. The boundary had felt punitive on the first day. By the fourth month, it had become useful in ways she distrusted. Osei no longer borrowed certainty from her position.

The pickups reported stable block geometry and a collar ovality outside service tolerance. Osei repeated the measurement with a pickup from another kit, then rotated all three positions. The same oval followed the collar rather than the instruments. He saved both runs. A single surprising result invited caution; a repeated one demanded a decision.

“Heat?” Nia asked.

“Within the conversion model.”

“Whose model?”

He opened the source line. Crown’s old ring table had supplied the collar coefficient. Callis had verified it on three spare collars but not on this installed piece because removal would have delayed conversion.

Osei looked toward the central control room. “We can replace it before the second stage, not before the window.”

“Can you seat it safely?”

“Not to certified depth.”

“Then no.”

A window alarm sounded along the gantry. Thirty-eight minutes.

Osei sent the fault to control. Ada answered from the supervised operator desk before the ring engineer did.

“Effect?” she asked.

“One clamp uncertified on ring three,” Osei said. “No active instability. We need the stage recalculated without this tuning block.”

“How long?”

Nia watched him make the estimate. “Nine minutes for the first model. Longer if it fails.”

“The six-habitat release begins in thirty-seven.”

“I see it.”

“Give me the fault field.”

He did. Ada did not ask Nia to confirm.

The ring engineer came onto the channel from the opposite gantry. “I can authorize a reduced seating depth under emergency discretion.”

“Based on what installed-collar test?” Osei asked.

“Operating history. The ring carried civic balance for sixty-one years.”

“Not at retuning load.”

“The clamp has redundant capture.”

“The second capture shares the distorted collar.”

Silence held the channel for half a breath.

Ada said, “Model it absent. No waiver until we know the alternative.”

Nia unclipped a passive gauge from Osei’s belt and set it beside the dark witness strip. Handling it crossed no license line. Her fingers still remembered the pressure of phase tools with an accuracy that sometimes woke her before shift. She used that memory for comparison, not authority.

Beyond the converted ring, the Archive’s shielded section passed through view. Choir bell C-9 occupied its preservation cradle behind two pressure doors and a court seal. The seal accepted integrity checks from outside. Only the authenticated preference held inside the bell could authorize a change to the activation barrier, and the custody system contained no override for schedule convenience.

The doors stayed closed.

The absent-block model failed at minute twenty-nine.

On the control room wall, the Mouth trace narrowed into a broken fork. The slower schedule already used less synchronous mass than Crown’s former windows. Removing one Callis block shifted the first edge beyond the published tolerance for Morcant’s catalyst convoy.

Ada stood between Crown’s surviving scheduler and the multi-polity allocation board. Every substitution appeared on the public operations channel with its effect by habitat. Jalen no longer occupied the room. Crown technicians still did, paid to operate bells they no longer scheduled alone.

Ema Orsen joined from Latchkey with pump noise crossing her link. “Can you move the window?”

“Later increases your heat-sink gap,” Ada said.

“Earlier?”

“Ring three cannot recover.”

Morcant’s allocator said, “Our convoy can shed freight.”

“Catalyst?”

“Not enough to recover the trace.”

Osei expanded the tuning map. “We do not need mass alone. We need the missing block’s phase relation.”

The Crown scheduler turned toward the sealed archive indicator on the far board. “C-9 carries an Eiren relation in that band.”

No one answered immediately.

The suggestion had technical sense. The non-conscious geometry authenticated during the hearing had contributed boundaries to the twenty-six-month model. A fresh destructive read could reveal drift at the exact band now failing. Before the judgment, someone could have called it emergency analysis and put the burden on whatever remained in the glass.

Osei closed the archive source on his display. “We have the fixed boundary. We do not have permission for another read.”

“I said the bell carries the relation. I did not request activation.”

“Any new interior measurement spends state.”

The scheduler looked to Ada. “Emergency safety control remains with the operator.”

“Inside the adopted schedule,” Ada said. “The archive seal lies outside it.”

“If the Mouth forks, safety authority reaches every contributing mass.”

“C-9 is not contributing mass to this window.”

“It could prevent failure.”

Nia watched the public ledger. The line identifying the proposal remained blank because no authorized person had entered a request. An emergency could become a story told afterward unless someone forced it into a field while refusal still mattered.

“State the request for the record,” she said.

The scheduler met her eyes. “I am not making one.”

“Then remove C-9 from the fault discussion.”

He cleared the archive indicator.

Osei restored the active mass map. “We can build the relation from two living sources if Sable changes its tuning work and Callis shifts the axial reserve.”

Callis control answered at once. “Axial reserve protects station balance during ring conversion.”

“How much can you release without crossing civic limit?”

“Not enough under full Archive cooling.”

Nia looked at the preservation load. Three bays had operated on rotation since conversion began. C-9’s field drew little compared with the conservation rooms holding freight recorders, damaged beacons, and family archives. Lowering cooling would not destroy them immediately. It would narrow their safe margin and require crews to move the most stressed glass by hand.

“We can suspend one conservation bay for the window,” she said.

Osei shook his head. “Bay two has the heat-fractured intake records.”

“Then move them to bay one.”

“In twenty minutes?”

“Eighteen now.”

The Archive chief entered the channel. “Nia, you do not authorize conservation load.”

“No. I am offering the effect. You authorize it or you do not.”

A beat passed while the chief read the risk table.

“Bay two suspends for one hour,” she said. “No extension without inspection. I want every case moved before cooling drops.”

Ada entered the Callis contribution. “Still short.”

Sable’s tuning lead came onto the board. “We can shift our work bell to the missing relation.”

“What do you lose?” Ada asked.

“Two mining windows and the mass-casting run behind them.”

“Safety effect?”

“None if we stop the casting now. We scrap the current heat.”

The allocation board required a civic seal. Sable’s representative applied it after eleven seconds.

Morcant offered to reduce non-catalyst freight. Vesper Fold delayed a seed shipment whose temperature margin could tolerate the later window. Near Orison released the fabrication priority already reduced under the judgment. Each contribution changed a local plan. None erased the missing clamp.

Osei built the new relation from Sable’s bell and Callis’s axial reserve. The Mouth trace joined, narrowed, then developed a shallow oscillation at the release edge.

“Pump effect?” Ada asked.

Ema had already overlaid Latchkey’s line. “Manageable if you extend our reduced throughput.”

“For how long?”

“Until the second recovery stage.”

“Your public minimum?”

“We stay above it.”

The pump noise behind her grew sharper. “But we shut one fabrication deck and cancel local heat allotments for the same period. Put that on the board before anyone accepts.”

Ada did.

No one called the result efficient. The public ledger showed scrapped Sable heat, delayed freight, closed Near Orison fabrication, Archive cooling risk, and Latchkey reductions. The old schedule would have hidden most of those losses under operator recovery.

The combined trace remained inside tolerance.

“Technical challenge?” Ada asked the Crown scheduler.

He examined the recovery edge. “Run one Callis trim loss.”

Osei introduced the fault. Oscillation rose and settled below limit.

“One Sable timing delay.”

The trace held.

“Reverse both.”

It held again.

The scheduler nodded once. “Executable.”

Ada looked at the allocation seals. “Authorized if Archive transfer completes and every contributing polity confirms the listed burden.”

Green marks began appearing beside the losses.

One line remained amber: Callis civic consent for the Archive draw. The control board had accepted the chief’s technical authorization, but the retuning order also required a public custodian to acknowledge any preservation risk above the ordinary rotation.

A woman from the displaced memorial office joined by audio. Nia knew her only from the temporary case labels. “Bay two contains private deposits placed under continuous-cooling promises,” the custodian said. “Do families receive notice before suspension?”

“Not before this window,” the Archive chief replied. “They receive the fault record and individual condition reports after inspection.”

“That breaks the service promise.”

“Yes.”

“Can another public load supply the reserve?”

Callis control displayed hospitals, pressure safety, water recovery, and the axial court chambers. Court load had already fallen to minimum. No ceremonial lighting remained on either converted ring.

Nia requested the channel. “The cases can tolerate the hour under current estimates. The risk comes from hidden stress we have not measured. We will move every near-limit record first and inspect all cases when cooling returns.”

“You said estimates.”

“I did.”

“If a family record fractures, who answers?”

“The Archive does. Not the retuning panel and not the corridor emergency.”

The custodian stayed silent long enough for the window alarm to sound again. “Enter that responsibility with the draw.”

The chief placed the Archive’s institutional seal beside the risk. The memorial custodian countersigned as public witness, not as consent on behalf of every depositor.

Callis turned green.

On another channel, Vesper Fold withdrew its seed-delay offer and resubmitted it with a temperature inspection at arrival. Sable attached worker compensation to the scrapped casting run. Near Orison added a restart priority for the fabrication deck it would close. Shared sacrifice acquired conditions because people had learned that an unqualified offer became someone else’s permanent convenience.

Nia left before the last operational annotation arrived. Bay two had to move.

The conservation cases weighed little in Callis’s ring gravity and carried enough inertia to break a wrist.

Nia guided the first case along a rail while two archive workers controlled its tail. Inside, five auric-glass records rested in pressure foam. Fine stress lines showed through their smoked amber layers. None contained a verified person. That did not make careless damage acceptable.

Osei joined them after transferring window control to the ring engineer.

“You should stay at the board,” Nia said.

“My model is certified. Ada owns execution until the pre-window inspection.” He took the tail strap from one worker. “You are short a licensed phase handler.”

“For external preservation.”

“For any case with responsive geometry.”

Nia released her grip so he could inspect the first seal. Training had changed shape after her license loss. She could explain why a tool resisted, where heat accumulated, and which omission would survive an unfriendly question. Osei had to decide what those facts authorized.

He cleared the case.

They moved eight more along the narrow rail. Fan vibration traveled through the handles. At the bay-one threshold, each case received a new position tag and thermal witness strip. The Archive chief called time every three minutes without urging anyone to hurry. Haste could turn a shared sacrifice into damage assigned to whoever held the weakest record.

The final case carried transit beacons recovered from minor decompressions. Nia recognized one acquisition number from her first year at Callis. She had written the intake condition in a hand she no longer used.

Osei checked its foam pressure. “This corner needs another pad.”

“The old seal will resist opening.”

“Then we brace outside.”

He cut a wedge from inert foam and secured it against the case shell. The correction took ninety seconds. Bay two’s cooling alarm sounded before they crossed the threshold.

Nia felt heat begin as a change in air movement rather than temperature. Fans slowed. The axial reserve released from the Archive load and moved into Callis control.

“Last case clear,” the chief reported.

Ada’s voice came over the public channel. “All burden seals confirmed. Retuning window proceeds on revised relation.”

Osei closed bay one. “I need to inspect ring three.”

“I know.”

“You are coming.”

It sounded like an order because his role permitted one.

Nia clipped the passive gauge to her belt. “Yes.”

At the control gantry, Osei performed the pre-window inspection while Nia read values back to him.

Ring three carried one absent block and no hidden waiver. A red mechanical tag covered the distorted clamp. Sable’s bell entered the revised relation on the public map. Callis’s axial reserve showed a temporary draw from Archive cooling. Every loss had an owner, a duration, and a review time.

“Phase edge?” Osei asked.

“Within published tolerance.”

“Thermal recovery?”

“Separated intervals holding.”

“Latchkey pump condition?”

Ema answered through the board. “Reduced and stable.”

“Archive?”

The chief said, “Bay two suspended. Bay one below soft limit and rising slowly.”

Osei set his seal into the window key.

The converted ring pressed against the gantry as its tuning blocks aligned. No spectacle marked the Meridian Mouth outside Callis. Freight crews saw timing fields. Habitat controllers saw pressure margins and arrival predictions. Nia felt the corridor form as a pattern of force through the rail, less abrupt than the old Crown openings and wider at the recovery edge.

“Window available,” Ada said.

Morcant’s catalyst convoy entered first. Its track advanced across the board without deviation. Medical feeder mass followed under protected designation. Sable held its altered relation while the scrapped casting heat cooled in a chamber no one here could see.

The Mouth opened on the slower schedule.

At the Archive indicator, the external integrity light for choir bell C-9 remained level. Its court seal showed no access request, no power change, and no destructive measurement.

Osei kept one hand on the key until the convoy cleared. Nia stood beside him without touching it.

C-9 remains silent under its seal.

Epilogue — A Law for the Unspoken

Osei waited for the new signal person’s breathing to settle before he placed the refusal form within reach.

The vocal body lay supported in warm saline mesh, one hand free above the restraint linen. A pressure timer counted the return in small amber increments. Across the room, an independent custodian watched the phase budget while a medical advocate held the channel open to the Court clerk.

No crowd waited outside. The discovery had entered Callis through an ordinary freight inspection: an emergency recorder sealed inside a damaged survey mass, identity incomplete, ownership suspended at intake. Twelve years earlier, such a find would have begun with arguments over salvage. Now the preservation order had preceded the transfer cradle.

The person called herself Teren Vale. That name appeared in the recorder, along with the end of a survey route and a request to warn the next crew about a magnetic fault. Her choice during the Novel Preference Test had not appeared anywhere in the source. She asked to hear the present Callis maintenance fans rather than a reconstruction of her vessel. Then she chose the blue cup over the identical gray one because blue reminded her of nothing she could name.

Temporary standing followed.

Teren read the form twice. “If I stop, you keep the recorder?”

“We preserve it under the access limits you choose,” Osei said.

“And if better equipment exists later?”

“A reviewer may test that equipment on unrelated material. No one spends your state to prove a claim of safety.”

“Can I change this after I am gone?”

“Only through a preference you form while you have capacity now.”

Her fingers rested on the refusal field. “That sounds like no.”

“It means I will not pretend there is another conversation available.”

The advocate looked at him, then returned to the form. Osei had learned the answer from Nia, though she had never given it in those words. A careful custodian did not soften a limit until consent crossed it by mistake.

Teren selected preservation, external integrity checks, and no second embodiment. She excluded destructive measurement for research, testimony, family search, or public education. At the last category she hesitated.

“What if they find someone who knew me?”

“They may give that person your existing records. They may not open you to make a new one.”

“Even if they ask?”

“Your choice governs access to you. It does not govern what they feel.”

She pressed her thumb to the form.

The witness lamps recorded capacity, pressure budget, medical stability, and every spoken question. Osei ran the refusal geometry through a non-conscious comparison. It matched the preference event without reconstructing thought. The independent custodian repeated his test on another instrument. Their digests agreed.

One field did not. The survey recorder gave Teren’s employment identity a damaged checksum, while the vocal body had supplied a complete family name. The Court clerk marked the identity provisional.

“Does that stop the refusal?” Teren asked.

“No. Capacity and preference do not depend on whether the registry can yet prove every part of your name.”

“Then why mark it?”

“So no one uses this activation to close a different question.”

The advocate requested a separate identity review using the non-conscious route and payroll fragments. Osei approved external comparison and barred another embodiment for clarification. The distinction took less than a minute to record. Years of cases had taught the Court that uncertainty in one field tempted institutions to reopen all of them.

Teren touched the provisional mark. “Keep the name I gave you with the doubt.”

“We will keep both.”

The Court clerk entered silent standing on the recorder.

No opinion accompanied the entry. Strict review had become ordinary practice: two custodians, one advocate, an external test, annual integrity reports, and particular evidence before any fraud petition could reach a judge. Ordinary did not mean easy. It meant the difficult steps already had names.

Teren’s return had begun to thin. Her breathing grew shallower against the mesh.

“Will I feel the channel close?” she asked.

“The body will become tired. We can reduce the field slowly.”

“I do not want a speech.”

“All right.”

“Tell the next survey crew about the fault.”

“That record does not require another access. It has already entered the route warnings.”

She nodded. “Then stop.”

Osei confirmed the request with the advocate and custodian. Teren pressed the physical stop field herself. The room’s activation light changed from green to amber.

Outside intake, the rebuilt Eiren registry occupied one long wall of the axial passage.

It hung at reading height behind plain auric glass. Complete names appeared beside occupational fragments and unresolved identities. The 312 transient workers remained within the main count, not on a lower panel. Visitors could inspect a record, leave a correction claim, or pass without being instructed to grieve.

Freight crews passed it on their way to the public Mouth board. Trade still moved on the slower schedule established after Crown lost exclusivity. Windows took longer to recover. Allocation changes showed every habitat burden before release. Delays caused arguments instead of disappearing into operator discretion.

Osei crossed the passage to the sealed control station. Teren’s recorder had already arrived in its preservation cradle. The annual-review field waited blank. No activation control appeared on the public side.

He entered the final procedure into the custody log: Osei Marr closes the activation channel at the person’s request.

The independent custodian countersigned. Osei turned the ceramic key to zero, removed it from the socket, and sealed the empty channel.
